We consider the evaluation map

\mathrm{ev}:\mathcal{M}_{N,l,k}(\mathbf{A};H,J_{Y})\to\mathcal{L}^{2N},\quad\mathrm{ev}(\mathbf{v}):=\big(\mathrm{ev}_{-,0}(\tilde{v}_{1}),\mathrm{ev}_{+,0}(\tilde{v}_{1}),\dots,\mathrm{ev}_{-,0}(\tilde{v}_{N}),\mathrm{ev}_{+,0}(\tilde{v}_{N})\big),

where
\mathrm{ev}_{\pm,0}(\tilde{v}):=\mathrm{ev}_{\pm}(v)(0)
.

Definition 3.10
.

Let us abbreviate
\mathcal{D}_{H}=(f_{\mathcal{L}},Z_{\mathcal{L}},H,J_{Y})
. For
(\tilde{p},k),(\tilde{q},l)\in\mathrm{Crit}f_{\mathcal{L}}\times m_{L}\mathbb{Z}
, we define

\mathcal{M}_{N}(\tilde{q}_{l},\tilde{p}_{k};\mathbf{A};\mathcal{D}_{H}):=(\mathrm{ev})^{-1}\left(W^{u}_{Z_{\mathcal{L}}}(\tilde{q})\times\Delta_{Z_{\mathcal{L}}}^{N-1}\times W^{s}_{Z_{\mathcal{L}}}(\tilde{p})\right),

where

\Delta_{Z_{\mathcal{L}}}:=\{(x,\varphi_{Z_{\mathcal{L}}}^{t}(x))\in\mathcal{L}\times\mathcal{L}\;|\;x\in\mathcal{L}\setminus\mathrm{Crit}f_{\mathcal{L}},\;t\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\},

see Figure
1
. For
N=1
, this means
\left(\mathrm{ev}\right)^{-1}(W^{u}_{Z_{\mathcal{L}}}(\tilde{q})\times W^{s}_{Z_{\mathcal{L}}}(\tilde{p}))
.
We also define

\mathcal{M}^{*}_{N}(\tilde{q}_{l},\tilde{p}_{k};\mathbf{A};\mathcal{D}_{H}):=\mathcal{M}_{N}(\tilde{q}_{l},\tilde{p}_{k};\mathbf{A};\mathcal{D}_{H})\,\cap\,\mathcal{M}^{*}_{N,l,k}(\mathbf{A};H,J_{Y}).

Figure 1
:
An element of
\mathcal{M}_{N}(\tilde{q}_{l},\tilde{p}_{k};\mathbf{A};\mathcal{D}_{H})
.

There is a canonical projection map

\Pi:\mathcal{M}_{N}(\tilde{q}_{l},\tilde{p}_{k};\mathbf{A};\mathcal{D}_{H})\to\mathcal{N}_{N}(q,p;{\bf A};\mathcal{D}),\quad\Pi(\mathbf{v}):=(\pi\circ v_{1},\dots,\pi\circ v_{N})

(3.15)

This map restricts to
\Pi:\mathcal{M}^{*}_{N}(\tilde{q}_{l},\tilde{p}_{k};\mathbf{A};\mathcal{D}_{H})\to\mathcal{N}^{*}_{N}(q,p;{\bf A};\mathcal{D})
.

Proposition 3.11
.

Let
\mathcal{J}_{(Y,\mathcal{L})}^{\mathrm{reg}}\subset\mathcal{J}_{Y}
be the subset of
\mathcal{J}_{Y}
whose horizontal part
J_{\Sigma}
belongs to
\mathcal{J}_{(\Sigma,L)}^{\mathrm{reg}}
defined in Proposition
2.4
.
Then for every choice of

(\tilde{p},k),(\tilde{q},l)\in\mathrm{Crit}f_{\mathcal{L}}\times m_{L}\mathbb{Z},\quad N\in\mathbb{N},\quad\text{and }\;\mathbf{A}=(A_{1},\dots,A_{N})\in(\pi_{2}(\Sigma,L))^{N},

and for every
J_{Y}\in\mathcal{J}_{(Y,\mathcal{L})}^{\mathrm{reg}}
, the moduli space

\mathcal{M}^{*}_{N}(\tilde{q}_{l},\tilde{p}_{k};\mathbf{A};\mathcal{D}_{H})

is a smooth manifold of dimension
\mu(\tilde{p}_{k})-\mu(\tilde{q}_{l})+N-1
.

Proof.

We first show that
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{N,l,k}(\mathbf{A};H,J_{Y})
of
J_{Y}\in\mathcal{J}_{(Y,\mathcal{L})}^{\mathrm{reg}}
is a smooth manifold of dimension
N\operatorname{dim}L+\mu_{L}(\mathbf{A})
where
\mu_{L}(\mathbf{A})=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}\mu_{L}(A_{i})
. We abbreviate
Z=\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]
and
\partial Z=\mathbb{R}\times\{0,1\}
, and consider the linearized operator associated to a Floer strip
\tilde{v}=(b,v)
:

D_{\tilde{v}}:W^{1,p,\delta}_{V_{\pm}}\big((Z,\partial Z),(\tilde{v}^{*}T(\mathbb{R}\times Y),\tilde{v}|_{\partial Z}^{*}T(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{L})\big)\to L^{p,\delta}\big(Z,\Omega^{0,1}_{Z}\otimes(\tilde{v}^{*}T(\mathbb{R}\times Y))\big)

for
p>2
and sufficiently small
\delta>0
.
Here,
V_{+}=T_{\tilde{x}}\mathcal{L}
and
V_{-}=T_{\tilde{y}}\mathcal{L}
for
\tilde{x}=\mathrm{ev}_{+}(v)(0)
and
\tilde{y}=\mathrm{ev}_{-}(v)(0)
. The kernels of the associated asymptotic operators are precisely given by
\{d\varphi_{X_{H}}^{t}(v_{\pm})\}_{t\in[0,1]}
for
v_{\pm}\in V_{\pm}
, where
\varphi_{X_{H}}^{t}
denotes the flow of
X_{H}
. The notation
W^{1,p,\delta}_{V_{\pm}}
means that it consists of sections
\sigma
of regularity
W_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,p}
such that
e^{\pm\delta s}(\sigma(\pm s,t)-\hat{v}_{\pm}(\pm s,t))
is of class
W^{1,p}
near the corresponding ends for some
v_{\pm}\in V_{\pm}
. Here
\hat{v}_{\pm}
is a smooth section satisfying
\hat{v}_{\pm}(\pm s,t)=d\varphi_{X_{H}}^{t}(v_{\pm})
for large
s
.
Writing
w:=\pi\circ v
, we consider the decomposition

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{v}^{*}T(\mathbb{R}\times Y)&\cong\tilde{v}^{*}(\mathbb{R}\partial_{r}\oplus\mathbb{R}R)\oplus w^{*}T\Sigma,\\[2.15277pt]
\tilde{v}|_{\partial Z}^{*}T(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{L})&\cong\tilde{v}|_{\partial Z}^{*}(\mathbb{R}\partial_{r}\oplus 0)\oplus w|_{\partial Z}^{*}TL.\end{split}

(3.16)

As in
[
DL19a
, Lemma 5.22]
or in
[
BKK24
, Equation (3.19)]
,
D_{\tilde{v}}
is written as

D_{\tilde{v}}=\begin{pmatrix}D_{\tilde{v}}^{\mathrm{v}}&L_{\mathrm{cpt}}\\[2.15277pt]
0&D_{\tilde{v}}^{\mathrm{h}}\end{pmatrix}

(3.17)

with respect to the splitting in (
3.16
). Here
L_{\mathrm{cpt}}
is a compact operator. The vertical part
D_{\tilde{v}}^{\mathrm{v}}
has the form

D_{\tilde{v}}^{\mathrm{v}}:W^{1,p,\delta}((Z,\partial Z),(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{R}))\to L^{p,\delta}(Z,\mathbb{C}),\quad D_{\tilde{v}}^{\mathrm{v}}\sigma:=\partial_{s}\sigma+J_{0}\partial_{t}\sigma+\begin{pmatrix}h^{\prime\prime}(e^{b})e^{b}&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}\sigma.

The horizontal part
D_{\tilde{v}}^{\mathrm{h}}
is the Cauchy–Riemann operator

D_{\tilde{v}}^{\mathrm{h}}=\nabla_{w}^{0,1}:W^{1,p,\delta}_{V_{\pm}}\big((Z,\partial Z),(w^{*}T\Sigma,w|_{\partial Z}^{*}TL)\big)\to L^{p,\delta}\big(Z,\Omega^{0,1}_{Z}\otimes w^{*}T\Sigma\big).

The kernels of the asymptotic operators of
D_{\tilde{v}}^{\mathrm{h}}
are
T_{x}L
and
T_{y}L
for
x=\pi(\tilde{x})
and
y=\pi(\tilde{y})
, which justifies the notational abuse
V_{+}=T_{\tilde{x}}\mathcal{L}\cong T_{x}L
and
V_{-}=T_{\tilde{y}}\mathcal{L}\cong T_{y}L
.
As mentioned in (
3.13
), we could think of
w
as a
J_{\Sigma}
-holomorphic map from the disk
(\mathbb{D},\partial\mathbb{D})
. Let
D_{w}
be the associated Cauchy–Riemann operator considered in (
2.1
). Then the kernel and cokernel of the horizontal part
D_{\tilde{v}}^{\mathrm{h}}
are canonically isomorphic to those of
D_{w}
, see
[
DL19a
, page 29]
for the proof in the periodic case. Since
J_{\Sigma}\in\mathcal{J}_{(\Sigma,L)}^{\mathrm{reg}}
,
D_{w}
and thus
D_{\tilde{v}}^{\mathrm{h}}
are surjective.

Next, we show that
D_{\tilde{v}}^{\mathrm{v}}
is surjective. The asymptotic operators of
D_{\tilde{v}}^{\mathrm{v}}
are given by

A_{\pm}=-J_{0}\frac{d}{dt}-\begin{pmatrix}h^{\prime\prime}(e^{b_{\pm}})e^{b_{\pm}}&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}:W^{1,2}(([0,1],\{0,1\}),(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{R}))\to L^{2}([0,1],\mathbb{C}),

where
J_{0}
is the complex structure on
\mathbb{C}
and
b_{\pm}:=\mathrm{ev}_{\pm}(b)
for
\mathrm{ev}_{\pm}
defined after (
3.12
).
Let
\alpha_{-}(A_{\pm})\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
be the maximum relative winding number of eigenfunctions associated with the negative eigenvalues of
A_{\pm}
. By
[
FK16
]
, we have

\mu_{\mathrm{RS}}(A_{\pm})=2\alpha_{-}(A_{\pm})+\frac{1}{2}.

(3.18)

Since
h^{\prime\prime}(e^{b_{\pm}})>0
, Lemma
4.5
below implies that
\alpha_{-}(A_{\pm})=0
, and hence
\mu_{\mathrm{RS}}(A_{\pm})=\frac{1}{2}
.
As
\operatorname{ind}(D_{\tilde{v}}^{\mathrm{v}})=\mu_{\mathrm{RS}}(A_{+})-\mu_{\mathrm{RS}}(A_{-})=0
, we have
\mathrm{coker\,}D_{\tilde{v}}^{\mathrm{v}}=0
if and only if
\operatorname{ker}D_{\tilde{v}}^{\mathrm{v}}=0
. Assume that there is nonzero
\zeta\in\operatorname{ker}D_{\tilde{v}}^{\mathrm{v}}
. Due to the asymptotic formula of
\zeta
established in
[
Abb04
, Theorem 3.12]
, the asymptotic relative winding numbers
\mathrm{wind}_{\pm\infty}(\zeta)
satisfy

\mathrm{wind}_{+\infty}(\zeta)\leq\alpha_{-}(A_{+})=0,\qquad\mathrm{wind}_{-\infty}(\zeta)\geq\alpha_{+}(A_{-})=\frac{1}{2},

(3.19)

where
\alpha_{+}(A_{-})=\alpha_{-}(A_{-})+\frac{1}{2}
is the minimum relative winding number of eigenfunctions associated with the positive eigenvalues of
A_{-}
.
The algebraic count of zeros of
\zeta
equals
\mathrm{wind}_{+\infty}(\zeta)-\mathrm{wind}_{-\infty}(\zeta)<0
, which contradicts the fact that all zeros of
\zeta
are positive. This proves that
\operatorname{ker}D_{\tilde{v}}^{\mathrm{v}}=0
and
D_{\tilde{v}}^{\mathrm{v}}
is surjective.

Therefore,
D_{\tilde{v}}
is surjective and its index is computed as

\operatorname{ind}(D_{\tilde{v}})=\operatorname{ind}(D_{\tilde{v}}^{\mathrm{v}})+\operatorname{ind}({D}_{w})=\operatorname{dim}L+\mu_{L}([w]).

This proves that
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{N,l,k}(\mathbf{A};H,J_{Y})
is a smooth manifold of dimension
N\operatorname{dim}L+\mu_{L}(\mathbf{A})
.

It remains to prove the following transversality result:

\left({\mathrm{ev}}:\mathcal{M}^{*}_{N,l,k}(\mathbf{A};H,J_{Y})\to\mathcal{L}^{2N}\right)\pitchfork\left(W^{u}_{Z_{\mathcal{L}}}(\tilde{q})\times\Delta_{Z_{\mathcal{L}}}^{N-1}\times W^{s}_{Z_{\mathcal{L}}}(\tilde{p})\right).

By (
3.17
) and the fact that
Z_{\mathcal{L}}
is the horizontal lift of
Z_{L}
, it suffices to achieve this transversality in the horizontal direction, or equivalently in the projection to
\Sigma
. The latter is part of Proposition
2.4
.(a). The dimension of
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{N}(\tilde{q}_{l},\tilde{p}_{k};\mathbf{A};\mathcal{D}_{H})
is computed as

\begin{split}&\operatorname{dim}\mathcal{M}^{*}_{N}(\tilde{q}_{l},\tilde{p}_{k};\mathbf{A};{\mathcal{D}}_{Y})\\[2.15277pt]
&\quad=N\operatorname{dim}L+\mu_{L}(\mathbf{A})-(\operatorname{dim}\mathcal{L}-\operatorname{ind}_{f_{\mathcal{L}}}(\tilde{q})+(N-1)(\operatorname{dim}\mathcal{L}-1)+\operatorname{ind}_{f_{\mathcal{L}}}(\tilde{p}))\\[2.15277pt]
&\quad=\operatorname{ind}_{f_{\mathcal{L}}}(\tilde{q})-\operatorname{ind}_{f_{\mathcal{L}}}(\tilde{p})+\mu_{L}(\mathbf{A})+N-1\\[2.15277pt]
&\quad=\mu(\tilde{p}_{k})-\mu(\tilde{q}_{l})+N-1,\end{split}

where the last line follows from Proposition
3.7
.
This completes the proof.
∎

Let
J_{Y}\in\mathcal{J}_{(Y,\mathcal{L})}^{\mathrm{reg}}
.
For
N=0
, let

\mathcal{M}_{N=0}(\tilde{q}_{l},\tilde{p}_{k}):=\begin{cases}W^{u}_{Z_{\mathcal{L}}}(\tilde{q})\cap W^{s}_{Z_{\mathcal{L}}}(\tilde{p})&if $k=l$,\\
\emptyset&if $k\neq l$.\end{cases}

There are a free
\mathbb{R}
-action on
\mathcal{M}_{N=0}(\tilde{q}_{l},\tilde{p}_{k})
given by
Z_{\mathcal{L}}
and a free
\mathbb{R}^{N}
-action on
\mathcal{M}_{N}(\tilde{q}_{l},\tilde{p}_{k};\mathbf{A};\mathcal{D}_{H})
given by the translation in the
s
-direction on each strip component.
We define

\begin{split}&\overline{\mathcal{M}}(\tilde{q}_{l},\tilde{p}_{k};\mathcal{D}_{H}):=\mathcal{M}_{N=0}(\tilde{q}_{l},\tilde{p}_{k})/\mathbb{R}\cup\bigcup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{M}_{N}(\tilde{q}_{l},\tilde{p}_{k};\mathbf{A};\mathcal{D}_{H})/\mathbb{R}^{N},\\[2.15277pt]
&\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{*}(\tilde{q}_{l},\tilde{p}_{k};\mathcal{D}_{H}):=\mathcal{M}_{N=0}(\tilde{q}_{l},\tilde{p}_{k})/\mathbb{R}\cup\bigcup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{M}^{*}_{N}(\tilde{q}_{l},\tilde{p}_{k};\mathbf{A};\mathcal{D}_{H})/\mathbb{R}^{N},\end{split}

(3.20)

where the latter space has dimension
\mu(\tilde{p}_{k})-\mu(\tilde{q}_{l})-1
by Proposition
3.11
.

Proposition 3.12
.

Assume that
\mu(\tilde{p}_{k})-\mu(\tilde{q}_{l})\leq 2
.

(a)

If
N_{L}\geq 2
, all elements of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}(\tilde{q}_{l},\tilde{p}_{k};\mathcal{D}_{H})
are simple, i.e.,

\overline{\mathcal{M}}(\tilde{q}_{l},\tilde{p}_{k};\mathcal{D}_{H})=\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{*}(\tilde{q}_{l},\tilde{p}_{k};\mathcal{D}_{H})
.

(b)

Assume
N_{L}>2
. Then, there exist
r_{\pm}\in\mathbb{R}
such that every element in this moduli space has its image in
(r_{-},r_{+})\times Y
. Consequently, this moduli space is compact up to breaking.

The proof of the above proposition is deferred to Section
3.4.3
.

3.3.3
Orientation lines

Let
\dot{\mathbb{D}}:=\mathbb{D}\setminus\{-1\}
be the punctured disk and let

\epsilon_{-}:(-\infty,0]\times[0,1]\longrightarrow\dot{\mathbb{D}}

be negative holomorphic strip-like coordinates near the boundary puncture
-1\in\mathbb{D}
. We take any
\tilde{p}_{k}\in\mathcal{L}_{k}^{H}
for
(\tilde{p},k)\in\mathrm{Crit}f_{\mathcal{L}}\times m_{L}\mathbb{Z}
. A capping of
\tilde{p}_{k}
is a smooth map

\hat{x}:(\dot{\mathbb{D}},\partial\dot{\mathbb{D}})\longrightarrow(\mathbb{R}\times Y,\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{L})

such that
\hat{x}(\epsilon_{-}(s,\cdot))
converges to
\tilde{p}_{k}
in the
C^{\infty}
-topology as
s\to-\infty
. Instead of providing a general construction of an orientation line
\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{p}_{k},\hat{x})
, which involves all relevant choices of an almost complex structure and a connection as in
[
Zap15
, Section 3.7]
, we define
\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{p}_{k},\hat{x})
with a specific choice of an almost complex structure and a connection. To this end, we decompose

\hat{x}^{*}T(\mathbb{R}\times Y)\cong\hat{x}^{*}(\mathbb{R}\partial_{r}\oplus\mathbb{R}R)\oplus\hat{x}^{*}\xi\cong(\dot{\mathbb{D}}\times\mathbb{C})\oplus w^{*}T\Sigma,

(3.21)

where
w:=\pi_{\mathbb{R}\times Y}\circ\hat{x}
, with
\pi_{\mathbb{R}\times Y}:\mathbb{R}\times Y\to\Sigma
denoting the natural projection induced by
\pi
, and where
\mathbb{R}\partial_{r}
and
\mathbb{R}R
correspond to
\mathbb{R}
and
i\mathbb{R}
, respectively.
Note that
\hat{x}^{*}T\mathcal{L}
corresponds to
w^{*}TL
through the isomorphism.
For
p>2
and
\delta>0
, we consider an operator

D_{\hat{x}}:W^{1,p,\delta}_{V}\big((\dot{\mathbb{D}},\partial\dot{\mathbb{D}}),(\hat{x}^{*}T(\mathbb{R}\times Y),\hat{x}|_{\partial\dot{\mathbb{D}}}^{*}T(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{L})\big)\to L^{p,\delta}\big(\dot{\mathbb{D}},\Omega^{0,1}_{\dot{\mathbb{D}}}\otimes(\hat{x}^{*}T(\mathbb{R}\times Y))\big)

which is of the form
D_{\hat{x}}=\begin{pmatrix}D_{\hat{x}}^{\mathrm{v}}&L_{\mathrm{cpt}}\\
0&D_{\hat{x}}^{\mathrm{h}}\end{pmatrix}
with respect to the above decomposition. The horizontal part
D_{\hat{x}}^{\mathrm{h}}
is the Cauchy–Riemann operator

D_{\hat{x}}^{\mathrm{h}}=\nabla_{w}^{0,1}:W^{1,p,\delta}_{V}\big((\dot{\mathbb{D}},\partial\dot{\mathbb{D}}),(w^{*}T\Sigma,w|_{\partial\dot{\mathbb{D}}}^{*}TL)\big)\to L^{p}\big(\dot{\mathbb{D}},\Omega^{0,1}_{\dot{\mathbb{D}}}\otimes w^{*}T\Sigma\big)

for the connection
\nabla_{w}=w^{*}\nabla
on
w^{*}T\Sigma
, cf. (
2.1
). Here,
V
is the kernel of the asymptotic operator associated to the puncture
-1\in\mathbb{D}
, which is canonically isomorphic to
T_{\tilde{p}}\mathcal{L}\cong T_{p}L
. The notation
W^{1,p,\delta}_{V}
is explained in the preceding section.
The vertical part
D_{\hat{x}}^{\mathrm{v}}
is given by

D_{\hat{x}}^{\mathrm{v}}:W^{1,p,\delta}((\dot{\mathbb{D}},\partial{\dot{\mathbb{D}}}),(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{R}))\to L^{p,\delta}(\dot{\mathbb{D}},\mathbb{C}),\qquad\sigma\mapsto\partial_{s}\sigma+J_{0}\partial_{t}\sigma+B\sigma

for some smooth map
B:\dot{\mathbb{D}}\to\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{C})
such that
B(\epsilon_{-}(s,t))=\begin{pmatrix}h^{\prime\prime}(e^{r_{x}})e^{r_{x}}&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}

for
s\ll 0
,
B(\epsilon_{-}(s,t))=0
for
s
sufficiently close to
0
, and
B=0
on the complement of
\epsilon_{-}
. The off-diagonal term
L_{\mathrm{cpt}}
denotes an arbitrary compact operator. We include it to account for the compact operator that appears in (
3.17
). For a sufficiently small
\delta>0
, the operator
D_{\hat{x}}
is Fredholm.

Using the evaluation map

\begin{split}&\mathrm{ev}_{-1}:W^{1,p,\delta}_{V}\big((\dot{\mathbb{D}},\partial\dot{\mathbb{D}}),(\hat{x}^{*}T(\mathbb{R}\times Y),\hat{x}|_{\partial\dot{\mathbb{D}}}^{*}T(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{L}))\big)\longrightarrow T_{\tilde{p}}\mathcal{L},\\[2.15277pt]
&\mathrm{ev}_{-1}(\zeta):=\lim_{s\to-\infty}\pi_{\mathcal{L}}\circ\zeta(\epsilon_{-}(s,0)),\end{split}

where
\pi_{\mathcal{L}}:\mathbb{R}\times T_{\tilde{p}}\mathcal{L}\to T_{\tilde{p}}\mathcal{L}
is the obvious projection,
we define the Fredholm operator

\displaystyle D_{\hat{x}}\#T_{\tilde{p}}W^{u}_{Z_{\mathcal{L}}}(\tilde{p}):=D_{\hat{x}}|_{(\mathrm{ev}_{-1})^{-1}(T_{\tilde{p}}W^{u}_{Z_{\mathcal{L}}}(\tilde{p}))}.

We denote the orientation line of
\det(D_{\hat{x}}\#T_{\tilde{p}}W^{u}_{Z_{\mathcal{L}}}(\tilde{p}))
by
\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{p}_{k},\hat{x})
. Note that
\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{L}
is relatively
\mathrm{Pin}^{\pm}
in
\mathbb{R}\times Y
since
L
is so in
\Sigma
, see Remark
3.13
. Therefore, if
\hat{x}^{\prime}
is another capping of
\tilde{p}_{k}
such that gluing
\hat{x}
and
\hat{x}^{\prime}
with the reversed orientation along
\tilde{p}_{k}
yields a disk that is trivial in
\pi_{2}(\mathbb{R}\times Y,\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{L})
, then, by
[
Zap15
, Lemma 3.6]
, there is a canonical isomorphism

\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{p}_{k},\hat{x})\cong\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{p}_{k},\hat{x}^{\prime}).

Remark 3.13
.

Let
\sigma
be a relative
\mathrm{Pin}^{\pm}(n)
-structure on
L\subset\Sigma
as in Remark
2.1
. We define a relative
\mathrm{Pin}^{\pm}(n+1)
-structure
\tilde{\sigma}
on
\mathbb{R}\times L\subset\mathbb{R}\times Y
as follows.
Let
(g,\beta)\in\check{\mathrm{C}}^{1}(\mathcal{U};\mathrm{Pin}^{\pm}(n))\times\check{\mathrm{Z}}^{2}(\mathcal{V};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
be a representative of
\sigma
.
The projection
\pi:Y\to\Sigma
induces a relative
\mathrm{Pin}^{\pm}(n)
-structure on
\mathcal{L}\subset Y
defined by the pair
(\pi^{*}g,\pi^{*}\beta)\in\check{\mathrm{C}}^{1}(\tilde{\mathcal{U}};\mathrm{Pin}^{\pm}(n))\times\check{\mathrm{Z}}^{2}(\tilde{\mathcal{V}};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
for some good covers
\tilde{\mathcal{V}}
on
Y
and
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}
on
\mathcal{L}
finer than
\{\pi^{-1}(V)\}_{V\in\mathcal{V}}
and
\{\pi^{-1}(U)\}_{U\in\mathcal{U}}
, respectively. Consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Pin}^{\pm}(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Pin}^{\pm}(n+1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O(n+1),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

where the vertical maps
i
are the obvious inclusions with respect to
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}=\mathbb{R}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Extending
(i_{*}\pi^{*}g,\pi^{*}\beta)\in\check{\mathrm{C}}^{1}(\tilde{\mathcal{U}};\mathrm{Pin}^{\pm}(n+1))\times\check{\mathrm{Z}}^{2}(\tilde{\mathcal{V}};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
trivially in the
\mathbb{R}
-direction, we obtain a relative
\mathrm{Pin}^{\pm}(n+1)
-structure
\tilde{\sigma}
on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{L}\subset\mathbb{R}\times Y
. We call
\tilde{\sigma}
the lift of
\sigma
.

The projection
w=\pi_{\mathbb{R}\times Y}\circ\hat{x}
smoothly extends over
-1
, and thus we may also regard
w
as a smooth map
w:(\mathbb{D},\partial\mathbb{D})\to(\Sigma,L)
.
Then, the kernel and cokernel of the horizontal part
D_{\hat{x}}^{\mathrm{h}}
are canonically isomorphic to those of the Cauchy–Riemann operator
D_{w}
in (
2.1
), see
[
DL19a
, page 29]
for the proof in the periodic case. This yields an isomorphism of determinant line bundles

\det(D_{\hat{x}})\cong\det(D_{\hat{x}}^{\mathrm{v}})\otimes\det(D_{\hat{x}}^{\mathrm{h}})\cong\det(D_{\hat{x}}^{\mathrm{v}})\otimes\det(D_{w}).

Moreover, through the isomorphism (
3.21
), we have

T_{\tilde{p}}W^{u}_{Z_{\mathcal{L}}}(\tilde{p})\cong 0\oplus T_{p}W^{u}_{Z_{L}}(p)

and

\det(D_{\hat{x}}\#T_{\tilde{p}}W^{u}_{Z_{\mathcal{L}}}(\tilde{p}))\cong\det(D_{\hat{x}}^{\mathrm{v}})\otimes\det(D_{w}\#T_{p}W^{u}_{Z_{L}}(p)).

(3.22)

Next, we observe that
\det(D_{\hat{x}}^{\mathrm{v}})
admits a canonical orientation.
We consider positive holomorphic strip-like coordinates
\epsilon_{+}:[0,\infty)\times[0,1]\to\dot{\mathbb{D}}
near the puncture
-1\in\mathbb{D}
.
We define an operator
D_{B}^{+}:W^{1,p,\delta}((\dot{\mathbb{D}},\partial{\dot{\mathbb{D}}}),(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{R}))\to L^{p,\delta}(\dot{\mathbb{D}},\mathbb{C})
analogously to
D_{\hat{x}}^{\mathrm{v}}
with
\epsilon_{-}
replaced by
\epsilon_{+}
.
Gluing
(\dot{\mathbb{D}},\epsilon_{+})
and
(\dot{\mathbb{D}},\epsilon_{-})
along the respective cylindrical ends, we obtain
\mathbb{D}
. We also glue the operators
D_{B}^{+}
and
D_{\hat{x}}^{\mathrm{v}}
, which have the same asymptotic operator, and obtain an operator
D_{B}:W^{1,p}((\mathbb{D},\partial\mathbb{D}),(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{R}))|_{(\mathrm{ev}_{-1})^{-1}(0)}\to L^{p}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C})
. We therefore have an isomorphism of determinant lines

\det(D_{B}^{+})\otimes\det(D_{\hat{x}}^{\mathrm{v}})\cong\det(D_{B}).

(3.23)

We homotope
D_{B}
to the Cauchy–Riemann operator
\bar{\partial}_{(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{R})}\#0:W^{1,p}((\mathbb{D},\partial{\mathbb{D}}),(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{R}))|_{(\mathrm{ev}_{-1})^{-1}(0)}\allowbreak\to L^{p}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C})
through Fredholm operators. Since
\bar{\partial}_{(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{R})}\#0
is an isomorphism,
\det(\bar{\partial}_{(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{R})}\#0)
has a canonical orientation, and this yields an orientation on
\det(D_{B})
. The computation in the proof of Proposition
3.11
shows that
D_{B}^{+}
has index zero and is surjective, and hence, it is an isomorphism. Thus,
\det(D_{B}^{+})
admits a canonical orientation. Using (
3.23
), we obtain a canonical orientation on
\det(D_{\hat{x}}^{\mathrm{v}})
.

The isomorphism in (
3.22
) and the orientation on
\det(D_{\hat{x}}^{\mathrm{v}}\#0)
induces an isomorphism of orientation lines

f_{\hat{x}}:\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{p}_{k},\hat{x})\longrightarrow\mathfrak{o}(p,w).

(3.24)

Moreover, for any
(\tilde{p},k),(\tilde{q},l)\in\mathrm{Crit}f_{\mathcal{L}}\times m_{L}\mathbb{Z}
satisfying
\mu(\tilde{p}_{k})-\mu(\tilde{q}_{l})=1
, every element
[\mathbf{v}]\in\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{*}(\tilde{q}_{l},\tilde{p}_{k};\mathcal{D}_{H})
induces an isomorphism

C([\mathbf{v}]):\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{p}_{k},\hat{x})\longrightarrow\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{q}_{l},\hat{y})

(3.25)

where
\hat{y}
is a capping of
\tilde{q}_{l}
obtained by gluing
\hat{x}
with
\mathbf{v}
, see
[
Zap15
, Section 4.2.2]
for details.

Proposition 3.14
.

With the notation above, let
\hat{x},\hat{x}^{\prime}
be two cappings of
\tilde{p}_{k}\in\mathcal{L}_{k}^{H}
. Then,
the relative
\mathrm{Pin}^{\pm}(n+1)
-structure
\tilde{\sigma}
on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{L}\subset\mathbb{R}\times Y
determines an isomorphism

\phi^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{(\hat{x},\hat{x}^{\prime})}:\mathfrak{o}({\tilde{p}_{k},\hat{x}})\longrightarrow\mathfrak{o}({\tilde{p}_{k},\hat{x}^{\prime}}).

(3.26)

Moreover, this isomorphism fits into the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{o}({\tilde{q}_{l},\hat{y}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{o}({\tilde{q}_{l},\hat{y}^{\prime}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{o}({\tilde{p}_{k},\hat{x}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{o}({\tilde{p}_{k},\hat{x}^{\prime}}),}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{o}({q,v})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{o}({q,v^{\prime}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{o}({p,w})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{o}({p,w^{\prime}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{(\hat{y},\hat{y}^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\hat{y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\hat{y}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C([\mathbf{v}])}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{(\hat{x},\hat{x}^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\hat{x}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C([\mathbf{v}])}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\hat{x}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\sigma}_{(v,v^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C([\mathbf{w}])}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\sigma}_{(w,w^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C([\mathbf{w}])}

where

•

the capping
\hat{y}
of
\tilde{q}_{l}
is obtained by gluing
\hat{x}
with
\mathbf{v}
, and likewise for
\hat{y}^{\prime}
,

•

w=\pi_{\mathbb{R}\times Y}\circ\hat{x}
and
v=\pi_{\mathbb{R}\times Y}\circ\hat{y}
, and similarly for
w^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime}
,

•

\mathbf{w}=\Pi(\mathbf{v})
, with
\Pi
as in (
3.15
),

•

the vertical maps are given by (
3.24
),

•

the maps
c([\mathbf{w}])
and
c([\mathbf{v}])
are defined just above (
2.4
) and in (
3.25
), respectively,

•

the horizontal maps on the bottom face are given by (
2.3
).

Proof.

The maps
\psi^{\sigma}_{(w,w^{\prime})}
and
\phi^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{(\hat{x},\hat{x}^{\prime})}
are defined as in
[
Zap15
, Proposition 7.4]
. We recall the construction of these maps and show the commutativity of the front face of the diagram.
We define disk maps

\tilde{v}:=(-\hat{x})\#\hat{x}^{\prime},\qquad v:=(-w)\#w^{\prime}

where the minus sign indicates reversal of orientation.
Since the Maslov class of
\mu_{\mathcal{L}}
on
\pi_{2}(Y,\mathcal{L})\cong\pi_{2}(\mathbb{R}\times Y,\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{L})
vanishes by Lemma
3.6
, we have
\mu_{L}(v)=\mu_{\mathcal{L}}(\tilde{v})=0
. We take any unitary trivialization of
v^{*}T\Sigma
. Then
v|_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{*}TL
induces a loop
F_{v}
of Lagrangian subspaces in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
, which is contractible since
\mu_{L}(v)=0
.

Let
\Omega_{0}(\mathrm{Lag}(\mathbb{C}^{n}))
be the space of contractible loops of Lagrangian subspaces in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
. On one hand, for each
F\in\Omega_{0}(\mathrm{Lag}(\mathbb{C}^{n}))
, we denote by
\mathfrak{o}_{F}
the orientation line of
\bar{\partial}_{(\mathbb{C}^{n},F)}\#0
, where
\bar{\partial}_{(\mathbb{C}^{n},F)}:W^{1,p}((\mathbb{D},\partial\mathbb{D}),(\mathbb{C}^{n},F))\to L^{p}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})
is the standard Cauchy–Riemann operator and
\#0
refers to the restriction to
(\mathrm{ev}_{-1})^{-1}(0)
as before.
On the other hand, viewing
F\in\Omega_{0}(\mathrm{Lag}(\mathbb{C}^{n}))
as a real vector bundle over
S^{1}
, we associate to
F
two
\mathrm{Pin}^{\pm}(n)
-structures. Then the maps

\mathfrak{o}_{F}\mapsto F,\qquad\{\text{$\mathrm{Pin}^{\pm}(n)$-structures on $F$}\}\mapsto F

define isomorphic double covers over
\Omega_{0}(\mathrm{Lag}(\mathbb{C}^{n}))
. Over a constant loop
F_{c}\in\Omega_{0}(\mathrm{Lag}(\mathbb{C}^{n}))
, we have the canonical orientation
o_{F_{c}}^{+}
of
\mathfrak{o}_{F_{c}}
as
\bar{\partial}_{(\mathbb{C}^{n},F_{c})}\#0
is an isomorphism, and the trivial
\mathrm{Pin}^{\pm}(n)
-structure
\mathrm{p}_{F_{c}}^{\mathrm{triv}}
.

Let
o_{F_{v}}
be one of the two orientations of
\mathfrak{o}_{F_{v}}
that belongs to the same component as
o_{F_{c}}^{+}
. The relative
\mathrm{Pin}^{\pm}(n)
-structure
\sigma
on
L\subset\Sigma
determines a
\mathrm{Pin}^{\pm}(n)
-structure
\sigma_{F_{v}}
of
F_{v}
. If
\sigma_{F_{v}}
lies in the same component as
\mathrm{p}_{F_{c}}^{\mathrm{triv}}
, then we choose the orientation
o_{F_{v}}
, and otherwise we choose
-o_{F_{v}}
.
Finally, the isomorphism
\psi^{\sigma}_{(w,w^{\prime})}
is given by

\displaystyle\det(D_{w}\#T_{p}W^{u}_{Z_{L}}(p))

\displaystyle\to\det(D_{w}\#T_{p}W^{u}_{Z_{L}}(p))\otimes\det(D_{v}\#0)\cong\det(D_{w^{\prime}}\#T_{p}W^{u}_{Z_{L}}(p)),

\displaystyle o_{(p,w)}

\displaystyle\mapsto o_{(p,w)}\otimes(\pm o_{F_{v}})

where the sign
\pm
is determined by
\sigma
as mentioned above.

Similarly, we choose a unitary trivialization of
\tilde{v}^{*}T(\mathbb{R}\times Y)
. Then
\tilde{v}|_{\partial{\mathbb{D}}}^{*}T(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{L})
gives a contractible loop
F_{\tilde{v}}\in\Omega_{0}(\mathrm{Lag}(\mathbb{C}^{n+1}))
. The relative
\mathrm{Pin}^{\pm}(n+1)
-structure
\tilde{\sigma}
determines an orientation
\pm o_{F_{\tilde{v}}}
of
\det(D_{\tilde{v}}\#0)
, and this defines an isomorphism

\phi^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{(\hat{x},\hat{x}^{\prime})}:\det(D_{\hat{x}}\#T_{\tilde{p}}W^{u}_{Z_{\mathcal{L}}}(\tilde{p}))\longrightarrow\det(D_{\hat{x}^{\prime}}\#T_{\tilde{p}}W^{u}_{Z_{\mathcal{L}}}(\tilde{p}))

as above. Due to (
3.22
), the front page of the diagram in the statement is isomorphic to

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\det(D_{\hat{x}}^{\mathrm{v}})\otimes\det(D_{w}\#T_{p}W^{u}_{Z_{L}}(p))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\det(D_{\hat{x}^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{v}})\otimes\det(D_{w^{\prime}}\#T_{p}W^{u}_{Z_{L}}(p))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\det(D_{w}\#T_{p}W^{u}_{Z_{L}}(p))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\det(D_{w^{\prime}}\#T_{p}W^{u}_{Z_{L}}(p))}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{(\hat{x},\hat{x}^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\hat{x}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\hat{x}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\sigma}_{(w,w^{\prime})}}

To show the commutativity of this diagram, we take a unitary trivialization of
\tilde{v}^{*}T(\mathbb{R}\times Y)
, which respects the splitting
\tilde{v}^{*}T(\mathbb{R}\times Y)\cong({\mathbb{D}}\times\mathbb{C})\oplus v^{*}T\Sigma
as in (
3.21
). Then,
F_{\tilde{v}}\in\Omega_{0}(\mathrm{Lag}(\mathbb{C}^{n+1}))
corresponding to
\tilde{v}|_{\partial{\mathbb{D}}}^{*}T(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{L})
has the form
\mathbb{R}\oplus F_{v}
, where
F_{v}\in\Omega_{0}(\mathrm{Lag}(\mathbb{C}^{n}))
corresponds to
v|_{\partial{\mathbb{D}}}^{*}TL
. Here
\mathbb{R}
denotes the real part of the first
\mathbb{C}
-factor of
\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
. Furthermore, the relative
\mathrm{Pin}^{\pm}(n+1)
-structure
\tilde{\sigma}
induces a
\mathrm{Pin}^{\pm}(n+1)
-structure
\mathrm{p}_{1}^{\mathrm{triv}}\otimes\sigma_{F_{v}}
on
F_{\tilde{v}}=\mathbb{R}\oplus F_{v}
, where
\mathrm{p}_{1}^{\mathrm{triv}}
denotes the trivial
\mathrm{Pin}^{\pm}(1)
-structure on the trivial line bundle over
S^{1}
.

The vertical part of
\phi^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{(\hat{x},\hat{x}^{\prime})}
is given by the gluing isomorphism

\det(D_{\hat{x}}^{\mathrm{v}})\otimes\det(D_{\tilde{v}}^{\mathrm{v}}\#0)\longrightarrow\det(D_{\hat{x}^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{v}})

together with the orientation on
\det(D_{\tilde{v}}^{\mathrm{v}}\#0)
determined by the relative
\mathrm{Pin}^{\pm}
-structure
\tilde{\sigma}
. Here the vertical part
D_{\tilde{v}}^{\mathrm{v}}
of
D_{\tilde{v}}
is homotopic to the standard Cauchy–Riemann operator
\overline{\partial}_{(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{R})}
. Since the associated
\mathrm{Pin}^{\pm}(1)
-structure is trivial,
\det(D_{\tilde{v}}^{\mathrm{v}}\#0)
is oriented by the canonical orientation. Then, one can readily see that
\phi^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{(\hat{x},\hat{x}^{\prime})}
maps the canonical orientation on
\det(D_{\hat{x}}^{\mathrm{v}})
to that on
\det(D_{\hat{x}^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{v}})
. This proves that the above diagram commutes.

The commutativity of the top and bottom faces of the diagram in the statement is established in
[
Zap15
]
.
The commutativity of the remaining faces follows by arguments similar to those given above.
∎

Let
\tilde{p}_{k}\in\mathcal{L}_{k}^{H}
with
(\tilde{p},k)\in\mathrm{Crit}f_{\mathcal{L}}\times m_{L}\mathbb{Z}
. Using (
3.26
), we define

\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{p}_{k}):=\varinjlim_{\hat{x}}\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{p}_{k},\hat{x}).

(3.27)

In view of Proposition
3.14
, and by abuse of notation, we write
C([\mathbf{v}])
for the induced isomorphism

C([\mathbf{v}]):\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{p}_{k})\longrightarrow\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{q}_{l}).

Following (
3.11
), we endow
\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{p}_{k})
with the grading

\deg\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{p}_{k}):=\mu(\tilde{p}_{k})=\operatorname{dim}L+\frac{2\tau_{\Sigma}k}{d}-\mathrm{ind}_{f_{L}}(p).

(3.28)

3.3.4
Floer chain complex

For real numbers
a<b
and
H\in\mathcal{H}
, we consider the set

\mathfrak{w}^{(a,b)}_{\mathcal{L}}(H):=\big\{k\in m_{L}\mathbb{Z}\,|\,\exists\,r\in(-\eta,\eta)\text{ with }h^{\prime}(e^{r})=\tfrac{k}{d}\text{ and }a<e^{r}h^{\prime}(e^{r})-h(e^{r})<b\big\},

where
H(r,y)=h(e^{r})
for
(r,y)\in\mathbb{R}\times Y
, as in Section
3.3.1
.
In view of Proposition
3.4
, (
3.7
), and (
3.8
),
(\tilde{p},k)\in\mathrm{Crit}f_{\mathcal{L}}\times\mathfrak{m}^{(a,b)}_{\mathcal{L}}(H)
determines
\tilde{p}_{k}=(r^{H}_{k},c_{\tilde{p}_{k}})\in\mathrm{Crit}\mathcal{A}_{H}
such that
c_{\tilde{p}_{k}}(0)=\tilde{p}
,
\mathfrak{w}(c_{\tilde{p}_{k}})=k
,
a<\mathcal{A}_{H}(\tilde{p}_{k})<b
, and
\tilde{p}_{k}
admits a capping.
We write
\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}}
for the Legendrian submanifold
\mathcal{L}
equipped with the lifted
\mathrm{Pin}^{\pm}
-structure
\tilde{\sigma}
.
The Floer complex associated to
{\mathcal{D}}_{H}=(f_{\mathcal{L}},Z_{\mathcal{L}},H,J_{Y})
on
\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}}
with action window
(a,b)\subset\mathbb{R}
is defined by

\mathrm{FC}^{(a,b)}_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}};\mathcal{D}_{H}):=\bigoplus_{(\tilde{p},k)}\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{p}_{k}).

where the direct sum ranges over
(\tilde{p},k)\in\mathrm{Crit}f_{\mathcal{L}}\times\mathfrak{m}^{(a,b)}_{\mathcal{L}}(H)
.
The boundary map is defined by

\partial_{\mathcal{D}_{H}}:\mathrm{FC}^{(a,b)}_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}};\mathcal{D}_{H})\to\mathrm{FC}^{(a,b)}_{*-1}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}};\mathcal{D}_{H}),\quad\partial_{\mathcal{D}_{H}}:=\bigoplus_{(\tilde{p},\tilde{q},k,l)}\sum_{[\mathbf{v}]}C([\mathbf{v}]).

(3.29)

The direct sum ranges over all
(\tilde{p},k),(\tilde{q},l)\in\mathrm{Crit}f_{\mathcal{L}}\times\mathfrak{m}^{(a,b)}_{\mathcal{L}}(H)
with
\mu(\tilde{p}_{k})-\mu(\tilde{q}_{l})=1
.
The sum runs over
[\mathbf{v}]\in\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{*}(\tilde{q}_{l},\tilde{p}_{k};\mathcal{D}_{H})
, where each
C([\mathbf{v}])
is an isomorphism from
\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{p}_{k})
to
\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{q}_{l})
. Note that
X_{H}
has chords other than those contained in
(-\eta,\eta)\times Y
. Nevertheless, by
[
CO18
, Lemmas 2.2 and 2.3]
, Floer cylinders asymptotic to chords in
(-\eta,\eta)\times Y
do not break along chords outside this region. Together with Proposition
3.12
, this implies that
\partial_{\mathcal{D}_{H}}
is a well-defined boundary operator. We denote the resulting Floer homology by

\mathrm{FH}_{i}^{(a,b)}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}};\mathcal{D}_{H}):=\mathrm{H}_{i}(\mathrm{FC}^{(a,b)}_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}};\mathcal{D}_{H}),\partial_{\mathcal{D}_{H}}).

3.4
Lagrangian Rabinowitz Floer homology

3.4.1
Floer continuation strips

The set
\mathcal{H}
of
\vee
-shaped Hamiltonians has a strict partial order
\prec
:
H_{+}\prec H_{-}
if
H_{+}<H_{-}
pointwise.
A monotone homotopy for
H_{+}\prec H_{-}
refers to a family of smooth functions
H_{s}:\mathbb{R}\times Y\to\mathbb{R}
, smoothly parametrized by
s\in\mathbb{R}
, satisfying the following requirements:

(i)

H_{s}
satisfies the condition (
3.4
) for
\mathcal{H}
with
a\in(0,\infty)
depending smoothly on
s
,

(ii)

H_{s}=H_{-}
for
s\leq-1
and
H_{s}=H_{+}
for
s\geq 1
, and
\frac{\partial}{\partial s}H_{s}\leq 0
.

By the definition of
\mathcal{H}
,
H_{s}
has the form
H_{s}(r,y)=h_{s}(e^{r})
for some smooth family of smooth functions
h_{s}:\mathbb{R}_{>0}\to\mathbb{R}
and
X_{H_{s}}
vanishes on
(-\infty,-\epsilon)\times Y
.

Let
H_{s}
be a monotone homotopy for
H_{+}\prec H_{-}
, and let
J_{Y}\in\mathcal{J}_{Y}
.
A smooth solution
\tilde{v}=(b,v):(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1],\mathbb{R}\times\{0,1\})\to(\mathbb{R}\times Y,\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{L})
of

\partial_{s}\tilde{v}+J_{Y}(\tilde{v})(\partial_{t}\tilde{v}-X_{H_{s}}(\tilde{v}))=0

(3.30)

with the asymptotic condition

\displaystyle\mathrm{ev}_{\pm}(\tilde{v})=(\mathrm{ev}_{\pm}(b),\mathrm{ev}_{\pm}(v)):=\lim_{s\to\pm\infty}\tilde{v}(s,\cdot)\in\mathrm{Crit}\mathcal{A}_{H_{\pm}}

is referred to as a
Floer continuation strip
with respect to
(H_{s},J_{Y})
.

Let
\mathbf{N}:=(N_{-},N_{+})\in(\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\})^{2}
,
N:=N_{-}+N_{+}+1
, and

\mathbf{A}=(A^{-}_{1},\dots,A^{-}_{N_{-}},A,A^{+}_{1},\dots,A^{+}_{N_{+}})\in\pi_{2}(\Sigma,L)^{N},

where
A^{-}_{i}\neq 0
and
A^{+}_{j}\neq 0
for all
1\leq i\leq N_{-}
and
1\leq j\leq N_{+}
.
Let
k_{\pm}\in m_{L}\mathbb{Z}
with
r^{H_{\pm}}_{k_{\pm}}\in(-\eta,\eta)
as in (
3.7
).
For a Floer continuation strip
\tilde{v}=(b,v)
, we use the same notation as in (
3.14
) and set

\mathfrak{a}_{\pm}(\tilde{v}):=(\mathrm{ev}_{\pm}(b),\mathfrak{w}(\mathrm{ev}_{\pm}(v))).

Definition 3.15
.

The moduli space

\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{N},k_{-},k_{+}}(\mathbf{A};H_{s},J_{Y})

consists of tuples

\mathbf{v}=(\tilde{v}^{-}_{1},\dots,\tilde{v}^{-}_{N_{-}},\tilde{v},\tilde{v}^{+}_{1},\dots,\tilde{v}^{+}_{N_{+}})

such that, for every
i=1,\dots,N_{-}
and for every
j=1,\dots,N_{+}
,

•

\tilde{v}^{-}_{i}=(b^{-}_{i},v^{-}_{i})
is a Floer strip with respect to
(H_{-},J_{Y})
with
[\pi\circ v^{-}_{i}]=A^{-}_{i}
,

•

\tilde{v}=(b,v)
is a Floer continuation strip with respect to
(H_{s},J_{Y})
with
[\pi\circ v]=A
,

•

\tilde{v}^{+}_{j}=(b^{+}_{j},v^{+}_{j})
is a Floer strip with respect to
(H_{+},J_{Y})
with
[\pi\circ v^{+}_{j}]=A^{+}_{j}
,

and for every
i=1,\dots,N_{-}-1
and
j=1,\dots,N_{+}-1
,

\displaystyle(r^{H_{-}}_{k_{-}},k_{-})=\mathfrak{a}_{-}(\tilde{v}^{-}_{1}),\quad\mathfrak{a}_{+}(\tilde{v}^{-}_{i})=\mathfrak{a}_{-}(\tilde{v}^{-}_{i+1}),\quad\mathfrak{a}_{+}(\tilde{v}^{-}_{N_{-}})=\mathfrak{a}_{-}(\tilde{v}),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{+}(\tilde{v})=\mathfrak{a}_{-}(\tilde{v}^{+}_{1}),\quad\mathfrak{a}_{+}(\tilde{v}^{+}_{j})=\mathfrak{a}_{-}(\tilde{v}^{+}_{j+1}),\quad\mathfrak{a}_{+}(\tilde{v}^{+}_{N_{+}})=(r^{H_{+}}_{k_{+}},k_{+}).

We define the subspace

\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\mathbf{N},k_{-},k_{+}}(\mathbf{A};H_{s},J_{Y})\subset\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{N},k_{-},k_{+}}(\mathbf{A};H_{s},J_{Y})

consisting of simple elements
\mathbf{v}
, i.e.,

(\pi\circ v^{-}_{1},\dots,\pi\circ v^{-}_{N_{-}},\pi\circ v,\pi\circ v^{+}_{1},\dots,\pi\circ v^{+}_{N_{+}})\in\mathcal{N}^{*}_{N}(\mathbf{A};J_{\Sigma}).

We also consider the evaluation map

\displaystyle{\mathrm{ev}}:\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{N},k_{-},k_{+}}(\mathbf{A};H_{s},J_{Y})\to\mathcal{L}^{2N_{-}}\times\mathcal{L}^{2}\times\mathcal{L}^{2N_{+}},

\displaystyle{\mathrm{ev}}(\mathbf{v}):=\big(\mathrm{ev}_{-,0}(\tilde{v}^{-}_{1}),\mathrm{ev}_{+,0}(\tilde{v}^{-}_{1}),\dots,\mathrm{ev}_{-,0}(\tilde{v}^{-}_{N_{-}}),\mathrm{ev}_{+,0}(\tilde{v}^{-}_{N_{-}}),\mathrm{ev}_{-,0}(\tilde{v}),\mathrm{ev}_{+,0}(\tilde{v}),

\displaystyle\hskip 54.06023pt\mathrm{ev}_{-,0}(\tilde{v}^{+}_{1}),\mathrm{ev}_{+,0}(\tilde{v}^{+}_{1}),\dots,\mathrm{ev}_{-,0}(\tilde{v}^{+}_{N_{+}}),\mathrm{ev}_{+,0}(\tilde{v}^{+}_{N_{+}})\big),

where
\mathrm{ev}_{\pm,0}(\tilde{v}):=\mathrm{ev}_{\pm}(v)(0)
.
Let us abbreviate
{\mathcal{D}}_{H_{s}}=(f_{\mathcal{L}},Z_{\mathcal{L}},H_{s},J_{Y})
. Let
\tilde{p}^{H_{+}}_{k_{+}}\in\mathrm{Crit}\mathcal{A}_{H_{+}}
and
\tilde{q}^{H_{-}}_{k_{-}}\in\mathrm{Crit}\mathcal{A}_{H_{-}}
be the Hamiltonian chords of
H_{\pm}
in
(-\eta,\eta)\times Y
with relative winding number
k_{\pm}
corresponding to
\tilde{p},\tilde{q}\in\mathrm{Crit}f_{\mathcal{L}}
via the correspondence (
3.9
), respectively.

Definition 3.16
.

We define the moduli space

\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{N}}(\tilde{q}^{H_{-}}_{k_{-}},\tilde{p}^{H_{+}}_{k_{+}};\mathbf{A};{\mathcal{D}}_{H_{s}}):=\mathrm{ev}^{-1}\left(W^{u}_{Z_{\mathcal{L}}}(\tilde{p})\times\Delta_{Z_{\mathcal{L}}}^{N-1}\times W^{s}_{Z_{\mathcal{L}}}(\tilde{q})\right).

The subspace of simple elements is defined by

\begin{split}&\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\mathbf{N}}(\tilde{q}^{H_{-}}_{k_{-}},\tilde{p}^{H_{+}}_{k_{+}};\mathbf{A};{\mathcal{D}}_{H_{s}}):=\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{N}}(\tilde{q}^{H_{-}}_{k_{-}},\tilde{p}^{H_{+}}_{k_{+}};\mathbf{A};{\mathcal{D}}_{H_{s}})\,\cap\,\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\mathbf{N},k_{-},k_{+}}(\mathbf{A};H_{s},J_{Y}).\end{split}

Proposition 3.17
.

Let
\mathcal{J}_{(Y,\mathcal{L})}^{\mathrm{reg}}\subset\mathcal{J}_{Y}
be as in Proposition
3.11
.
Then, for every choice of
\tilde{p}^{H_{+}}_{k_{+}}\in\mathrm{Crit}\mathcal{A}_{H_{+}}
,
\tilde{q}^{H_{-}}_{k_{-}}\in\mathrm{Crit}\mathcal{A}_{H_{-}}
,
\mathbf{N}
,
\mathbf{A}
as above
and for every
J_{Y}\in\mathcal{J}_{(Y,\mathcal{L})}^{\mathrm{reg}}
, the moduli space

\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\mathbf{N}}(\tilde{q}^{H_{-}}_{k_{-}},\tilde{p}^{H_{+}}_{k_{+}};\mathbf{A};\mathcal{D}_{H_{s}})

is a smooth manifold of dimension

\mu(\tilde{p}^{H_{+}}_{k_{+}})-\mu(\tilde{q}^{H_{-}}_{k_{-}})+N-1
.

Proof.

The proof is almost identical to that of Proposition
3.11
. For the corresponding proof in the periodic case, we refer to
[
BKK24
, Proposition 4.5.(a)]
.
∎

There is a free
\mathbb{R}^{N-1}
-action on
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{N}}^{*}(\tilde{q}_{k_{-}}^{H_{-}},\tilde{p}_{k_{+}}^{H_{+}};\mathbf{A};\mathcal{D}_{H_{s}})
translating each
\tilde{v}_{i}^{-}
and
\tilde{v}_{j}^{+}
. The quotient space

\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{*}(\tilde{q}_{k_{-}}^{H_{-}},\tilde{p}_{k_{+}}^{H_{+}};\mathcal{D}_{H_{s}}):=\bigcup_{\mathbf{N},\mathbf{A}}\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\mathbf{N}}(\tilde{q}_{k_{-}}^{H_{-}},\tilde{p}_{k_{+}}^{H_{+}};\mathbf{A};\mathcal{D}_{H_{s}})/\mathbb{R}^{N-1},

is a smooth manifold of dimension
\mu(\tilde{p}_{k_{+}}^{H_{+}})-\mu(\tilde{q}_{k_{-}}^{H_{-}})
by Proposition
3.17
.

Proposition 3.18
.

Assume that we are in the same setting as in Proposition
3.17
.

(a)

Assume condition (C2), i.e.,
N_{L}>2
. If
\mu(\tilde{p}^{H_{+}}_{k_{+}})-\mu(\tilde{q}^{H_{-}}_{k_{-}})\leq 1
, all elements of the moduli space
{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathbf{N}}(\tilde{q}^{H_{-}}_{k_{-}},\tilde{p}^{H_{+}}_{k_{+}};\mathbf{A};\mathcal{D}_{H_{s}})
are simple, i.e.,

{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathbf{N}}(\tilde{q}^{H_{-}}_{k_{-}},\tilde{p}^{H_{+}}_{k_{+}};\mathbf{A};\mathcal{D}_{H_{s}})={\mathcal{M}}^{*}_{\mathbf{N}}(\tilde{q}^{H_{-}}_{k_{-}},\tilde{p}^{H_{+}}_{k_{+}};\mathbf{A};\mathcal{D}_{H_{s}}).

Moreover, there exist
r_{\pm}\in\mathbb{R}
such that every element in this moduli space has its image in
(r_{-},r_{+})\times Y
. Consequently, this moduli space is compact up to breaking.

(b)

Assume
N_{L}\geq 2
and
\mu(\tilde{p}^{H_{+}}_{k_{+}})=\mu(\tilde{q}^{H_{-}}_{k_{-}})
. Then

{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathbf{N}}(\tilde{q}^{H_{-}}_{k_{-}},\tilde{p}^{H_{+}}_{k_{+}};\mathbf{A};\mathcal{D}_{H_{s}})

is nonempty if and only if

N_{+}=N_{-}=0,\quad\tilde{p}=\tilde{q},\quad k_{+}=k_{-}.

In this case, it consists of a single element
\mathbf{v}=\tilde{v}
whose projection to
\Sigma
is a constant map at
p=q
.

We postpone the proof of the proposition to Section
3.4.3
.

3.4.2
Lagrangian Rabinowitz Floer homology of
\mathcal{L}

Let
H_{s}
be a monotone homotopy for
H_{+}\prec H_{-}
, and let
J_{Y}\in\mathcal{J}^{\mathrm{reg}}_{(Y,\mathcal{L})}
.
Proposition
3.18
.(b) implies that the only relevant moduli spaces for the filtered continuation map with action window
(a,b)
are
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{*}(\tilde{p}_{k}^{H_{-}},\tilde{p}_{k}^{H_{+}};\mathcal{D}_{H_{s}})=\{\mathbf{v}=\tilde{v}\}
, where
(\tilde{p},k)
ranges over
\mathrm{Crit}f_{\mathcal{L}}\times\mathfrak{w}^{(a,b)}_{\mathcal{L}}(H_{+})
.
Gluing a capping disk
\hat{x}
of
\tilde{p}^{H_{+}}_{k}
and
\mathbf{v}
, we obtain a capping disk
\hat{y}
of
\tilde{p}^{H_{-}}_{k}
. As in the boundary map case discussed earlier, see also
[
Zap15
, Section 3.9.1]
, we obtain an isomorphism of determinant lines

\det(D_{\hat{x}}\#T_{\tilde{p}}W^{u}_{Z_{\mathcal{L}}}(\tilde{p}))\longrightarrow\det(D_{\hat{y}}\#T_{\tilde{p}}W^{u}_{Z_{\mathcal{L}}}(\tilde{p}))

and in turn an isomorphism of orientation lines

C([\mathbf{v}]):\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{p}^{H_{+}}_{k},\hat{x})\to\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{p}^{H_{-}}_{k},\hat{y}).

As in Proposition
3.14
, one can see that this isomorphism is independent of the choice of cappings. Thus we simply write

C([\mathbf{v}]):\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{p}^{H_{+}}_{k})\longrightarrow\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{p}^{H_{-}}_{k}).

Moreover, the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{p}^{H_{+}}_{k},\hat{x})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{p}^{H_{-}}_{k},\hat{y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{o}(p,w)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{o}(p,w),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C({[\mathbf{v}]})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\hat{x}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\hat{y}}}

where
w=\pi_{\mathbb{R}\times Y}\circ\hat{x}=\pi_{\mathbb{R}\times Y}\circ\hat{y}
.
We define the chain-level continuation map by

\mathfrak{c}=\mathfrak{c}_{H_{+},H_{-}}:\mathrm{FC}_{*}^{(a,b)}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}};{\mathcal{D}}_{H_{+}})\to\mathrm{FC}_{*}^{(a,b)}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}};{\mathcal{D}}_{H_{-}}),\qquad\mathfrak{c}:=\bigoplus_{(\tilde{p},k)}C([\mathbf{v}]),

where the direct sum runs over all
(\tilde{p},k)\in\mathrm{Crit}f_{\mathcal{L}}\times\mathfrak{w}^{(a,b)}_{\mathcal{L}}(H_{+})
, and
\mathbf{v}
is the unique element of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}(\tilde{p}^{H_{-}}_{k},\tilde{p}^{H_{+}}_{k};\mathcal{D}_{H_{s}})
.
The map
\mathfrak{c}
is a chain map by Proposition
3.18
.(a), and the induced continuation homomorphism on homology is independent of the choice of
H_{s}
.
The Floer homology modules
\mathrm{FH}_{*}^{(a,b)}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}};{\mathcal{D}}_{H})
and the continuation homomorphisms form a direct system over
(\mathcal{H},\prec)
.
We define the Rabinowitz Floer homology of
\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}}
for the finite action window
(a,b)\subset\mathbb{R}
by

\mathrm{RFH}_{*}^{(a,b)}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}}):=\varinjlim_{H\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{FH}_{*}^{(a,b)}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}};{\mathcal{D}}_{H}).

On the other hand, the natural action filtration chain homomorphisms

\mathrm{FC}_{*}^{(a,b)}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}};\mathcal{D}_{H})\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{FC}_{*}^{(a^{\prime},b)}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}};\mathcal{D}_{H}),\quad\mathrm{FC}_{*}^{(a,b)}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}};\mathcal{D}_{H})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{FC}_{*}^{(a,b^{\prime})}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}};\mathcal{D}_{H})

for
a<a^{\prime}<b<b^{\prime}
induce a bidirect system on
\mathrm{RFH}_{*}^{(a,b)}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}})
.
The (full) Rabinowitz Floer homology of
\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}}
is defined by

\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}}):=\varinjlim_{b\uparrow+\infty}\varprojlim_{a\downarrow-\infty}\mathrm{RFH}_{*}^{(a,b)}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}}).

Note that the limits on action window and Hamiltonians stabilize in each degree since there are only finitely many generators in each degree according to (
3.11
) and
\mathfrak{c}(\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{p}^{H_{+}}_{k}))=\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{p}^{H_{-}}_{k})
.
Therefore, it is reasonable to think of

\mathrm{RFH}_{j}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}})
as the homology of

\mathrm{RFC}_{j}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}}):=\varinjlim_{H\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{FC}_{j}^{(a,b)}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}};\mathcal{D}_{H})

for a sufficiently large action window
(a,b)
depending on
j
. We write

\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{p}_{k}):=\varinjlim_{H\in\mathcal{H}}\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{p}^{H}_{k})

for the rank-one free submodule of
\mathrm{RFC}_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}})
associated with
(\tilde{p},k)
.
It also follows that there is a canonical isomorphism

\mathrm{RFH}_{j}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}})\cong\mathrm{FH}_{j}^{(a,b)}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}};\mathcal{D}_{H})

for sufficiently large
H\in\mathcal{H}
and
(a,b)
depending on
j
.

Remark 3.19
.

When
N_{L}<\infty
, Remark
2.6
shows that
\mathrm{QC}_{*}(L^{\sigma})
admits a
\mathbb{Z}[T,T^{-1}]
-module structure. By the proof of Theorem
1.1
, see (
4.5
), this induces a
\mathbb{Z}[T,T^{-1}]
-module structure on
\mathrm{RFC}_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}})
, where the action of
T
is given by
T:\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{p}_{k})\to\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{p}_{k+m_{L}})
when
N_{L}
is even, and by
T:\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{p}_{k})\to\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{p}_{k+2m_{L}})
otherwise. Thus,

\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}})
admits a
\mathbb{Z}[T,T^{-1}]
-module structure. The multiplication by
T
should coincide with the Seidel morphism constructed using the
S^{1}
-action on
Y
given by the Reeb flow, see
[
Sei97
,
HL09
,
Ueb19
]
.

Lemma 3.20
.

If
N_{L}<\infty
, then
\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}})
is a finitely generated
\mathbb{Z}[T,T^{-1}]
-module.

Proof.

From the index formula in (
3.11
), we know that
\mathrm{RFC}_{j}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}})
has finitely many generators for each
j\in\mathbb{Z}
. Since
T:\mathrm{RFH}_{j}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}})\to\mathrm{RFH}_{j+N_{L}}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}})
is an isomorphism for every
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, we conclude that
\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}})
is finitely generated over
\mathbb{Z}[T,T^{-1}]
.
∎

3.4.3
Proofs of Proposition
3.12
and Proposition
3.18

Before embarking on the proofs, we briefly describe the setup for Floer strips with punctures. We refer to
[
DL19a
,
BKK24
]
for details.
Let
\Gamma:=\Gamma_{\partial}\cup\Gamma_{\mathrm{int}}
where
\Gamma_{\partial}
and
\Gamma_{\mathrm{int}}
are (possibly empty) finite subsets of
\mathbb{R}\times\{0,1\}
and
\mathbb{R}\times(0,1)
, respectively.
We consider smooth maps

\tilde{v}=(b,v):\big((\mathbb{R}\times[0,1])\setminus\Gamma,(\mathbb{R}\times\{0,1\})\setminus\Gamma_{\partial}\big)\longrightarrow(\mathbb{R}\times Y,\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{L})

solving the Floer equation in (
3.12
)
and converging to critical points of
\mathcal{A}_{H}
at the positive and negative ends.
Furthermore, we require the map
\tilde{v}
to satisfy the following asymptotic behavior near punctures. For
z\in\Gamma_{\partial}
and holomorphic strip-like coordinates
\epsilon_{z}:(-\infty,0]\times[0,1]\to(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1])\setminus\{z\}
near
z
, there is a Reeb chord
c_{z}:([0,T],\{0,T\})\to(Y,\mathcal{L})
such that

\lim_{s\to-\infty}(b\circ\epsilon_{z}(s,t),v\circ\epsilon_{z}(s,t))=(-\infty,c_{z}(Tt)).

For
z\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{int}}
, a similar asymptotic behavior holds with
c_{z}
replaced by a closed Reeb orbit
\gamma_{z}:\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z}\to Y
.
We refer to such solutions as
punctured Floer strips
.
If
\tilde{v}
is a punctured Floer strip, then the projection
\pi\circ v
has finite energy.
By the removal of singularity theorem,
\pi\circ v
extends to a
J_{\Sigma}
-holomorphic map defined on
\mathbb{D}
, identifying
\mathbb{D}\setminus\{\pm 1\}
with
\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]
.
We abuse notation and write
\pi\circ v:(\mathbb{D},\partial\mathbb{D})\to(\Sigma,L)
for the extended map.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{N,m}(\tilde{q}_{l},\tilde{p}_{k};\mathbf{A};\mathcal{D}_{H})
be the moduli space of punctured Floer strips with trajectories, defined by replacing Floer strips with punctured ones in Definition
3.10
.
Here,
m
denotes the total number of punctures, which are allowed to move freely within
\mathbb{R}\times\{0,1\}
or
\mathbb{R}\times(0,1)
, depending on whether they belong to
\Gamma_{\partial}
or
\Gamma_{\mathrm{int}}
, respectively. We again have

\begin{split}\Pi:\mathcal{M}_{N,m}(\tilde{q}_{l},\tilde{p}_{k};\mathbf{A};\mathcal{D}_{H})&\longrightarrow\mathcal{N}_{N}(q,p;{\bf A};\mathcal{D})\\[2.15277pt]
(\tilde{v}_{1},\dots,\tilde{v}_{N})&\longmapsto(\pi\circ v_{1},\dots,\pi\circ v_{N}).\end{split}

For a monotone homotopy
H_{s}
for
H_{+}\prec H_{-}
, one can analogously define the punctured Floer continuation strip and the moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{N},m}(\tilde{q}^{H_{-}}_{k_{-}},\tilde{p}^{H_{+}}_{k_{+}};\mathbf{A};\mathcal{D}_{H_{s}})
.

Proof of Proposition
3.12
.

Statement (a) follows from
[
BC07
, Proposition 3.1.3]
.
We therefore focus on the proof of (b).
The proof of (b) proceeds along similar lines to those in
[
DL19b
, Theorem 9.1]
and
[
BKK24
, Proposition 4.5]
.

Assume for contradiction that there exists a sequence
\{\mathbf{v}_{\nu}\}_{\nu\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}(\tilde{q}_{l},\tilde{p}_{k};\mathcal{D}_{H})
with
\mu(\tilde{p}_{k})-\mu(\tilde{q}_{l})\leq 2
such that Floer cylinders in
\mathbf{v}_{\nu}
are not contained in a bounded subset of
\mathbb{R}\times Y
. Then,
by the maximum principle, they escape to the negative end of
\mathbb{R}\times Y
.
By the SFT compactness theorem in
[
BEH+03
,
BO09
]
,
\mathbf{v}_{\nu}
converges to a Floer-holomorphic building whose top component lies in
\mathcal{M}_{N,m}(\tilde{q}_{l},\tilde{p}_{k};\mathbf{A};\mathcal{D}_{H})
with
m\geq 1
. We call this component
\mathbf{v}_{\infty}
.

Let
\{k_{i}\}^{m_{\mathrm{int}}}_{i=1}
and
\{k^{\prime}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m_{\partial}}
be the (relative) winding numbers of the asymptotic closed Reeb orbits
\{\gamma_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m_{\mathrm{int}}}
and Reeb chords
\{c_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m_{\partial}}
of
\mathbf{v}_{\infty}
at punctures, respectively, where
m_{\mathrm{int}}+m_{\partial}=m
.
A closed Reeb orbit
\gamma_{i}
can be viewed as a map between circles,
\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z}\to Y_{p}
for some
p\in\Sigma
, and the degree of this map is
k_{i}
.
From Proposition
3.7
and a similar computation for
\gamma_{i}
in
[
BKK24
, Equation (2.11)]
, we have

\mu_{\mathrm{CZ}}(\gamma_{i})=2\tau_{\Sigma}k_{i},\qquad\mu_{\mathrm{RS}}(c_{j})=\frac{2\tau_{\Sigma}k_{j}^{\prime}}{d},

(3.31)

where
\mu_{\mathrm{CZ}}
denotes the Conley–Zehnder index.
We compute

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mu(\tilde{p}_{k})-\mu(\tilde{q}_{l})&=\operatorname{ind}_{f_{L}}({q})-\operatorname{ind}_{f_{L}}({p})+2\frac{\tau_{\Sigma}}{d}(k-l)\\
&=\operatorname{ind}_{f_{L}}(q)-\operatorname{ind}_{f_{L}}(p)+2\frac{\tau_{\Sigma}}{d}\Big(k-l-d\sum_{i=1}^{m_{\mathrm{int}}}k_{i}-\sum_{j=1}^{m_{\partial}}k^{\prime}_{j}\Big)\\
&\quad+2\frac{\tau_{\Sigma}}{d}\Big(d\sum_{i=1}^{m_{\mathrm{int}}}k_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{m_{\partial}}k^{\prime}_{j}\Big)\\
&=\operatorname{ind}_{f_{L}}(q)-\operatorname{ind}_{f_{L}}(p)+\mu_{L}(\mathbf{A})+\sum_{i=1}^{m_{\mathrm{int}}}\mu_{\mathrm{CZ}}(\gamma_{i})+\sum_{j=1}^{m_{\partial}}\mu_{\mathrm{RS}}(c_{j}).\end{split}

(3.32)

The last equality follows from (
3.31
) and a similar computation for
\mu_{L}(\mathbf{A})
.

We reach a contradiction by showing that
\Pi(\mathbf{v}_{\infty})\in\mathcal{N}_{N}(q,p;\mathbf{A};\mathcal{D})
cannot exist.
We first treat the case of
\operatorname{dim}L\geq 3
. Following the argument of
[
BC07
, Proposition 3.1.3]
, which also assumes
\operatorname{dim}L\geq 3
, we take an underlying simple chain of pearls of
\Pi(\mathbf{v}_{\infty})
, and denote by
\mathcal{N}^{*}_{N^{*}}(q,p;\mathbf{A}^{*};\mathcal{D})
the moduli space containing it, where
N^{*}\leq N
.
From the proof of Proposition
2.4
.(a), it follows that

\operatorname{dim}\mathcal{N}^{*}_{N^{*}}(q,p;\mathbf{A}^{*};\mathcal{D})=\operatorname{ind}_{f_{L}}(q)-\operatorname{ind}_{f_{L}}(p)+\mu_{L}(\mathbf{A}^{*})+N^{*}-1.

This formula still holds when some entries of
\mathbf{A}^{*}
are zero.
Let
N^{*}_{0}
denote the number of zero entries in
\mathbf{A}^{*}
.
Due to a free
\mathbb{R}
-action on each non-constant disk, we have

\operatorname{dim}\mathcal{N}^{*}_{N^{*}}(q,p;\mathbf{A}^{*};\mathcal{D})\geq N^{*}_{1}:=N^{*}-N^{*}_{0}.

Furthermore, a Floer strip in
\mathbf{v}_{\infty}
which projects to a constant disk in
\Sigma
has at least one puncture.
Hence, we have

m=m_{\mathrm{int}}+m_{\partial}\geq N^{*}_{0}.

(3.33)

Combining the above estimates with the assumption
\mu(\tilde{p}_{k})-\mu(\tilde{q}_{l})\leq 2
, we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}2&\geq\operatorname{dim}\mathcal{N}^{*}_{N^{*}}(q,p;\mathbf{A}^{*};\mathcal{D})-N^{*}+1+\mu_{L}(\mathbf{A})-\mu_{L}(\mathbf{A}^{*})+\sum_{i=1}^{m_{\mathrm{int}}}\mu_{\mathrm{CZ}}(\gamma_{i})+\sum_{j=1}^{m_{\partial}}\mu_{\mathrm{RS}}(c_{j})\\
&\geq-N^{*}_{0}+1+\mu_{L}(\mathbf{A})-\mu_{L}(\mathbf{A}^{*})+\sum_{i=1}^{m_{\mathrm{int}}}\mu_{\mathrm{CZ}}(\gamma_{i})+\sum_{j=1}^{m_{\partial}}\mu_{\mathrm{RS}}(c_{j})\\
&\geq-m_{\mathrm{int}}-m_{\partial}+1+\mu_{L}(\mathbf{A})-\mu_{L}(\mathbf{A}^{*})+\sum_{i=1}^{m_{\mathrm{int}}}\mu_{\mathrm{CZ}}(\gamma_{i})+\sum_{j=1}^{m_{\partial}}\mu_{\mathrm{RS}}(c_{j})\\
&\geq 1+(\mu_{L}(\mathbf{A})-\mu_{L}(\mathbf{A}^{*}))+\sum_{i=1}^{m_{\mathrm{int}}}(\mu_{\mathrm{CZ}}(\gamma_{i})-1)+\sum_{j=1}^{m_{\partial}}(\mu_{\mathrm{RS}}(c_{j})-1).\end{split}

(3.34)

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
\omega(\pi_{2}(\Sigma))\neq 0
since otherwise there is no contractible periodic Reeb orbit and
m_{\mathrm{int}}=0
. Thus, the minimal Chern number
c_{\Sigma}
of
\Sigma
is finite, and
N_{L}
divides
2c_{\Sigma}
.
By condition (C2), i.e.,
N_{L}>2
, we have
c_{\Sigma}\geq 2
.
Since
\gamma_{i}
and
c_{j}
are contractible, (
3.31
) yields

\mu_{\mathrm{CZ}}(\gamma_{i})\geq 2c_{\Sigma}\geq 4,\qquad\mu_{\mathrm{RS}}(c_{j})\geq N_{L}\geq 3.

(3.35)

These inequalities together with (
3.34
) force
m_{\mathrm{int}}=m_{\partial}=0
.
This contradicts the assumption
m\geq 1
, completing the proof of Proposition
3.12
.(b) for the case
\operatorname{dim}L\geq 3
.

For the case that
\operatorname{dim}L\leq 2
, we compute from (
3.32
)

\displaystyle 0\leq\mu_{L}(\mathbf{A})

\displaystyle\leq 2+\operatorname{ind}_{f_{L}}(p)-\operatorname{ind}_{f_{L}}(q)-\sum_{i=1}^{m_{\mathrm{int}}}\mu_{\mathrm{CZ}}(\gamma_{i})-\sum_{j=1}^{m_{\partial}}\mu_{\mathrm{RS}}(c_{j})

\displaystyle\leq 4-\sum_{i=1}^{m_{\mathrm{int}}}\mu_{\mathrm{CZ}}(\gamma_{i})-\sum_{j=1}^{m_{\partial}}\mu_{\mathrm{RS}}(c_{j}).

Using the inequalities in (
3.35
), we deduce
\mathbf{A}=0
. This implies that there is a flow line of
Z_{L}
from
q
to
p
, and thus
\operatorname{ind}_{f_{L}}(q)\geq\operatorname{ind}_{f_{L}}(p)
. The above estimate together with (
3.35
) yields the contradiction
\mu_{L}(\mathbf{A})<0
.
This completes the proof of Proposition
3.12
.(b). ∎

Proof of Proposition
3.18
.(a).

For
\mathbf{v}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathbf{N}}(\tilde{q}^{H_{-}}_{k_{-}},\tilde{p}^{H_{+}}_{k_{+}};\mathbf{A};\mathcal{D}_{H_{s}})
with
\mu(\tilde{p}^{H_{+}}_{k_{+}})-\mu(\tilde{q}^{H_{-}}_{k_{-}})\leq 1
, we show that
\Pi(\mathbf{v})\in\mathcal{N}_{N}(q,p;\mathbf{A};\mathcal{D})
is simple. Let
\mathcal{N}^{*}_{N^{*}}(q,p;\mathbf{A}^{*};\mathcal{D})
be the moduli space containing the underlying simple chain of pearls of
\Pi(\mathbf{v})
. Note that at most one entry of
\mathbf{A}^{*}
, namely the one corresponding to a Floer continuation strip, can be zero. If all entries of
\mathbf{A}^{*}
are nonzero, there is a free
\mathbb{R}^{N^{*}}
-action given by the translations of the nonconstant disks. Similarly, in the case that one entry of
\mathbf{A}^{*}
is zero, there is instead a free
\mathbb{R}^{N^{*}-1}
-action. However, the constant disk corresponding to the zero entry of
\mathbf{A}^{*}
is free to move along an integral curve of
Z_{L}
. This gives an additional free
\mathbb{R}
-action.
Therefore, in either case, we have
\operatorname{dim}\mathcal{N}^{*}_{N^{*}}(q,p;\mathbf{A}^{*};\mathcal{D})\geq N^{*}
, and deduce

\begin{split}1\geq\mu(\tilde{p}^{H_{+}}_{k_{+}})-\mu(\tilde{q}^{H_{-}}_{k_{-}})&=\operatorname{ind}_{f_{L}}(q)-\operatorname{ind}_{f_{L}}(p)+\mu_{L}(\mathbf{A})\\
&=\operatorname{dim}\mathcal{N}^{*}_{N^{*}}(q,p;\mathbf{A}^{*};\mathcal{D})-N^{*}+1-\mu_{L}(\mathbf{A}^{*})+\mu_{L}(\mathbf{A})\\
&\geq 1-\mu_{L}(\mathbf{A}^{*})+\mu_{L}(\mathbf{A}).\end{split}

This implies
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{A}^{*}
, and we conclude
\Pi(\mathbf{v})
is simple.

For compactness, we argue as in the proof of Proposition
3.12
.(b). The only difference is that (
3.33
) is replaced by
m_{\mathrm{int}}+m_{\partial}\geq N^{*}_{0}-1
.
The term
-1
arises from the fact that, in contrast to a nontrivial Floer strip, a nontrivial Floer continuation strip may project to a constant disk in
\Sigma
. This does not pose a problem as the left-hand side of the estimate in (
3.34
) is replaced by 1 instead of 2 in this case.
This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.18
.(b).

The proof is similar to that of
[
BKK24
, Proposition 4.5.(b)]
, but simpler.
Although (
3.35
) is weakened to

\mu_{\mathrm{CZ}}(\gamma_{i})\geq 2c_{\Sigma}\geq 2,\qquad\mu_{\mathrm{RS}}(c_{j})\geq N_{L}\geq 2

under our relaxed assumption
N_{L}\geq 2
, this remains sufficient to deduce the simpleness of the moduli space in the case where
\mu(\tilde{p}^{H_{+}}_{k_{+}})=\mu(\tilde{q}^{H_{-}}_{k_{-}})
.

Next, we show that, for every
\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{N}}(\tilde{q}^{H_{-}}_{k_{-}},\tilde{p}^{H_{+}}_{k_{+}};\mathbf{A};\mathcal{D}_{H_{s}})
,
\Pi(\mathbf{v})\in\mathcal{N}^{*}_{N}(q,p;\mathbf{A};\mathcal{D})
is a constant disk. Recall that there is a free
\mathbb{R}^{N-1}
-action on the shadow of Floer cylinders in
\mathbf{v}
. If the projection of the Floer continuation cylinder
\tilde{v}
in
\mathbf{v}
is nonconstant, we have an additional free
\mathbb{R}
-action. This contradicts the fact that
\operatorname{dim}\mathcal{N}^{*}_{N}(q,p;\mathbf{A};\mathcal{D})=N-1
, which follows from
\mu(\tilde{p}^{H_{+}}_{k_{+}})=\mu(\tilde{q}^{H_{-}}_{k_{-}})
. Thus, the projection of
\tilde{v}
is constant. Suppose
N>1
, i.e. there is a Floer cylinder in
\mathbf{v}
, or
p\neq q
. In either case, there is a free
\mathbb{R}
-action induced by translating the constant disk
\pi_{\mathbb{R}\times Y}\circ\tilde{v}
along the integral curves of
Z_{L}
, which again leads to a contradiction. Therefore, we conclude
N=1
,
p=q
, and
k_{-}=k_{+}
by the index formula.

It remains to show that such
\tilde{v}
uniquely exists. Since
p=q=\pi_{\mathbb{R}\times Y}\circ\tilde{v}
is constant, we write

\tilde{v}=(b,v):(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1],\mathbb{R}\times\{0,1\})\longrightarrow(\mathbb{R}\times Y_{p},\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{L}_{p}).

Since
\mathcal{L}_{p}=Y_{p}\cap\mathcal{L}
is a finite set,
v(s,0)
and
v(s,1)
are constant in
s
. This proves
\tilde{p}=\tilde{q}
.
The Floer equation (
3.30
) reads

\begin{cases}\partial_{s}b-\alpha|_{Y_{p}}(\partial_{t}v)+h^{\prime}_{s}(e^{b})=0,\\[2.15277pt]
\partial_{t}b+\alpha|_{Y_{p}}(\partial_{s}v)=0,\end{cases}

with the boundary condition, namely
v(\mathbb{R},0)
and
v(\mathbb{R},1)
are points in
\mathcal{L}_{p}
.
Identifying
(Y_{p},\mathcal{L}_{p})
with
(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z},\frac{1}{d}\mathbb{Z}/\mathbb{Z})
, we have
v:(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1],\mathbb{R}\times\{0,1\})\to(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z},\{0,\tfrac{k}{d}\})
, where
k:=k_{+}=k_{-}
.
We define
g(s,t):=v(s,t)-\frac{k}{d}t
. Then, we have

(b,g):(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1],\mathbb{R}\times\{0,1\})\longrightarrow(\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}),\mathbb{R}\times\{0\})

and

\begin{cases}\partial_{s}b-\partial_{t}g+h^{\prime}_{s}(e^{b})-\frac{k}{d}=0,\\[2.15277pt]
\partial_{t}b+\partial_{s}g=0,\\[2.15277pt]
\lim\limits_{s\to\pm\infty}g(s,\cdot)=0.\end{cases}

(3.36)

Differentiating (
3.36
), we obtain
\Delta g+h_{s}^{\prime\prime}(e^{b})e^{b}\partial_{s}g=0
.
Since
g
vanishes on the boundary
\mathbb{R}\times\{0,1\}
and converges to zero as
s\to\pm\infty
, the maximum principle implies that
g
is identically zero.
Then the equations in (
3.36
) reduce to an ODE for
b
, which is the same as the one in
[
BKK24
, Equation (4.7)]
. Therefore, the proof of
[
BKK24
, Proposition 4.7]
shows that there exists a unique solution
b(s,t)
, which is independent of
t
. This completes the proof.
∎

3.5
Product structure on Lagrangian Rabinowitz Floer homology

In contrast to the assumption (C2), namely
N_{L}>2
, we assume the following stronger condition for necessary compactness results for a product structure on
\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}})
.

(
\rm{C^{P}}
)

The minimal Maslov number
N_{L}
of
L
is greater than
\max\{\frac{1}{2}(\operatorname{dim}L+1),2\}
.

We consider a strip with a slit:

S^{P}:=\big((\mathbb{R}\times[-1,0])\sqcup(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1])\big)/\sim.

The equivalence relation is given by
(s,0^{+})\sim(s,0^{-})
for
s\leq 0
, where
0^{+}\in[0,1]
and
0^{-}\in[-1,0]
.
We endow
S^{P}
with a natural complex structure. Then, the global coordinate
z=s+it
is holomorphic everywhere except at the point
(0,0^{-})=(0,0^{+})
, see
[
AS10
, Section 3.2]
for details.
For
(H,J_{Y})\in\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{J}_{Y}
, we consider a smooth map
\tilde{v}=(b,v):(S^{P},\partial S^{P})\to(\mathbb{R}\times Y,\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{L})
that solves the Floer equation

\partial_{s}\tilde{v}+J_{Y}(\tilde{v})(\partial_{t}\tilde{v}-X_{H}(\tilde{v}))=0

in the interior of
S^{P}
, and is
asymptotic to a critical point of
\mathcal{A}_{H}
at the positive ends and to a critical point of
\mathcal{A}_{2H}
at the negative end, i.e.,

\displaystyle(\mathrm{ev}^{m}_{+}(\tilde{v}))(t)

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{ev}^{m}_{+}(b),\mathrm{ev}^{m}_{+}(v))(t):=\lim_{s\to+\infty}\tilde{v}(s,t-2+m)\in\mathrm{Crit}\mathcal{A}_{H}\;\text{ for }m\in\{1,2\},

\displaystyle(\mathrm{ev}_{-}(\tilde{v}))(t)

\displaystyle=({\mathrm{ev}}_{-}(b),{\mathrm{ev}}_{-}(v))(t):=\lim_{s\to-\infty}\tilde{v}(s,2t-1)\in\mathrm{Crit}\mathcal{A}_{2H}.

We refer to such maps as
Floer triangles
with respect to the pair
(H,J_{Y})
.
As before, the projection
\pi\circ v
extends to a
J_{\Sigma}
-holomorphic disk with boundary in
L
, passing through the points
\pi(\mathrm{ev}_{+}^{1}(v))
,
\pi(\mathrm{ev}_{+}^{2}(v))
, and
\pi(\mathrm{ev}_{-}(v))
.

Let
\mathbf{k}=(k,k^{\prime},k^{\prime\prime})\in(m_{L}\mathbb{Z})^{3}
. Throughout this section, whenever
\mathbf{k}
is used, we assume that
r^{H}_{k},r^{H}_{k^{\prime}},r^{2H}_{k^{\prime\prime}}\in(-\eta,\eta)
exist as in (
3.7
). Let
\mathbf{N},N
, and
\mathbf{A}
be as in (
2.7
), i.e.,

\begin{split}&\mathbf{N}:=(N_{1},N_{2},N_{3})\in(\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\})^{3},\qquad N:=N_{1}+N_{2}+N_{3},\\
&\mathbf{A}:=(A_{1},\dots,A_{N_{1}},A,A_{N_{1}+1},\dots,A_{N})\in(\pi_{2}(\Sigma,L))^{N+1},\quad A_{i}\neq 0\quad\;\;i\in\{1,\dots,N\}.\end{split}

For a Floer triangle
\tilde{v}=(b,v)
, we use the analogous notation

\mathfrak{a}_{-}(\tilde{v}):=(\mathrm{ev}_{-}(b),\mathfrak{w}(\mathrm{ev}_{-}(v))),\quad\mathfrak{a}_{+}^{m}(\tilde{v}):=(\mathrm{ev}_{+}^{m}(b),\mathfrak{w}(\mathrm{ev}_{+}^{m}(v)))\;\text{ for }m\in\{1,2\}.

Definition 3.21
.

The moduli space

\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{N},\mathbf{k}}(\mathbf{A};H,J_{Y})=\big\{\mathbf{v}=(\tilde{v}^{2H}_{1},\dots,\tilde{v}^{2H}_{N_{1}},\tilde{v},\tilde{v}^{H}_{1},\dots,\tilde{v}^{H}_{N_{2}+N_{3}})\big\}

consists of tuples of curves satisfying the following conditions. For every
i=1,\dots,N_{1}
and
j=1,\dots,N_{2}+N_{3}
,

•

\tilde{v}^{2H}_{i}=(b^{2H}_{i},v^{2H}_{i})
is a Floer strip with respect to
(2H,J_{Y})
satisfying
[\pi\circ v^{2H}_{i}]=A_{i}

•

\tilde{v}=(b,v)
is a Floer triangle with respect to
(H,J_{Y})
satisfying
[\pi\circ v]=A
,

•

\tilde{v}^{H}_{j}=(b^{H}_{j},v^{H}_{j})
is a Floer strip with respect to
(H,J_{Y})
satisfying
[\pi\circ v^{H}_{j}]=A_{N_{1}+j}
,

and for every
i=1,\dots,N_{1}-1
,
j_{1}=1,\dots,N_{2}-1
, and
j_{2}=N_{2}+1,\dots,N_{2}+N_{3}-1
,

\begin{split}&(r^{2H}_{k^{\prime\prime}},k^{\prime\prime})=\mathfrak{a}_{-}(\tilde{v}^{2H}_{1}),\quad\mathfrak{a}_{+}(\tilde{v}^{2H}_{i})=\mathfrak{a}_{-}(\tilde{v}^{2H}_{i+1}),\quad\mathfrak{a}_{+}(\tilde{v}^{2H}_{N_{1}})=\mathfrak{a}_{-}(\tilde{v}),\\[2.15277pt]
&\mathfrak{a}^{1}_{+}(\tilde{v})=\mathfrak{a}_{-}(\tilde{v}^{H}_{1}),\quad\mathfrak{a}_{+}(\tilde{v}^{H}_{j_{1}})=\mathfrak{a}_{-}(\tilde{v}^{H}_{{j_{1}}+1}),\quad\mathfrak{a}_{+}(\tilde{v}^{H}_{N_{2}})=(r^{H}_{k},k),\\[2.15277pt]
&\mathfrak{a}^{2}_{+}(\tilde{v})=\mathfrak{a}_{-}(\tilde{v}^{H}_{N_{2}+1}),\quad\mathfrak{a}_{+}(\tilde{v}^{H}_{j_{2}})=\mathfrak{a}_{-}(\tilde{v}^{H}_{j_{2}+1}),\quad\mathfrak{a}_{+}(\tilde{v}^{H}_{N_{2}+N_{3}})=(r^{H}_{k^{\prime}},k^{\prime}).\end{split}

We also define the subspace

\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\mathbf{N},\mathbf{k}}(\mathbf{A};H,J_{Y})\subset\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{N},\mathbf{k}}(\mathbf{A};H,J_{Y})

consisting of simple elements
\mathbf{v}
, that is

\left(\pi\circ{v}^{2H}_{1},\dots,\pi\circ{v}^{2H}_{N_{1}},\pi\circ{v},\pi\circ{v}^{H}_{1},\dots,\pi\circ{v}^{H}_{N_{2}+N_{3}}\right)\in\mathcal{N}^{*}_{\mathbf{N}}(\mathbf{A};J_{\Sigma}).

We consider the evaluation map

\displaystyle\mathrm{ev}:\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{N},\mathbf{k}}(\mathbf{A};H,J_{Y})\to\mathcal{L}^{2N+3}

\displaystyle\mathrm{ev}(\mathbf{v}):=\big(\mathrm{ev}_{-,0}(\tilde{v}^{2H}_{1}),\mathrm{ev}_{+,0}(\tilde{v}^{2H}_{1}),\dots,\mathrm{ev}_{-,0}(\tilde{v}^{2H}_{N_{1}}),\mathrm{ev}_{+,0}(\tilde{v}^{2H}_{N_{1}}),\mathrm{ev}_{-,0}(\tilde{v}),

\displaystyle\hskip 54.06006pt\mathrm{ev}^{1}_{+,0}(\tilde{v}),\mathrm{ev}_{-,0}(\tilde{v}^{H}_{1}),\mathrm{ev}_{+,0}(\tilde{v}^{H}_{1}),\dots,\mathrm{ev}_{-,0}(\tilde{v}^{H}_{N_{2}}),\mathrm{ev}_{+,0}(\tilde{v}^{H}_{N_{2}}),

\displaystyle\hskip 54.06006pt\mathrm{ev}^{2}_{+,0}(\tilde{v}),\mathrm{ev}_{-,0}(\tilde{v}^{H}_{N_{2}+1}),\mathrm{ev}_{+,0}(\tilde{v}^{H}_{N_{2}+1}),\dots,\mathrm{ev}_{-,0}(\tilde{v}^{H}_{N_{2}+N_{3}}),\mathrm{ev}_{+,0}(\tilde{v}^{H}_{N_{2}+N_{3}})\big),

where
\mathrm{ev}_{-,0}(\tilde{v}):=\mathrm{ev}_{-}(v)(0)
and
\mathrm{ev}^{m}_{+,0}(\tilde{v}):=\mathrm{ev}^{m}_{+}(v)(0)
for
m\in\{1,2\}
.

Let
(f_{L},Z^{i}_{L})
for
i=1,2,3
be Morse-Smale pairs on
L
as in Section
2.2
. Let
(f_{\mathcal{L}},Z^{i}_{\mathcal{L}})
for
i=1,2,3
be the lifted Morse-Smale pairs on
\mathcal{L}
. We denote
\mathcal{D}^{P}_{H}:=(f_{\mathcal{L}},Z^{1}_{\mathcal{L}},Z^{2}_{\mathcal{L}},Z^{3}_{\mathcal{L}},H,J_{Y})
.

Definition 3.22
.

For
(\tilde{p},k),(\tilde{q},k^{\prime}),(\tilde{r},k^{\prime\prime})\in\mathrm{Crit}f_{\mathcal{L}}\times m_{L}\mathbb{Z}
, we define the moduli space

\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{N}}(\tilde{r}_{k^{\prime\prime}},\tilde{p}_{k},\tilde{q}_{k^{\prime}};\mathbf{A};\mathcal{D}^{P}_{H}):=\mathrm{ev}^{-1}\Big(W^{u}_{Z^{1}_{\mathcal{L}}}(\tilde{r})\times\Delta_{Z^{1}_{\mathcal{L}}}^{N_{1}}\times\Delta_{Z^{2}_{\mathcal{L}}}^{N_{2}}\times W^{s}_{Z^{2}_{\mathcal{L}}}(\tilde{p})\times\Delta_{Z^{3}_{\mathcal{L}}}^{N_{3}}\times W^{s}_{Z^{3}_{\mathcal{L}}}(\tilde{q})\Big),

where

\Delta_{Z^{i}_{\mathcal{L}}}:=\{(x,\varphi^{t}_{Z^{i}_{\mathcal{L}}}(x))\in\mathcal{L}\times\mathcal{L}\;\mid\;x\in(\mathcal{L}\setminus\mathrm{Crit}f_{\mathcal{L}}),\;t\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\}\quad\text{for }i=1,2,3,

see Figure
2
.
The subspace of simple elements is defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\mathbf{N}}(\tilde{r}_{k^{\prime\prime}},\tilde{p}_{k},\tilde{q}_{k^{\prime}};\mathbf{A};\mathcal{D}^{P}_{H}):=\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{N}}(\tilde{r}_{k^{\prime\prime}},\tilde{p}_{k},\tilde{q}_{k^{\prime}};\mathbf{A};\mathcal{D}^{P}_{H})\,\cap\,\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\mathbf{N},\mathbf{k}}(\mathbf{A};H,J_{Y}).

Figure 2
:
An element of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{N}}(\tilde{r}_{k^{\prime\prime}},\tilde{p}_{k},\tilde{q}_{k^{\prime}};\mathbf{A};\mathcal{D}^{P}_{H})

There is a canonical projection map

\Pi:\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{N}}(\tilde{r}_{k^{\prime\prime}},\tilde{p}_{k},\tilde{q}_{k^{\prime}};\mathbf{A};\mathcal{D}^{P}_{H})\longrightarrow\mathcal{N}_{\mathbf{N}}(r,p,q;\mathbf{A};\mathcal{D}^{P}),

(3.37)

defined by
\Pi(\mathbf{v}):=\mathbf{w}
, where
\mathbf{w}:=(\pi\circ{v}^{2H}_{1},\dots,\pi\circ{v}^{H}_{N_{2}+N_{3}})
.
We have the same projection map with
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{N}}
and
\mathcal{N}_{\mathbf{N}}
replaced by
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{N}}^{*}
and
\mathcal{N}_{\mathbf{N}}^{*}
, respectively.

Proposition 3.23
.

Let
\mathcal{J}^{\mathrm{reg},P}_{(Y,\mathcal{L})}
be the subset of
\mathcal{J}_{Y}
consisting of
J_{Y}
whose horizontal part
J_{\Sigma}
is an element of
\mathcal{J}^{\mathrm{reg},P}_{(\Sigma,L)}
given in Proposition
2.8
.
Let
J_{Y}\in\mathcal{J}^{\mathrm{reg},P}_{(Y,\mathcal{L})}
, and let
(Z^{1}_{\mathcal{L}},Z^{2}_{\mathcal{L}},Z^{3}_{\mathcal{L}})
be generic. Then, for every
(\tilde{p},k),(\tilde{q},k^{\prime}),(\tilde{r},k^{\prime\prime})\in\mathrm{Crit}f_{\mathcal{L}}\times m_{L}\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathbf{N}
, and
\mathbf{A}
, the moduli space
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\mathbf{N}}(\tilde{r}_{k^{\prime\prime}},\tilde{p}_{k},\tilde{q}_{k^{\prime}};\mathbf{A};\mathcal{D}^{P}_{H})
is a smooth manifold of dimension

\mu(\tilde{p}_{k})+\mu(\tilde{q}_{k^{\prime}})-\mu(\tilde{r}_{k^{\prime\prime}})+N-\operatorname{dim}\mathcal{L}
.

Proof.

The proof proceeds along similar lines to that of Proposition
3.11
. As in that proof, the linearized operator for the Floer triangle
\tilde{v}=(b,v)
decomposes into

D_{\tilde{v}}=\begin{pmatrix}D_{\tilde{v}}^{\mathrm{v}}&L_{\mathrm{cpt}}\\[2.15277pt]
0&D_{\tilde{v}}^{\mathrm{h}}\end{pmatrix}.

The horizontal part
D_{\tilde{v}}^{\mathrm{h}}
is surjective due to our choice of
J_{Y}
. The vertical part has the form

\begin{split}&D_{\tilde{v}}^{\mathrm{v}}:W^{1,p,\delta}((S^{P},\partial S^{P}),(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{R}))\longrightarrow L^{p,\delta}(S^{P},\mathbb{C}),\\
&D_{\tilde{v}}^{\mathrm{v}}(\sigma)=\partial_{s}\sigma+J_{0}\partial_{t}\sigma+\left(\begin{matrix}h^{{}^{\prime\prime}}(e^{b})e^{b}&0\\
0&0\end{matrix}\right)\sigma,\end{split}

with the asymptotic operators
A_{b_{i}}:=-J_{0}\frac{d}{dt}-\begin{pmatrix}h^{{}^{\prime\prime}}(e^{b_{i}})e^{b_{i}}&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}
for
b_{1}=\mathrm{ev}_{+}^{1}(b)
,
b_{2}=\mathrm{ev}_{+}^{2}(b)
, and
b_{3}=\mathrm{ev}_{-}(b)
. Since
h^{\prime\prime}(e^{b_{i}})>0
for
i=1,2,3
, Lemma
4.5
and (
3.18
) imply that
\mu_{\mathrm{RS}}(A_{b_{i}})=\frac{1}{2}
for
i=1,2,3
, and thus

\operatorname{ind}D_{\tilde{v}}^{\mathrm{v}}=\chi(S^{P})-\frac{3}{2}+\mu_{\mathrm{RS}}(A_{b_{1}})+\mu_{\mathrm{RS}}(A_{b_{2}})-\mu_{\mathrm{RS}}(A_{b_{3}})=0.\\

Assume that there exists a nonzero element
\zeta\in\operatorname{ker}D^{\mathrm{v}}_{\tilde{v}}
.
Then, the asymptotic relative winding numbers
\mathrm{wind}_{+\infty,m}(\zeta)
for
m=1,2
and
\mathrm{wind}_{-\infty}(\zeta)
satisfy

\mathrm{wind}_{+\infty,m}(\zeta)\leq\alpha_{-}(A_{b_{m}})=0,\qquad\mathrm{wind}_{-\infty}(\zeta)\geq\alpha_{+}(A_{b_{3}})=\frac{1}{2},

as in (
3.19
).
Hence, the algebraic count of zeros of
\zeta
satisfies

\mathrm{wind}_{+\infty,1}(\zeta)+\mathrm{wind}_{+\infty,2}(\zeta)-\mathrm{wind}_{-\infty}(\zeta)<0,

which contradicts the positivity of zeros of
\zeta
.
Therefore,
D^{\mathrm{v}}_{\tilde{v}}
is injective. Since
\operatorname{ind}D^{\mathrm{v}}_{\tilde{v}}=0
, it follows that
D^{\mathrm{v}}_{\tilde{v}}
and hence
D_{\tilde{v}}
are surjective.

The dimension of
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\mathbf{N}}(\tilde{r}_{k^{\prime\prime}},\tilde{p}_{k},\tilde{q}_{k^{\prime}};\mathbf{A};\mathcal{D}^{P}_{H})
is computed in the same way as in the proof of Proposition
3.11
.
This completes the proof of Proposition
3.23
.
∎

Let
J_{Y}\in\mathcal{J}_{(Y,\mathcal{L})}^{\mathrm{reg}}\cap\mathcal{J}_{(Y,\mathcal{L})}^{\mathrm{reg},P}
.
We denote

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{N}}(\tilde{r}_{k^{\prime\prime}},\tilde{p}_{k},\tilde{q}_{k^{\prime}};\mathcal{D}^{P}_{H}):=\bigcup_{\mathbf{A}}\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{N}}(\tilde{r}_{k^{\prime\prime}},\tilde{p}_{k},\tilde{q}_{k^{\prime}};\mathbf{A};\mathcal{D}^{P}_{H}).

There is a free
\mathbb{R}^{N}
-action on
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{N}}(\tilde{r}_{k^{\prime\prime}},\tilde{p}_{k},\tilde{q}_{k^{\prime}};\mathcal{D}^{P}_{H})
given by translating the domain
\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]
of each
\tilde{v}^{2H}_{i}
and
\tilde{v}^{H}_{j}
in the
\mathbb{R}
-direction.
We denote

\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathcal{M}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu(\tilde{r}_{k^{\prime\prime}},\tilde{p}_{k},\tilde{q}_{k^{\prime}};\mathcal{D}^{P}_{H}):=\bigcup_{\mathbf{N}}\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{N}}(\tilde{r}_{k^{\prime\prime}},\tilde{p}_{k},\tilde{q}_{k^{\prime}};\mathcal{D}^{P}_{H})/\mathbb{R}^{N},\\

As usual, we write
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathcal{M}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{*}
to denote the subspace consisting of simple elements.

Proposition 3.24
.

If
\mu(\tilde{p}_{k})+\mu(\tilde{q}_{k^{\prime}})-\mu(\tilde{r}_{k^{\prime\prime}})-\operatorname{dim}\mathcal{L}\leq 1
, the following hold.

(a)

Assume
N_{L}\geq 2
. All elements of
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathcal{M}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu(\tilde{r}_{k^{\prime\prime}},\tilde{p}_{k},\tilde{q}_{k^{\prime}};\mathcal{D}^{P}_{H})
are simple.

(b)

Assume
\rm(C^{P})
, i.e.,
N_{L}>\max\{\frac{1}{2}(\operatorname{dim}L+1),2\}
. Then there exist real numbers
r_{-}<r_{+}
such that the image of every element of this moduli space lies in
(r_{-},r_{+})\times Y
. Consequently, the moduli space is compact up to breaking.

Proof.

The statement (a) follows directly from
[
BC07
, Lemma 5.2.2]
.

For (b), we proceed along similar lines as in the proof of Proposition
3.12
.
Assume that there exists a sequence
\{\mathbf{v}_{\nu}\}_{\nu\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathcal{M}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu(\tilde{r}_{k^{\prime\prime}},\tilde{p}_{k},\tilde{q}_{k^{\prime}};\mathcal{D}^{P}_{H})
such that Floer strips or Floer triangles in
\mathbf{v}_{\nu}
are not contained in a bounded subset of
\mathbb{R}\times Y
. We analyze the top component
\mathbf{v}_{\infty}
of the limit of
\mathbf{v}_{\nu}
. There are two possible scenarios. First,
\mathbf{v}_{\infty}
may develop a puncture. However, this is ruled out by the assumption
N_{L}>2
as in the proof of Proposition
3.12
.

For simplicity, we describe the alternative scenario in the case of
\mathbf{N}=0
. In this situation,
\mathbf{v}_{\infty}=v_{\infty}
consists of two connected curves
{v}_{\infty,\mathrm{I}}
and
{v}_{\infty,\mathrm{II}}
. Here

{v}_{\infty,\mathrm{I}}
is a Floer-holomorphic strip with a
Z_{\mathcal{L}}
-trajectory from a Reeb chord to
\tilde{q}_{k^{\prime}}
, while
{v}_{\infty,\mathrm{II}}
is a punctured Floer strip with
Z_{\mathcal{L}}
-trajectories from
\tilde{r}_{k^{\prime\prime}}
to
\tilde{p}_{k}
. In the next level of the limit, there appears a U-shaped
J_{Y}
-holomorphic strip connecting the asymptotic Reeb chord
c_{1}
of
{v}_{\infty,\mathrm{I}}
and the asymptotic Reeb chord
c_{2}
at the puncture of
{v}_{\infty,\mathrm{II}}
; see Figure
3
. In both
{v}_{\infty,\mathrm{I}}
and
{v}_{\infty,\mathrm{II}}
, there is no additional puncture by the assumption
N_{L}>2
.

Figure 3
:
The limit configuration of
\mathbf{v}_{\nu}
when
\mathbf{N}=0

We first assume that
\operatorname{dim}L\geq 3
.
Following the notation used in the proof of Proposition
3.12
, let
\mathcal{N}_{1}(\emptyset,q;{A}_{\mathrm{I}};\mathcal{D})
be the moduli space of chains of pearls positively asymptotic to
q
, with no asymptotic condition at the negative end, containing
\Pi({v}_{\infty,\mathrm{I}})
. Similarly, let
\mathcal{N}_{1}(r,p;{A}_{\mathrm{II}};\mathcal{D})
be moduli space containing
\Pi({v}_{\infty,\mathrm{II}})
. As observed in (
3.32
), the asymptotic Reeb chords
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
satisfy

\frac{2\tau_{\Sigma}}{d}(k+k^{\prime}-k^{\prime\prime})=\mu_{L}({A}_{\mathrm{I}})+\mu_{L}({A}_{\mathrm{II}})+\mu_{\mathrm{RS}}(c_{1})+\mu_{\mathrm{RS}}(c_{2}).

(3.38)

Since
\operatorname{dim}L\geq 3
, we may take the underlying simple chains of pearls of
\Pi({v}_{\infty,\mathrm{I}})
and
\Pi({v}_{\infty,\mathrm{II}})
. We denote by
\mathcal{N}^{*}_{1}(\emptyset,q;{A}_{\mathrm{I}}^{*};\mathcal{D})
and
\mathcal{N}^{*}_{1}(r,p;{A}_{\mathrm{II}}^{*};\mathcal{D})
the moduli spaces containing these underlying simple chains of pearls, respectively.
They have

\begin{split}&\operatorname{dim}\mathcal{N}^{*}_{1}(\emptyset,q;{A}_{\mathrm{I}}^{*};\mathcal{D})=\operatorname{dim}L-\operatorname{ind}_{f_{L}}(q)+\mu_{L}({A}_{\mathrm{I}}^{*})\geq 0,\\[2.15277pt]
&\operatorname{dim}\mathcal{N}^{*}_{1}(r,p;{A}_{\mathrm{II}}^{*};\mathcal{D})=\operatorname{ind}_{f_{L}}(r)-\operatorname{ind}_{f_{L}}(p)+\mu_{L}({A}_{\mathrm{II}}^{*})\geq 0.\end{split}

(3.39)

Using the index condition on
\tilde{p}_{k}
,
\tilde{q}_{k^{\prime}}
, and
\tilde{r}_{k^{\prime\prime}}
, we obtain

\begin{split}1&\geq\mu(\tilde{p}_{k})+\mu(\tilde{q}_{k^{\prime}})-\mu(\tilde{r}_{k^{\prime\prime}})-\operatorname{dim}\mathcal{L}\\[2.15277pt]
&=\operatorname{ind}_{f_{L}}(r)-\operatorname{ind}_{f_{L}}(p)-\operatorname{ind}_{f_{L}}(q)+\mu_{L}({A}_{\mathrm{I}})+\mu_{L}({A}_{\mathrm{II}})+\mu_{\mathrm{RS}}(c_{1})+\mu_{\mathrm{RS}}(c_{2})\\[2.15277pt]
&\geq-\operatorname{dim}L+\mu_{\mathrm{RS}}(c_{1})+\mu_{\mathrm{RS}}(c_{2}).\end{split}

The second line follows from (
3.11
) and (
3.38
). The last line follows from
\mu_{L}({A}_{\mathrm{I}})\geq\mu_{L}({A}_{\mathrm{I}}^{*})
,
\mu_{L}({A}_{\mathrm{II}})\geq\mu_{L}({A}_{\mathrm{II}}^{*})
, and (
3.39
). Both
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
are trivial in
\pi_{1}(Y,\mathcal{L})
as
\tilde{p}_{k}
,
\tilde{q}_{k^{\prime}}
, and
\tilde{r}_{k^{\prime\prime}}
are so. Thus
\mu_{\mathrm{RS}}(c_{1})
and
\mu_{\mathrm{RS}}(c_{2})
are at least
N_{L}
. By our assumption
N_{L}>\frac{1}{2}(\operatorname{dim}L+1)
, we arrive at a contradiction. In the case of
\mathbf{N}\neq 0
, an analogous argument, incorporating translations of the additional disks, yields a contradiction.

It remains to consider the case
\operatorname{dim}L\leq 2
. As before, we have

\begin{split}1&\geq\mu(\tilde{p}_{k})+\mu(\tilde{q}_{k^{\prime}})-\mu(\tilde{r}_{k^{\prime\prime}})-\operatorname{dim}\mathcal{L}\\[2.15277pt]
&=\operatorname{ind}_{f_{L}}(r)-\operatorname{ind}_{f_{L}}(p)-\operatorname{ind}_{f_{L}}(q)+\mu_{L}({A}_{\mathrm{I}})+\mu_{L}({A}_{\mathrm{II}})+\mu_{\mathrm{RS}}(c_{1})+\mu_{\mathrm{RS}}(c_{2})\\[2.15277pt]
&\geq 0-2-2+0+0+3+3=2,\end{split}

which is a contradiction.
This completes the proof of Proposition
3.24
.(b).
∎

Each
\mathbf{v}\in\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathcal{M}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{*}(\tilde{r}_{k^{\prime\prime}},\tilde{p}_{k},\tilde{q}_{k^{\prime}};\mathcal{D}^{P}_{H})
with
\mu(\tilde{p}_{k})+\mu(\tilde{q}_{k^{\prime}})-\mu(\tilde{r}_{k^{\prime\prime}})=\operatorname{dim}\mathcal{L}
induces an isomorphism

C([\mathbf{v}]):\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{p}_{k})\otimes\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{q}_{k^{\prime}})\longrightarrow\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{r}_{k^{\prime\prime}}).

The chain-level triangle product is defined by
