\begin{split}&\star_{H}:=\bigoplus_{(\tilde{p},\tilde{q},\tilde{r},k,k^{\prime},k^{\prime\prime})}\sum_{[\mathbf{v}]}C([\mathbf{v}]):\mathrm{FC}^{(a,b)}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}};\mathcal{D}_{H})\otimes\mathrm{FC}^{(a,b)}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}};\mathcal{D}_{H})\to\mathrm{FC}^{(a+b,2b)}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}};\mathcal{D}_{2H})\end{split}

The direct sum runs over
(\tilde{p},k),(\tilde{q},k^{\prime})\in\mathrm{Crit}f_{\mathcal{L}}\times\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(a,b)}(H)
and
(\tilde{r},k^{\prime\prime})\in\mathrm{Crit}f_{\mathcal{L}}\times\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(a+b,2b)}(2H)
satisfying
\mu(\tilde{p}_{k})+\mu(\tilde{q}_{k^{\prime}})-\mu(\tilde{r}_{k^{\prime\prime}})=\operatorname{dim}\mathcal{L}
.
The sum runs over
[\mathbf{v}]\in\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathcal{M}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{*}(\tilde{r}_{k^{\prime\prime}},\tilde{p}_{k},\tilde{q}_{k^{\prime}};\mathcal{D}^{P}_{H})
.
It is a chain map by Proposition
3.24
and induces

\mathrm{FH}^{(a,b)}_{l}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}};\mathcal{D}_{H})\otimes\mathrm{FH}^{(a,b)}_{m}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}};\mathcal{D}_{H})\longrightarrow\mathrm{FH}^{(a+b,2b)}_{l+m-\operatorname{dim}\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}};\mathcal{D}_{2H}).

By taking limits
\varinjlim\limits_{b\uparrow+\infty}\varprojlim\limits_{a\downarrow-\infty}\varinjlim\limits_{H\in\mathcal{H}}
, we define the triangle product

\star:\mathrm{RFH}_{l}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}})\otimes\mathrm{RFH}_{m}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}})\longrightarrow\mathrm{RFH}_{l+m-\operatorname{dim}\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}}).

Finally, we remark that the
\mathbb{Z}[T,T^{-1}]
-module structure in Remark
3.19
is compatible with this product, and therefore
\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}})
is an algebra over
\mathbb{Z}[T,T^{-1}]
.

4
Proof of the main results

In this section, we prove Theorem
1.1
by establishing a correspondence between the moduli spaces used to define
\mathrm{QH}(L^{\sigma})
and those used in the definition of
\mathrm{RFH}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}})
.
We first show that a
J_{\Sigma}
-holomorphic disk in
(\Sigma,L)
lifts to a
J_{Y}
-holomorphic strip in
(\mathbb{R}\times Y,\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{L})
. See
[
DR16
,
KPS24
]
for related works.
We then establish a bijection between
J_{Y}
-holomorphic strips and Floer strips in
(\mathbb{R}\times Y,\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{L})
.

4.1
Lifting of holomorphic disks

We fix
J_{Y}\in\mathcal{J}_{Y}
and denote its horizontal part by
J_{\Sigma}
. We denote

\mathbb{R}_{0}:=\{x+iy\in\partial\mathbb{D}\mid y<0\},\qquad\mathbb{R}_{1}:=\{x+iy\in\partial\mathbb{D}\mid y>0\}.

We tacitly use a biholomorphism
(\mathbb{D}\setminus\{\pm 1\},\mathbb{R}_{0},\mathbb{R}_{1})\cong(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1],\mathbb{R}\times\{0\},\mathbb{R}\times\{1\})
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
w:(\mathbb{D},\partial\mathbb{D})\to(\Sigma,L)
be a
J_{\Sigma}
-holomorphic disk with
q:=w(-1)
and
p:=w(1)
. Let
c_{q}:([0,1],\{0,1\})\to(Y_{q},\mathcal{L}_{q})
be any generalized Reeb chord. Then, there exists a
J_{Y}
-holomorphic strip
\tilde{u}=(a,u):\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]\to(\mathbb{R}\times Y,\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{L})
such that
\pi\circ u=w
and

\lim_{s\to-\infty}u(s,t)=c_{q}(t),\qquad\lim_{s\to\infty}u(s,t)=c_{p}(t),

where
c_{p}:([0,1],\{0,1\})\to(Y_{p},\mathcal{L}_{p})
is a generalized Reeb chord with
\mathfrak{w}(c_{p})-\mathfrak{w}(c_{q})=d\cdot\omega([w])
.
Moreover, if there is another such strip
\tilde{u}^{\prime}=(a^{\prime},u^{\prime})
, then
u=u^{\prime}
and
a-a^{\prime}
is constant.

Proof.

The proof is inspired by the argument in
[
BK13
, Lemma 7.1]
.
We consider the bundle
\pi_{\mathbb{R}\times Y}:\mathbb{R}\times Y\to\Sigma
with fibers
\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\cong\mathbb{C}^{*}:=\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
. We
choose a unitary trivialization
\Phi:(w^{*}(\mathbb{R}\times Y),w^{*}J_{Y})\to(\mathbb{D}\times\mathbb{C}^{*},J_{0})
of the pullback bundle by
w
and denote

\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{R}_{i}}:=\Phi(w|_{\mathbb{R}_{i}}^{*}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{L}))\subset\mathbb{R}_{i}\times\mathbb{C}^{*},\quad i=0,1.

Note that there are smooth functions
f:\mathbb{R}_{0}\to\mathbb{R}
and
g:\mathbb{R}_{1}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{R}_{0}}=\{(\xi,re^{i(f(\xi)+\frac{2\pi\ell}{d})})\in\mathbb{R}_{0}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}\mid r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0},\,\ell=1,\dots,d\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{R}_{1}}=\{(\xi,re^{i(g(\xi)+\frac{2\pi\ell}{d})})\in\mathbb{R}_{1}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}\mid r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0},\,\ell=1,\dots,d\},

and

\lim_{\sigma\to-\infty}(f-g)(\sigma)=0,\qquad\lim_{\sigma\to\infty}(f-g)(\sigma)\in\frac{1}{d}\mathbb{Z}.

Viewing
f
and
g+\mathfrak{w}(c_{q})
together as a function on
\partial\mathbb{D}
of class
L^{1}
, we know that there is a harmonic function
h:\mathbb{D}\setminus\partial\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{R}
whose radial limit agrees with
f
on
\mathbb{R}_{0}
and with
g+\mathfrak{w}(c_{q})
on
\mathbb{R}_{1}
. Let
\bar{h}
be a harmonic conjugate of
h
. We define a holomorphic function

\mu:=e^{\bar{h}+ih}:\mathbb{D}\setminus\{\pm 1\}\cong\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{*}.

Through the trivialization
\Phi
, the pair
(w|_{\mathbb{D}\setminus\{\pm 1\}},\mu)
gives rise to a
J_{Y}
-holomorphic lift
\tilde{u}
of
w|_{\mathbb{D}\setminus\{\pm 1\}}
such that the negative asymptotic orbit is a generalized Reeb chord over
q
with relative winding number
\mathfrak{w}(c_{q})
. After multiplying
\mu
by a suitable factor
e^{\frac{2\pi i\ell}{d}}
with
1\leq\ell\leq d
, we can arrange that the negative asymptotic orbit of
\tilde{u}
coincides with
c_{q}
. The positive asymptotic orbit of
\tilde{u}
is a generalized Reeb chord
c_{p}
over
p
, and the identity

\omega([w])=\int_{\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]}\tilde{u}^{*}d\alpha=\frac{1}{d}(\mathfrak{w}(c_{p})-\mathfrak{w}(c_{q}))

follows from
\pi^{*}\omega=d\alpha
and Stokes’ theorem.
The described correspondence between
\tilde{u}
and
\mu
is bijective up to the addition of a constant to
\bar{h}
. This finishes the proof.
∎

Remark 4.2
.

In the previous lemma, if
\mathfrak{w}(c_{q})=0
or
\mathfrak{w}(c_{p})=0
, then
\displaystyle\lim_{s\to-\infty}a
or
\displaystyle\lim_{s\to\infty}a
is finite, respectively.
Otherwise, we have

\mathfrak{w}(c_{q})\cdot\big(\lim_{s\to-\infty}a\big)=-\infty,\qquad\mathfrak{w}(c_{p})\cdot\big(\lim_{s\to\infty}a\big)=+\infty.

In fact, Lemma
4.1
extends to
J_{\Sigma}
-holomorphic disks with an arbitrary number of boundary punctures. The following lemma addresses the case with three punctures. Its proof is a straightforward modification of that of Lemma
4.1
.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
w:(\mathbb{D},\partial\mathbb{D})\to(\Sigma,L)
be a
J_{\Sigma}
-holomorphic disk with
p:=w(e^{\frac{-\pi i}{3}})
,
q:=w(e^{\frac{\pi i}{3}})
, and
r:=w(-1)
.
Let
c_{p}:([0,1],\{0,1\})\to(Y_{p},\mathcal{L}_{p})
and
c_{q}:([0,1],\{0,1\})\to(Y_{q},\mathcal{L}_{q})
be any generalized Reeb chords.
Then, there exists a
J_{Y}
-holomorphic triangle
\tilde{u}=(a,u):(S^{P},\partial S^{P})\to(\mathbb{R}\times Y,\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{L})
, together with an integer
\ell\in\{1,\dots,d\}
, such that
\pi\circ u=w
and

\lim_{s\to\infty}u(s,t-1)=c_{p}(t),\quad\lim_{s\to\infty}u(s,t)=\phi_{R}^{\frac{\ell}{d}}(c_{q}(t)),\quad\lim_{s\to-\infty}u(s,2t-1)=c_{r}(t),

where
c_{r}:([0,1],\{0,1\})\to(Y_{r},\mathcal{L}_{r})
is a generalized Reeb chord with
\mathfrak{w}(c_{p})+\mathfrak{w}(c_{q})-\mathfrak{w}(c_{r})=d\cdot\omega([w])
.
Moreover, if there exists another such triangle
\tilde{u}^{\prime}=(a^{\prime},u^{\prime})
, together with an integer
\ell^{\prime}
, satisfying the same properties, then
u=u^{\prime}
,
\ell=\ell^{\prime}
, and
a-a^{\prime}
is constant.

4.2
Correspondence between Floer and
J_{Y}
-holomorphic strips

In this section, we establish a correspondence between Floer strips and
J_{Y}
-holomorphic strips by adapting the proof of
[
DL19b
, Proposition 6.1]
, which treats the periodic case. Let
(H,J_{Y})\in\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{J}_{Y}
with
H(r,y)=h(e^{r})
.

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
\tilde{u}=(a,u):(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1],\mathbb{R}\times\{0,1\})\to(\mathbb{R}\times Y,\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{L})
be a
J_{Y}
-holomorphic strip. Assume that
\mathrm{ev}_{\pm}(u)
exist and there are
r_{\pm}\in(-\eta,\eta)
such that
d\cdot h^{\prime}(e^{r_{\pm}})=\mathfrak{w}(\mathrm{ev}_{\pm}(u))
, i.e., there are chords of
X_{H}
in
(-\eta,\eta)\times Y
corresponding to
\mathrm{ev}_{\pm}(u)
.
Then, there exists a unique pair of a Floer strip
\tilde{v}=(b,v):(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1],\mathbb{R}\times\{0,1\})\to(\mathbb{R}\times Y,\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{L})
and a smooth map
\mathbf{e}=(e_{1},e_{2}):(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1],\mathbb{R}\times\{0,1\})\to(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{R})
satisfying

de_{1}-de_{2}\circ i+h^{\prime}(e^{a+e_{1}})ds=0,

(4.1)

and

\mathrm{ev}_{\pm}(\tilde{v})=(r_{\pm},\mathrm{ev}_{\pm}(u)),\qquad(a,u)=(b-e_{1},\phi_{R}^{-e_{2}}\circ v).

Proof.

We take a smooth function
\nu:\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying
\partial_{t}\nu=0
and

\nu(s,t)=\begin{cases}h^{\prime}(e^{r_{+}})s,&$s>1$,\\[2.15277pt]
h^{\prime}(e^{r_{-}})s,&$s<-1$.\end{cases}

We consider the following family of equations parametrized by
\tau\in[0,1]
,

d(a+e_{1})-de_{2}\circ i+h^{\prime}(e^{a+e_{1}})ds-\tau da-(1-\tau)d\nu=0,

(4.2)

which interpolates (
4.1
) and

d(a+e_{1})-de_{2}\circ i+h^{\prime}(e^{a+e_{1}})ds-d\nu=0.

(4.3)

Substituting
(f_{1},f_{2}):=(a+e_{1},e_{2})
, we are interested in the zero set of

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}_{\tau}:\mathcal{Y}&\longrightarrow L^{p}(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1],\mathbb{C})\\[2.15277pt]
(f_{1},f_{2})&\longmapsto df_{1}-df_{2}\circ i+h^{\prime}(e^{f_{1}})ds-\tau da-(1-\tau)d\nu,\end{split}

where
\mathcal{Y}
consists of
(f_{1},f_{2})
satisfying
(f_{1}-\partial_{s}\nu,f_{2})\in W^{1,p}((\mathbb{R}\times[0,1],\mathbb{R}\times\{0,1\}),(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{R}))
. Let
(f_{1},f_{2})
be a zero of
\mathcal{F}_{\tau}
. By linearizing
\mathcal{F}_{\tau}
at
(f_{1},f_{2})
, we obtain a linear operator

\displaystyle D\mathcal{F}_{\tau}:T_{(f_{1},f_{2})}\mathcal{Y}=W^{1,p}((\mathbb{R}\times[0,1],\mathbb{R}\times\{0,1\}),(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{R}))\longrightarrow L^{p}(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1],\mathbb{C}),

\displaystyle D\mathcal{F}_{\tau}(\sigma)=\partial_{s}\sigma+J_{0}\partial_{t}\sigma+\left(\begin{matrix}h^{{}^{\prime\prime}}(e^{f_{1}})e^{f_{1}}&0\\
0&0\end{matrix}\right)\sigma.

The asymptotic operators of
D\mathcal{F}_{\tau}
are given by

A_{\pm}:=-J_{0}\frac{d}{dt}-\begin{pmatrix}h^{{}^{\prime\prime}}(e^{r_{\pm}})e^{r_{\pm}}&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}:W^{1,2}(([0,1],\{0,1\}),(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{R}))\to L^{2}([0,1],\mathbb{C}).

The operator
D\mathcal{F}_{\tau}
is Fredholm, and since
h^{\prime\prime}(e^{r_{\pm}})>0
, Lemma
4.5
and (
3.18
) imply that
\operatorname{ind}D\mathcal{F}_{\tau}=\mu_{\mathrm{RS}}(A_{+})-\mu_{\mathrm{RS}}(A_{-})=0
.
The argument in Proposition
3.11
shows that
D\mathcal{F}_{\tau}
is an isomorphism for every
\tau\in[0,1]
.
This yields a bijection between solutions of (
4.1
) and those of (
4.3
).

Suppose now that
(f_{1},f_{2})=(a+e_{1},e_{2})
solves (
4.3
). Then,

\Delta f_{2}=\partial_{s}(-\partial_{t}f_{1})+\partial_{t}(\partial_{s}f_{1}+h^{\prime}(e^{f_{1}})-\partial_{s}\nu)=h^{\prime\prime}(e^{f_{1}})e^{f_{1}}\partial_{t}f_{1}=-h^{\prime\prime}(e^{f_{1}})e^{f_{1}}\partial_{s}f_{2}.

Since
f_{2}
vanishes on the boundary
\mathbb{R}\times\{0,1\}
and converges to
0
as
s\to\pm\infty
,
f_{2}
is identically zero by the maximum principle.
Thus, equation (
4.3
) reduces to the ODE:

\begin{cases}\;\partial_{s}f_{1}+h^{\prime}(e^{f_{1}})-\partial_{s}\nu=0,\\
\;\partial_{t}f_{1}=0.\end{cases}

An argument in
[
DL19b
, Lemma 6.16]
(see also
[
BKK24
, Proof of Proposition 4.7]
) shows that this equation admits a unique solution.
Therefore, (
4.1
) also has a unique solution
\mathbf{e}
. Moreover, a straightforward computation shows that
(b,v)
induced by
\tilde{u}
and
\mathbf{e}
as in the statement is indeed a solution to the Floer equation.
This completes the proof. ∎

Lemma 4.5
.

For
C\geq 0
, the spectrum
\sigma(A_{C})
of the operator

A_{C}:=-J_{0}\frac{d}{dt}-\begin{pmatrix}C&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}:W^{1,2}(([0,1],\{0,1\}),(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{R}))\to L^{2}([0,1],\mathbb{C})

is given by

\left\{\frac{-C+\sqrt{C^{2}+4\pi^{2}k^{2}}}{2}\;|\;k\in\mathbb{N}\right\}\cup\left\{\frac{-C-\sqrt{C^{2}+4\pi^{2}k^{2}}}{2}\;|\;k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}\right\}.

Moreover, eigenfunctions associated with eigenvalues
\frac{-C\pm\sqrt{C^{2}+4\pi^{2}k^{2}}}{2}
have relative winding numbers
\pm\frac{k}{2}
, respectively.
Note also that
0\notin\sigma(A_{C})
for
C>0
, while
\sigma(A_{0})=\pi\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

Let
\lambda\in\sigma(A_{C})
.
Then, there exists
v:([0,1],\{0,1\})\to(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{R})
satisfying

-\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\dot{v}-\begin{pmatrix}C&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}v=\lambda v\;\iff\;\dot{v}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-\lambda\\
C+\lambda&0\end{pmatrix}v.

(4.4)

Due to the boundary condition, the eigenvalues of the matrix
\begin{pmatrix}0&-\lambda\\
C+\lambda&0\end{pmatrix}
must be of the form
i\pi k
for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Solving the characteristic equation yields
\lambda=\frac{-C\pm\sqrt{C^{2}+4\pi^{2}k^{2}}}{2}
.
For
C>0
,
\lambda=0
is not an eigenvalue since the only solution to (
4.4
) with
\lambda=0
and the boundary condition is the zero function.
For other
\lambda=\frac{-C\pm\sqrt{C^{2}+4\pi^{2}k^{2}}}{2}
,
v(t)=e^{\pm i\pi kt}v(0)
with
v(0)\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
is an eigenfunction with relative winding number
\pm\frac{k}{2}
.
∎

The converse of Proposition
4.4
also holds. We include it here for completeness, although it will not be used.

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
\tilde{v}=(b,v):(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1],\mathbb{R}\times\{0,1\})\to(\mathbb{R}\times Y,\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{L})
be a Floer strip with
\mathrm{ev}_{\pm}(b)\subset(-\eta,\eta)
. Then, there exists a pair of a
J_{Y}
-holomorphic strip
\tilde{u}=(a,u):(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1],\mathbb{R}\times\{0,1\})\to(\mathbb{R}\times Y,\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{L})
and a smooth map
\mathbf{e}=(e_{1},e_{2}):(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1],\mathbb{R}\times\{0,1\})\to(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{R})
satisfying

de_{1}-de_{2}\circ i+h^{\prime}(e^{b})ds=0,\quad\mathrm{ev}_{\pm}(u)=\mathrm{ev}_{\pm}(v),\quad(b,v)=(a+e_{1},\phi_{R}^{e_{2}}\circ u).

Moreover, such a pair
(\tilde{u},\mathbf{e})
is determined uniquely up to the translation:

c\cdot((a,u),(e_{1},e_{2}))=((a+c,u),(e_{1}-c,e_{2}))\quad\forall c\in\mathbb{R}.

Proof.

We consider the Cauchy–Riemann operators

\begin{split}\overline{\partial}&=\partial_{s}+J_{0}\partial_{t}:W^{1,p,\delta}_{\mathbb{R}}((\mathbb{R}\times[0,1],\mathbb{R}\times\{0,1\}),(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{R}))\to L^{p,\delta}(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1],\mathbb{C}),\\[2.15277pt]
\overline{\partial}^{\prime}&=\partial_{s}+J_{0}\partial_{t}:W^{1,p,-\delta}((\mathbb{R}\times[0,1],\mathbb{R}\times\{0,1\}),(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{R}))\to L^{p,-\delta}(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1],\mathbb{C}),\end{split}

for
0<\delta<\pi
.
The notation
W^{1,p,\delta}_{\mathbb{R}}
means that it consists of sections
\sigma
of
W^{1,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}
such that
e^{\delta|s|}(\sigma(s,t)-v_{\pm})\in W^{1,p}
near the corresponding ends for some
v_{\pm}\in\mathbb{R}
, while
W^{1,p,-\delta}
means that it consists of sections
\sigma
such that
e^{-\delta|s|}\sigma(s,t)\in W^{1,p}
. These two operators have isomorphic kernels and cokernels, see
[
DL19a
, Lemma 5.20]
. The relevant asymptotic operators for
\overline{\partial}^{\prime}
are
A-\delta
at the positive end and
A+\delta
at the negative end, where
A:=-J_{0}\frac{d}{dt}
. In particular, we compute

\operatorname{ind}\overline{\partial}=\operatorname{ind}\overline{\partial}^{\prime}=\mu_{\mathrm{RS}}(A-\delta)-\mu_{\mathrm{RS}}(A+\delta)=1
.

To show the surjectivity of
\overline{\partial}^{\prime}
,
we consider the formal adjoint operator
(\overline{\partial}^{\prime})^{*}
of
\overline{\partial}^{\prime}
.
The relevant asymptotic operators for
(\overline{\partial}^{\prime})^{*}
are
A+\delta
and
A-\delta
at the positive and negative ends, respectively.
Assume that there is a nonzero element
\zeta\in\operatorname{ker}(\overline{\partial}^{\prime})^{*}
.
Then, as in (
3.19
), its asymptotic winding numbers satisfy

\mathrm{wind}_{+\infty}(\zeta)\leq\alpha_{-}(A+\delta)=0,\qquad\mathrm{wind}_{-\infty}(\zeta)\geq\alpha_{+}(A-\delta)=\frac{1}{2}.

This leads to a contradiction that the algebraic count of zeros of
\zeta
,
\mathrm{wind}_{+\infty}(\zeta)-\mathrm{wind}_{-\infty}(\zeta)
, is negative.
Therefore,
\operatorname{ker}(\overline{\partial}^{\prime})^{*}=0
.
It follows that
\overline{\partial}^{\prime}
and hence
\overline{\partial}
are surjective.
Since
\operatorname{ind}\overline{\partial}=\operatorname{ind}\overline{\partial}^{\prime}=1
,
\operatorname{ker}\overline{\partial}
is 1-dimensional and consists precisely of real-valued constant functions.

Next, let
\nu:\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
be a smooth function satisfying
\partial_{t}\nu=0
and

\nu(s,t)=\begin{cases}h^{\prime}(e^{\mathrm{ev}_{+}(b)})s,&$s>1$,\\[2.15277pt]
h^{\prime}(e^{\mathrm{ev}_{-}(b)})s,&$s<-1$.\end{cases}

By the exponential convergence of
b
, we have
\partial_{s}\nu-h^{\prime}(e^{b})\in L^{p,\delta}(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1],\mathbb{C})
for small
\delta>0
. Since
\overline{\partial}
is surjective, there exists
f_{1}+if_{2}\in W^{1,p,\delta}_{\mathbb{R}}((\mathbb{R}\times[0,1],\mathbb{R}\times\{0,1\}),(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{R}))
such that

\overline{\partial}(f_{1}+if_{2})=\partial_{s}\nu-h^{\prime}(e^{b}).

A direct computation as in
[
DL19b
, p.998]
shows that
(e_{1},e_{2}):=(f_{1}-\nu,f_{2})
satisfies the claimed properties.
This completes the proof.
∎

An analogue of Proposition
4.4
for Floer triangles also holds.

Proposition 4.7
.

Let
\tilde{u}=(a,u):(S^{P},\partial S^{P})\to(\mathbb{R}\times Y,\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{L})
be a
J_{Y}
-holomorphic triangle. Assume that
\mathrm{ev}_{-}(u)
,
\mathrm{ev}_{+}^{1}(u)
,
\mathrm{ev}_{+}^{2}(u)
exist and there are
r_{0},r_{1},r_{2}\in(-\eta,\eta)
such that
dh^{\prime}(e^{r_{0}})=\mathfrak{w}(\mathrm{ev}_{-}(u))
,
dh^{\prime}(e^{r_{1}})=\mathfrak{w}(\mathrm{ev}_{+}^{1}(u))
, and
dh^{\prime}(e^{r_{2}})=\mathfrak{w}(\mathrm{ev}_{+}^{2}(u))
, i.e., there are chords of
X_{H}
in
(-\eta,\eta)\times Y
corresponding to
\mathrm{ev}_{-}(u)
,
\mathrm{ev}_{+}^{1}(u)
,
\mathrm{ev}_{+}^{2}(u)
.
Then there exists a unique pair of a Floer triangle
\tilde{v}=(b,v):(S^{P},\partial S^{P})\to(\mathbb{R}\times Y,\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{L})
and a smooth map
\mathbf{e}=(e_{1},e_{2}):(S^{P},\partial S^{P})\to(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{R})
such that

de_{1}-de_{2}\circ i+h^{\prime}(e^{a+e_{1}})ds=0,

in the interior of
S^{P}
,
(a,u)=(b-e_{1},\phi_{R}^{-e_{2}}\circ v)
, and

\mathrm{ev}_{-}(\tilde{v})=(r_{0},\mathrm{ev}_{-}(u)),\quad\mathrm{ev}_{+}^{1}(\tilde{v})=(r_{1},\mathrm{ev}_{+}^{1}(u)),\quad\mathrm{ev}_{+}^{2}(\tilde{v})=(r_{2},\mathrm{ev}_{+}^{2}(u)).

Proof.

The proof is essentially identical to that of Proposition
4.4
. We only highlight the points where it differs. We again consider the family of equations in (
4.2
), this time with a smooth function
\nu:S^{P}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying
\partial_{t}\nu=0
and

\nu(s,t)=\begin{cases}h^{\prime}(e^{r_{0}})s,&$s<-1$ and $t\in[-1,1]$,\\[2.15277pt]
h^{\prime}(e^{r_{1}})s,&$s>1$ and $t\in[-1,0]$,\\[2.15277pt]
h^{\prime}(e^{r_{2}})s,&$s>1$ and $t\in[0,1]$.\end{cases}

We then analyze the linearized operator
D\mathcal{F}_{\tau}:W^{1,p}((S^{P},\partial{S}^{P}),(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{R}))\to L^{p}(S^{P},\mathbb{C})
,
whose asymptotic operators are

A_{\ell}:=-J_{0}\frac{d}{dt}-\begin{pmatrix}h^{{}^{\prime\prime}}(e^{r_{\ell}})e^{r_{\ell}}&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}:W^{1,2}(([0,1],\{0,1\}),(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{R}))\to L^{2}([0,1],\mathbb{C})

for
\ell=0,1,2
. By Lemma
4.5
and the standard Fredholm index formula,
D\mathcal{F}_{\tau}
is Fredholm with index

\operatorname{ind}D\mathcal{F}_{\tau}=\chi(S^{P})-\frac{3}{2}+\mu_{\mathrm{RS}}(A_{1})+\mu_{\mathrm{RS}}(A_{2})-\mu_{\mathrm{RS}}(A_{0})=0,

as
\mu_{\mathrm{RS}}(A_{\ell})=\frac{1}{2}
for all
\ell=0,1,2
.
The argument in Proposition
3.23
shows that
D\mathcal{F}_{\tau}
is an isomorphism for every
\tau\in[0,1]
. The rest is the same as the proof of Proposition
4.4
.
∎

The converse of Proposition
4.7
is also true, and its proof is parallel to that of Proposition
4.6
. We omit this, as it is not needed.

4.3
Proof of Theorem
1.1

The cyclic subgroup
\mathbb{Z}_{d}\subset S^{1}=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
acts freely on
Y
and
\mathcal{L}
. Due to our choice of
(H,J_{Y})\in\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{J}_{Y}
, both of which are invariant under the
S^{1}
-action, the chain complex
\mathrm{FC}_{*}^{(a,b)}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}};\mathcal{D}_{H})
and hence
\mathrm{RFC}_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}})
admit a free
\mathbb{Z}_{d}
-action, see (
3.10
). Thus, the
\mathbb{Z}_{d}
-equivariant complex
\mathrm{RFC}_{*}^{\mathbb{Z}_{d}}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}}):=\mathrm{RFC}_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}})/\mathbb{Z}_{d}
is well-defined.
Furthermore, the chain-level triangle product on
\mathrm{RFC}_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}})
defined in Section
3.5
descends to
\mathrm{RFC}_{*}^{\mathbb{Z}_{d}}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}})
. Thus,
\mathrm{RFH}_{*}^{\mathbb{Z}_{d}}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}})
is defined as a
\mathbb{Z}
-module provided
N_{L}>2
, and as a ring under the assumption
N_{L}>\max\{\frac{1}{2}(\operatorname{dim}L+1),2\}
.

The quotient space
Y/\mathbb{Z}_{d}
is a prequantization bundle over
\Sigma
with Euler class
-d[\omega]_{\mathbb{Z}}
, and
\mathcal{L}/\mathbb{Z}_{d}
is a Legendrian lift of
L
to
Y/\mathbb{Z}_{d}
. Note that
(Y,\mathcal{L})\to(Y/\mathbb{Z}_{d},\mathcal{L}/\mathbb{Z}_{d})
is a
d
-fold covering, and that
\mathrm{RFC}_{*}^{\mathbb{Z}_{d}}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}})
is canonically isomorphic to the ordinary Rabinowitz Floer complex
\mathrm{RFC}_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}}/\mathbb{Z}_{d})
for
\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{L}/\mathbb{Z}_{d}\subset\mathbb{R}\times Y/\mathbb{Z}_{d}
.
Hence, it suffices to assume
d=1
, namely
\pi|_{\mathcal{L}}:\mathcal{L}\to L
is a diffeomorphism, and construct the isomorphism
\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}})\cong\mathrm{QH}_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\sigma})
as stated in Theorem
1.1
.(b) and (c).
To this end, we fix a degree
j\in\mathbb{N}
and recall from Section
3.4.2
that

\mathrm{RFC}_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}})=\mathrm{FC}_{*}^{(a,b)}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}};\mathcal{D}_{H}),\qquad*\in\{j-1,j,j+1\}

for sufficiently large
H\in\mathcal{H}
and
(a,b)
. We claim that
\mathrm{FC}_{*}^{(a,b)}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}};\mathcal{D}_{H})
is isomorphic to
\mathrm{QC}_{*}(L;\mathcal{D})
in such degrees. By the commutativity of the front face of the diagram in Proposition
3.14
, we have a degree-preserving isomorphism

f_{\tilde{p}_{k}}:\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{p}_{k})\longrightarrow\mathfrak{o}(p,\tfrac{-k}{m_{L}}).

(4.5)

The definitions and gradings of
\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{p}_{k})
and
\mathfrak{o}(p,\frac{-k}{m_{L}})
are given in (
2.2
), (
3.27
), and (
3.28
).
This together with Remark
3.5
yields that the map

\Theta:=\bigoplus_{(\tilde{p},k)}f_{\tilde{p}_{k}}:\mathrm{FC}_{*}^{(a,b)}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}};\mathcal{D}_{H})\longrightarrow\mathrm{QC}_{*}(L^{\sigma};\mathcal{D})

(4.6)

for
*\in\{j-1,j,j+1\}
, where the direct sum ranges over all
(\tilde{p},k)\in\mathrm{Crit}f_{\mathcal{L}}\times\mathfrak{w}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(a,b)}(H)
with
\mu(\tilde{p}_{k})=*
, is an isomorphism between the two chain modules.

It remains to verify that
\Theta
is a chain map. Recall from (
3.29
) and (
2.6
) that the boundary maps
\partial_{\mathcal{D}_{H}}
on
\mathrm{FC}_{*}^{(a,b)}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}};\mathcal{D}_{H})
and
\partial_{\mathcal{D}}
on
\mathrm{QC}_{*}(L^{\sigma};\mathcal{D})
restricted to
\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{p}_{k})
and
\mathfrak{o}(p,\frac{-k}{m_{L}})
, respectively, are given by

\partial_{\mathcal{D}_{H}}|_{\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{p}_{k})}=\bigoplus_{(\tilde{q},{l})}\sum_{[\mathbf{v}]}C([\mathbf{v}]),\qquad\partial_{\mathcal{D}}|_{\mathfrak{o}(p,-k/m_{L})}=\bigoplus_{(q,\frac{-l}{m_{L}})}\sum_{[\mathbf{w}]}C([\mathbf{w}]).

Here, the direct sums run over all
(\tilde{q},l)\in\mathrm{Crit}f_{\mathcal{L}}\times\mathfrak{w}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(a,b)}(H)
with
\deg\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{q}_{l})=\deg\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{p}_{k})-1
and
(q,\frac{-l}{m_{L}})\in\mathrm{Crit}f_{L}\times\mathbb{Z}
with
\deg\mathfrak{o}(q,\frac{-l}{m_{L}})=\deg\mathfrak{o}(p,\frac{-k}{m_{L}})-1
.
The sums run over all
[\mathbf{v}]\in\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{*}(\tilde{q}_{l},\tilde{p}_{k};\mathcal{D}_{H})
and
[\mathbf{w}]\in\overline{\mathcal{N}}^{*}((q,\frac{-l}{m_{L}}),(p,\frac{-k}{m_{L}});\mathcal{D})
.
See (
2.5
) and (
3.20
) for the definitions of the moduli spaces. The projection map
\Pi
given in (
3.15
) yields

\Pi:\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{*}(\tilde{q}_{l},\tilde{p}_{k};\mathcal{D}_{H})\longrightarrow\overline{\mathcal{N}}^{*}\big((q,\tfrac{-l}{m_{L}}),(p,\tfrac{-k}{m_{L}});\mathcal{D}\big),

which is bijective since it admits an inverse by Lemma
4.1
and Proposition
4.4
. Therefore, the commutativity of the side faces of the diagram in Proposition
3.14
yields that
\Theta
is a chain map, i.e.,
\Theta\circ\partial_{\mathcal{D}_{H}}=\partial_{\mathcal{D}}\circ\Theta
. This finishes the proof of Theorem
1.1
.(b).

Similarly, Lemma
4.3
and Proposition
4.7
imply that the projection map in (
3.37
) induces a bijection between moduli spaces involved in the definitions of the product structures on
\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}})
and
\mathrm{QH}_{*}(L^{\sigma})
. This yields that, under the assumption
N_{L}>\max\{\frac{1}{2}(\operatorname{dim}L+1),2\}
, the module isomorphism
\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}})\cong\mathrm{QH}_{*}(L^{\sigma})
given by (
4.6
) is a ring homomorphism. This completes the proof. ∎

5
Lagrangian RFH and wrapped Floer homology

Let
\mathcal{L}\subset Y
be a Legendrian lift of
L\subset\Sigma
as before. Throughout this section, we assume that there exist an exact (or Liouville) filling
(W,\lambda)
of
Y
and an exact Lagrangian filling
\mathcal{L}_{W}\subset W
of
\mathcal{L}
.
By attaching the cylindrical ends using the Liouville flow, we obtain

\widehat{W}:=W\cup([0,\infty)\times Y),\qquad\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{W}:=\mathcal{L}_{W}\cup([0,\infty)\times\mathcal{L}).

The entire symplectization
(\mathbb{R}\times Y,\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{L})
is embedded in
(\widehat{W},\mathcal{L}_{W})
.
By abuse of notation, we still denote the extension of the Liouville 1-form
\lambda
to the completion
\widehat{W}
by
\lambda
.
In this section, we analyze the interplay between the wrapped Floer homology for
\mathcal{L}_{W}
and the Rabinowitz Floer homology for
\mathcal{L}
or
(\mathcal{L}_{W},\mathcal{L})
under the following conditions:

(B1)

The exact filling
W
is topologically simple, meaning that the inclusion-induced map
\pi_{1}(Y)\to\pi_{1}(W)
is injective and the first Chern class
c_{1}^{TW}
of
W
vanishes on
\pi_{2}(W)
.

(B2)

The exact Lagrangian filling
\mathcal{L}_{W}
is topologically simple, i.e., the inclusion-induced map
\pi_{1}(Y,\mathcal{L})\to\pi_{1}(W,\mathcal{L}_{W})
is injective and the Maslov class
\mu_{\mathcal{L}_{W}}
vanishes on
\pi_{2}(W,\mathcal{L}_{W})
. Moreover, the minimal Maslov number
N_{L}
of
L
satisfies
N_{L}\geq 2
.

(B3)

There is a relative
\mathrm{Pin}^{\pm}
-structure
\hat{\sigma}
on
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{W}
that restricts to the relative
\mathrm{Pin}^{\pm}
-structure
\tilde{\sigma}
on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{L}
. More precisely, with respect to the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{\mathrm{C}}^{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{W};\mathrm{Pin}^{\pm}(n+1))\times\check{\mathrm{Z}}^{2}(\widehat{W};\mathbb{Z}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{\mathrm{C}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{L};\mathrm{Pin}^{\pm}(n+1))\times\check{\mathrm{Z}}^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times Y;\mathbb{Z}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{\mathrm{C}}^{1}(L;\mathrm{Pin}^{\pm}(n))\times\check{\mathrm{Z}}^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}_{2}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{*}}

(5.1)

of Čech cochains and cocycles with respect to suitable good covers,
\iota^{*}\hat{\sigma}=\tilde{\sigma}
holds, where
\tilde{\sigma}
is obtained from
\sigma
by the lifting described in Remark
3.13
and indicated by the vertical arrow.
Here,
\iota:(\mathbb{R}\times Y,\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{L})\to(\widehat{W},\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{W})
denotes the embedding.

Here, our standing assumption (C2), i.e.,
N_{L}>2
, is weakened to
N_{L}\geq 2
.

5.1
Lagrangian RFH in the presence of fillings

We briefly recall the construction of
\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(\mathcal{L}_{W}^{\hat{\sigma}},\mathcal{L})
, where
\hat{\sigma}
is a relative
\mathrm{Pin}^{\pm}
-structure on
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{W}
. Without this structure, the homology is understood to be defined over
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. We denote by
H_{W}:\widehat{W}\to\mathbb{R}
the Hamiltonian obtained by extending
H\in\mathcal{H}
defined in (
3.4
) to be constant over the rest of
\widehat{W}
. Since
\mathcal{L}_{W}
is exact, there is a smooth function
g
on
{\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{W}}
such that
dg=\lambda|_{\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{W}}
. We define

\begin{split}&\mathcal{A}_{H_{W}}:C^{\infty}(([0,1],\{0,1\}),(\widehat{W},\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{W}))\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\\
&\mathcal{A}_{H_{W}}(x):=\int_{0}^{1}x^{*}\lambda-\int_{0}^{1}H_{W}(x(t))dt-g(x(1))+g(x(0)).\end{split}

(5.2)

Critical points of
\mathcal{A}_{H_{W}}
on
(\mathbb{R}\times Y,\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{L})\subset(\widehat{W},\mathcal{L}_{W})
coincide with those of
\mathcal{A}_{H}
defined in (
3.5
). We use the same notation
\tilde{p}_{k}\in\mathrm{Crit}\mathcal{A}_{H_{W}}
for a chord in
(-\eta,\eta)\times Y\subset\widehat{W}
associated to
(\tilde{p},k)\in\mathrm{Crit}f_{\mathcal{L}}\times\mathbb{Z}
. Throughout this section, the same convention applies to all chords denoted by
\tilde{p}_{k}
,
\tilde{q}_{l}
,
\tilde{q}_{k^{\prime}}
, and
\tilde{r}_{k^{\prime\prime}}
. For any action window
(a,b)\subset\mathbb{R}
, the chain-level generators for
\mathrm{RFH}_{*}^{(a,b)}(\mathcal{L}_{W}^{\hat{\sigma}},\mathcal{L})
consist of the orientation lines associated with
\tilde{p}_{k}
that are contractible in
W
relative to
\mathcal{L}_{W}
, equipped with equivalence classes of cappings whose actions lie in
(a,b)
. Here two cappings are equivalent if the disk obtained by gluing them has vanishing Maslov index. We write
\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(\mathcal{L}_{W}^{\hat{\sigma}},\mathcal{L})=\mathrm{RFH}_{*}^{(-\infty,\infty)}(\mathcal{L}_{W}^{\hat{\sigma}},\mathcal{L})
. In general, it may possess a larger set of chain-level generators than the chain complex for
\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(\mathcal{L})
.

For our purpose, we use a specific choice of almost complex structures on
\widehat{W}
. Let
J_{W}
be a
d\lambda
-compatible almost complex structure on
\widehat{W}
such that, on
(-2,\infty)\times Y
,
J_{W}
agrees with some
J_{Y}\in\mathcal{J}_{(Y,\mathcal{L})}^{\mathrm{reg}}
. Let
\{J_{W}^{\kappa}\}_{\kappa\in\mathbb{N}}
be a family of almost complex structures on
\widehat{W}
obtained by stretching
J_{W}
along
\{-1\}\times Y\subset\widehat{W}
, e.g. see
[
DL19b
, Lemma 2.7]
. Here
\kappa
is the stretching parameter.
We abbreviate
\mathcal{D}_{H_{W}}^{\kappa}:=(f_{\mathcal{L}},Z_{\mathcal{L}},H_{W},J_{W}^{\kappa})

and define the moduli space

\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{*}(\tilde{q}_{l},\tilde{p}_{k};\mathcal{D}_{H_{W}}^{\kappa})=\big\{[\mathbf{v}]=[(\tilde{v}_{1},\dots,\tilde{v}_{N})]\big\},

where
\tilde{v}_{1},\dots,\tilde{v}_{N}
are simple Floer strips in
(\widehat{W},\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{W})
, joined by trajectories of
Z_{\mathcal{L}}
, which connect
\tilde{q}_{l}
and
\tilde{p}_{k}
.
The only difference from
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{*}(\tilde{q}_{l},\tilde{p}_{k};\mathcal{D}_{H})
is that
\tilde{v}_{1},\dots,\tilde{v}_{N}
are smooth maps from
(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1],\mathbb{R}\times\{0,1\})
to
(\widehat{W},\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{W})
, rather than to
(\mathbb{R}\times Y,\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{L})
, solving

\partial_{s}\tilde{v}+J_{W}^{\kappa}(\tilde{v})(\partial_{t}\tilde{v}-X_{H_{W}}(\tilde{v}))=0.

Floer continuation strips in
(\widehat{W},\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{W})
are defined analogously, and the Rabinowitz Floer homology
\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(\mathcal{L}_{W}^{\hat{\sigma}},\mathcal{L})
is constructed in the same manner as
\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}})
. Similarly, the moduli space
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{*}(\tilde{r}_{k^{\prime\prime}},\tilde{p}_{k},\tilde{q}_{k^{\prime}};\mathcal{D}^{P,\kappa}_{H_{W}})
of simple Floer triangles with trajectories in
(\widehat{W},\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{W})
associated to
\mathcal{D}^{P,\kappa}_{H_{W}}=(f_{\mathcal{L}},Z^{1}_{\mathcal{L}},Z^{2}_{\mathcal{L}},Z^{3}_{\mathcal{L}},H_{W},J^{\kappa}_{W})
defines a ring structure on
\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(\mathcal{L}_{W}^{\hat{\sigma}},\mathcal{L})
.

Proposition 5.1
.

Assume that the stretching parameter
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
is sufficiently large. Assume also conditions (B1) and (B2). Then the following hold.

(a)

For any
(\tilde{p},k),(\tilde{q},l)\in\mathrm{Crit}f_{\mathcal{L}}\times m_{L}\mathbb{Z}
with
\mu(\tilde{p}_{k})-\mu(\tilde{q}_{l})=1
,
every element in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{*}(\tilde{q}_{l},\tilde{p}_{k};\mathcal{D}_{H_{W}}^{\kappa})
lies in
(-1,\infty)\times Y
. The corresponding statement for moduli spaces of Floer continuation strips with trajectories holds when the asymptotic chords have the same index.

(b)

Assume
N_{L}\geq\mathrm{max}\{\frac{1}{2}(\operatorname{dim}L+1),2\}
. Then, for any
(\tilde{p},k),(\tilde{q},k^{\prime}),(\tilde{r},k^{\prime\prime})\in\mathrm{Crit}f_{\mathcal{L}}\times m_{L}\mathbb{Z}
with
\mu(\tilde{p}_{k})+\mu(\tilde{q}_{k^{\prime}})-\mu(\tilde{r}_{k^{\prime\prime}})=\operatorname{dim}L
, every element in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{*}(\tilde{r}_{k^{\prime\prime}},\tilde{p}_{k},\tilde{q}_{k^{\prime}};\mathcal{D}^{P,\kappa}_{H_{W}})

lies in
(-1,\infty)\times Y
.

This proposition follows from the proofs of Proposition
3.12
and Proposition
3.24
with a minor modification. See
[
Ueb19
, Corollary 3.7.(ii)]
for the corresponding statement regarding Floer cylinders. Since we only consider the cases
\mu(\tilde{p}_{k})-\mu(\tilde{q}_{l})=1
and
\mu(\tilde{p}_{k})+\mu(\tilde{q}_{k^{\prime}})-\mu(\tilde{r}_{k^{\prime\prime}})=\operatorname{dim}L
, the weaker assumption
N_{L}\geq 2
is sufficient.

Proposition 5.2
.

Assume conditions (B1) and (B2). Then the following hold.

(a)

The homology
\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(\mathcal{L};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
is well-defined and isomorphic to
\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(\mathcal{L}_{W},\mathcal{L};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
.

(b)

If further
N_{L}\geq\mathrm{max}\{\frac{1}{2}(\operatorname{dim}L+1),2\}
, then
\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(\mathcal{L};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
admits the triangle product structure and the isomorphism in statement (a) is a ring isomorphism.

(c)

If condition (B3) is also satisfied,
\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}})
is well-defined, and statements (a) and (b) hold for
\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(\mathcal{L})
and
\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(\mathcal{L}_{W}^{\hat{\sigma}},\mathcal{L})
.

Proof.

This proposition is the Lagrangian counterpart of
[
Ueb19
, Corollaries 3.7 and 3.16]
. Recall that the chain-level generators for
\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(\mathcal{L};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
are
\tilde{p}_{k}
for
(\tilde{p},k)\in\mathrm{Crit}f_{\mathcal{L}}\times m_{L}\mathbb{Z}
, where
k\in m_{L}\mathbb{Z}
is equivalent to
\tilde{p}_{k}
being contractible in
Y
relative to
\mathcal{L}
by Proposition
3.4
. By condition (B2),
\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(\mathcal{L};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
and
\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(\mathcal{L}_{W},\mathcal{L};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
have the same chain-level generators. Moreover, by Proposition
5.1
, the boundary operators, continuation homomorphisms, and triangle products defining
\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(\mathcal{L};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
and
\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(\mathcal{L}_{W},\mathcal{L};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
coincide via neck-stretching. This proves statements (a) and (b).
Statement (c) can be proved analogously.
∎

Remark 5.3
.

In the language of augmentations, Proposition
5.1
.(a) shows that conditions (B1) and (B2) ensure that the augmentation induced by the filling
(W,\mathcal{L}_{W})
is trivial.
If these conditions are dropped,
\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(\mathcal{L}_{W},\mathcal{L};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
can be viewed as the Rabinowitz Floer homology of
\mathcal{L}
twisted by a potentially nontrivial augmentation induced by
(W,\mathcal{L}_{W})
. It is tempting to conjecture that a variant of Theorem
1.1
holds in this setting, namely,
\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(\mathcal{L}_{W},\mathcal{L};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
corresponds to the quantum homology of
L
equipped with a twisted differential.

5.2
Wrapped Floer homology and Viterbo transfer map

In the rest of Section
5
, we assume conditions (B1), (B2), and (B3). We recall the wrapped Floer homology
\mathrm{HW}_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W})
of
\mathcal{L}_{W}^{\hat{\sigma}}
and the Viterbo transfer map
\mathcal{V}:\mathrm{HW}_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W})\to\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W},\mathcal{L})
.

To describe the map
\mathcal{V}
, we define
\mathrm{HW}_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W})
using
H_{W}:\widehat{W}\to\mathbb{R}
appeared in the previous section. There are three types of critical points for
\mathcal{A}_{H_{W}}
given in (
5.2
):

(I)

constant chords on
\widehat{W}\setminus((-\epsilon,0]\times Y)
,

(II)

non-constant chords in the region
(-\epsilon,-\epsilon+\eta)\times Y
, and

(III)

constant and non-constant chords in the region
(-\eta,\eta)\times Y
.

When defining the Rabinowitz Floer homology of
\mathcal{L}
or
(\mathcal{L}_{W},\mathcal{L})
, we exclude type I and II chords. In contrast, the wrapped Floer homology of
\mathcal{L}_{W}
incorporates all three types of chords.
For any
b\in\mathbb{R}
, we consider the set
\mathfrak{m}^{(a,b)}_{\mathcal{L}}(H)
defined in Section
3.3.4
with
a=-\infty
,

\mathfrak{m}^{(-\infty,b)}_{\mathcal{L}}(H)=\{k\in m_{L}\mathbb{Z}\,|\,\exists\,r\in(-\eta,\eta)\text{ with }h^{\prime}(e^{r})=\tfrac{k}{d}\text{ and }e^{r}h^{\prime}(e^{r})-h(e^{r})<b\}.

Type II chords also appear in families similar to type III chords. For each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, we set

\mathcal{L}_{k}^{H,\mathrm{II}}:=\big\{x=(r_{x},c_{x})\in\mathrm{Crit}\mathcal{A}_{H_{W}}\mid r_{x}\in(-\epsilon,-\epsilon+\eta),\,h^{\prime}(e^{r_{x}})=k/d\big\},

cf. (
3.6
). Since
h^{\prime}<0
on
(e^{-\epsilon},e^{-\epsilon+\eta})
, see (
3.4
),
\mathcal{L}_{k}^{H,\mathrm{II}}
is nonempty only for
k<0
. If
\mathcal{L}_{k}^{H,\mathrm{II}}
is nonempty, then it is naturally identified with
\mathcal{L}
by
(r_{x},c_{x})\mapsto c_{x}(0)
. We set

\mathfrak{m}^{\mathrm{II}}_{\mathcal{L}}(H):=\big\{k\in m_{L}\mathbb{Z}\,|\,\exists\,r\in(-\epsilon,-\epsilon+\eta)\text{ with }h^{\prime}(e^{r})=k/d\big\}.

Each
(\tilde{p},k)\in\mathrm{Crit}f_{\mathcal{L}}\times\mathfrak{m}^{\mathrm{II}}_{\mathcal{L}}(H)
determines
\tilde{p}_{k}^{\mathrm{II}}\in\mathcal{L}_{k}^{H,\mathrm{II}}
by
\mathfrak{w}(\tilde{p}_{k}^{\mathrm{II}})=k
and
\tilde{p}_{k}^{\mathrm{II}}(0)=\tilde{p}
. The orientation line
\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{p}_{k}^{\mathrm{II}})
is defined in the same way as for type III chords and graded by

\mu(\tilde{p}_{k}^{\mathrm{II}}):=\operatorname{dim}\mathcal{L}-\operatorname{ind}_{f_{\mathcal{L}}}(\tilde{p})+\frac{2\tau_{\Sigma}k}{d}-1,

(5.3)

where
-1
is due to the concavity of
h
on
(e^{-\epsilon},e^{-\epsilon+\eta})
, cf. (
3.11
).

The space of type I chords is canonically isomorphic to
\mathcal{L}_{W}\setminus((-\epsilon,0]\times\mathcal{L})
. We also use a Morse-Bott approach to type I chords following
[
DL19b
, Section 3]
. We choose a Morse-Smale pair
(f_{\mathcal{L}_{W}},Z_{\mathcal{L}_{W}})
on
\mathcal{L}_{W}
such that
Z_{\mathcal{L}_{W}}
equals
-\partial_{r}
on
(-\epsilon,0]\times\mathcal{L}
. Let
x_{q}
denote the type I chord at
q\in\mathrm{Crit}f_{\mathcal{L}_{W}}
.
As before, we have the associated orientation line
\mathfrak{o}(x_{q})
graded by

\mu(x_{q}):=\operatorname{dim}\mathcal{L}_{W}-1-\operatorname{ind}_{f_{\mathcal{L}_{W}}}(q)=\operatorname{dim}\mathcal{L}-\operatorname{ind}_{f_{\mathcal{L}_{W}}}(q).

We define

\mathrm{FC}_{*}^{(-\infty,b)}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W};H_{W}):=\bigoplus_{q\in\mathrm{Crit}f_{\mathcal{L}_{W}}}\mathfrak{o}(x_{q})\oplus\bigoplus_{(\tilde{p},k)}\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{p}_{k}^{\mathrm{II}})\oplus\bigoplus_{(\tilde{p},k)}\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{p}_{k}),

where the second and third big direct sums are over all
(\tilde{p},k)\in\mathrm{Crit}f_{\mathcal{L}}\times\mathfrak{m}^{\mathrm{II}}_{\mathcal{L}}(H)
and
(\tilde{p},k)\in\mathrm{Crit}f_{\mathcal{L}}\times\mathfrak{m}^{(-\infty,b)}_{\mathcal{L}}(H)
, respectively. Note that if
H_{W}
is sufficiently large in
(\mathcal{H},\prec)
, then the Hamiltonian actions of type I and II chords are lower than any chosen
b
.
The boundary map, as well as the continuation maps, are defined in a similar way as before.
Let us denote by
\mathrm{FH}^{(-\infty,b)}_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W};H_{W})
the resulting homology. The filtered and full wrapped Floer homologies of
\mathcal{L}_{W}^{\hat{\sigma}}
are defined by

\mathrm{HW}_{*}^{(-\infty,b)}(\mathcal{L}_{W}^{\hat{\sigma}}):=\varinjlim_{H_{W}}\mathrm{FH}^{(-\infty,b)}_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W};H_{W}),\qquad\mathrm{HW}_{*}(\mathcal{L}_{W}^{\hat{\sigma}}):=\varinjlim_{b\to\infty}\mathrm{HW}_{*}^{(-\infty,b)}(\mathcal{L}_{W}^{\hat{\sigma}}).

The triangle product induces a ring structure on these homologies.

Let
n=\operatorname{dim}L
.
For any
a<0<b
, the short exact sequence of chain complexes

0\to\mathrm{FC}_{*}^{(-\infty,a)}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W};{H_{W}})\to\mathrm{FC}_{*}^{(-\infty,b)}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W};{H_{W}})\to\mathrm{FC}_{*}^{(a,b)}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W};{H_{W}})\to 0,

where the last one is the quotient complex, induces a long exact sequence

\cdots\to\mathrm{HW}^{n-i}_{(-\infty,-a)}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W})\to\mathrm{HW}_{i}^{(-\infty,b)}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W})\to\mathrm{RFH}_{i}^{(a,b)}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W},\mathcal{L})\to\mathrm{HW}^{n-i+1}_{(-\infty,-a)}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W})\to\cdots

by taking the limit over
H_{W}
, see
[
CFO10
]
for details. Here and below
\mathrm{HW}^{*}
refers to wrapped Floer cohomology.
Passing to the limits
\varinjlim\limits_{b\uparrow+\infty}\varprojlim\limits_{a\downarrow-\infty}
, we obtain

\cdots\to\mathrm{HW}^{n-i}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W})\to\mathrm{HW}_{i}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W})\xrightarrow{\mathcal{V}}\mathrm{RFH}_{i}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W},\mathcal{L})\to\mathrm{HW}^{n-i+1}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W})\to\cdots.

(5.4)

The Viterbo transfer map
\mathcal{V}
is a ring homomorphism.

Following
[
Ueb19
,
CO26
]
, we define the reduced wrapped Floer homology

\mathrm{HW}^{\mathrm{red}}_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W}):=\mathrm{HW}_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W})/\operatorname{ker}\mathcal{V}.

Since
\mathcal{V}
is a ring homomorphism,
\operatorname{ker}\mathcal{V}
is an ideal and
\mathrm{HW}^{\mathrm{red}}_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W})
is a ring with unit. Thus
\mathcal{V}
induces an injective ring homomorphism

\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{red}}:\mathrm{HW}^{\mathrm{red}}_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W})\longrightarrow\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W},\mathcal{L}).

We observe from the construction of (
5.4
) that
\mathrm{HW}^{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W})
is generated by type I and II chords. Thus, by (
5.3
),
\mathrm{HW}^{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W})
is supported in degrees bounded from below. This implies

\mathrm{HW}_{i}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W})=\mathrm{HW}_{i}^{\mathrm{red}}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W}),\qquad\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{red}}:\mathrm{HW}_{i}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}\mathrm{RFH}_{i}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W},\mathcal{L}),\quad\forall i\gg 0.

(5.5)

In fact, the map
\mathrm{HW}^{n-i}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W})\to\mathrm{HW}_{i}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W})
in (
5.4
) factors through the map
\mathrm{H}_{i+1}(\mathcal{L}_{W};\mathcal{O})\to\mathrm{H}_{i+1}(\mathcal{L}_{W},\mathcal{L};\mathcal{O})
induced by the inclusion, where
\mathcal{O}
denotes the relevant orientation local system. Thus,
\mathrm{HW}_{i}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W})=\mathrm{HW}^{\mathrm{red}}_{i}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W})
for all
i>n=\operatorname{dim}L
.

5.3
Localization of reduced wrapped Floer homology

Throughout this section, we assume that
N_{L}
is finite and satisfies
N_{L}\geq\mathrm{max}\{\frac{1}{2}(\operatorname{dim}L+1),2\}
. By Proposition
5.2
,
\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}})
is isomorphic to
\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(\mathcal{L}_{W}^{\hat{\sigma}},\mathcal{L})
as a ring, and we denote the multiplicative unit by
1_{\mathrm{RFH}}
. We choose a Morse function
f_{L}
on
L
with exactly one local minimum point, which we denote by
q\in\mathrm{Crit}f_{L}
.
As before, for
i=1,\dots,d
, let
q^{i}_{0}\in\mathcal{L}^{H}_{0}
be the Hamiltonian chord with
\mathfrak{w}(q^{i}_{0})=0
and
q^{i}_{0}(0)=q^{i}\in\mathrm{Crit}f_{\mathcal{L}}
that projects to
q
, i.e.,
q_{0}^{i}
is the constant chord at
q^{i}
. The orientation line
\mathfrak{o}(q^{i}_{0})
has a canonical orientation.
To see this, let
\hat{x}
and
w
be the constant cappings of
q^{i}_{0}
and
q
, respectively. The horizontal part of
D_{\hat{x}}\#0
and
D_{w}\#0
have isomorphic kernel and cokernel as discussed in Section
3.3.3
. Thus, the horizontal part has a canonical orientation. The vertical part of
D_{\hat{x}}\#0
has a canonical orientation as discussed below (
3.23
). As a consequence,
D_{\hat{x}}\#0
has a canonical orientation, which determines a generator
o_{q^{i}_{0}}\in\mathfrak{o}(q^{i}_{0})
. The unit
1_{\mathrm{RFH}}
is represented by

o_{q_{0}}:=\sum_{i=1}^{d}o_{q^{i}_{0}}\in\mathrm{RFC}_{n}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}}).

(5.6)

As discussed in Remark
3.19
,
\mathrm{RFC}_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}})
admits a
\mathbb{Z}[T,T^{-1}]
-algebra structure which is compatible with the one in
\mathrm{QC}_{*}(L^{\sigma})
. In the case where
\deg T=N_{L}
,
T(1_{\mathrm{RFH}})\in\mathrm{RFH}_{n+N_{L}}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}})
is represented by

o_{q_{m_{L}}}:=\sum_{i=1}^{d}o_{q^{i}_{m_{L}}}\in\mathrm{RFC}_{n+N_{L}}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}}),

where
o_{q^{i}_{m_{L}}}:=To_{q^{i}_{0}}\in\mathfrak{o}(q^{i}_{m_{L}})
for
i=1,\dots,d
. When
\deg T=2N_{L}
, we have the same formula with
N_{L}
and
m_{L}
replaced by
2N_{L}
and
2m_{L}
, respectively.

Lemma 5.4
.

The homology class
T(1_{\mathrm{RFH}})
is in the image of the map
\mathcal{V}
.

Proof.

We present the proof for the case where
\deg T=N_{L}
. The same proof applies to the other case.
Recall from Section
5.2
that
\mathcal{V}
is induced by the quotient map

\mathrm{FC}_{*}^{(-\infty,b)}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W};{H_{W}})\longrightarrow\mathrm{FC}_{*}^{(a,b)}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W};{H_{W}}).

Let
a\ll 0\ll b
and let
H_{W}
be sufficiently large. The chain
o_{q_{m_{L}}}\in\mathrm{FC}_{*}^{(a,b)}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W};{H_{W}})
can be viewed as a chain in
\mathrm{FC}_{*}^{(-\infty,b)}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W};{H_{W}})
, where these two chains are in correspondence via the above quotient map.
Therefore, it suffices to show that
o_{q_{m_{L}}}
is a cycle in
\mathrm{FC}_{*}^{(-\infty,b)}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W};{H_{W}})
, i.e.,
\partial_{H_{W}}(o_{q_{m_{L}}})=0
, where
\partial_{H_{W}}
is the boundary map.
To this end, we write

\partial_{H_{W}}(o_{q_{m_{L}}})=\sum_{z\in\mathrm{Crit}f_{\mathcal{L}_{W}}}a_{z}o_{x_{z}}+\sum_{(\tilde{p},k)}b_{\tilde{p},k}o_{\tilde{p}_{k}^{\mathrm{II}}}+\sum_{(\tilde{p},k)}c_{\tilde{p},k}o_{\tilde{p}_{k}}

where the last two sums range over
(\tilde{p},k)\in\mathrm{Crit}f_{\mathcal{L}}\times\mathfrak{m}^{\mathrm{II}}_{\mathcal{L}}(H)
and
(\tilde{p},k)\in\mathrm{Crit}f_{\mathcal{L}}\times\mathfrak{m}^{(-\infty,b)}_{\mathcal{L}}(H)
.
Here,
o_{x_{z}}\in\mathfrak{o}(x_{z})
,
o_{\tilde{p}_{k}^{\mathrm{II}}}\in\mathfrak{o}(\tilde{p}_{k}^{\mathrm{II}})
,
o_{\tilde{p}_{k}}\in\mathfrak{o}({\tilde{p}_{k}})
are generators, and
a_{z},b_{\tilde{p},k},c_{\tilde{p},k}\in\mathbb{Z}
.

We first show
c_{\tilde{p},k}=0
. Since the Hamiltonian action decreases along
\partial_{H_{W}}
, we have
c_{\tilde{p},k}=0
provided
k>m_{L}
.
For
k<m_{L}
, if
c_{\tilde{p},k}\neq 0
, we arrive at a contradiction:

1=\mu(\tilde{q}_{m_{L}})-\mu(\tilde{p}_{k})=\frac{2\tau_{\Sigma}m_{L}}{d}-\frac{2\tau_{\Sigma}k}{d}-\operatorname{ind}_{f_{\mathcal{L}}}(\tilde{q})+\operatorname{ind}_{f_{\mathcal{L}}}(\tilde{p})\geq N_{L}\geq 2.

Here we used that
N_{L}=\frac{2\tau_{\Sigma}m_{L}}{d}
and
\operatorname{ind}_{f_{\mathcal{L}}}(\tilde{q})=0
. In the case of
k=m_{L}
, the boundary map contributing to
c_{\tilde{p},m_{L}}
is solely given by flow lines of
Z_{\mathcal{L}}
. However, this contribution also vanishes since
q
is the unique minimum point of
f_{L}
. This proves
c_{\tilde{p},m_{L}}=0
.

An analogous degree argument also shows
b_{\tilde{p},k}=0
. Finally,
a_{z}=0
since otherwise

1=\mu(\tilde{q}_{m_{L}})-\mu(z)=n+\frac{2\tau_{\Sigma}m_{L}}{d}-\operatorname{ind}_{f_{\mathcal{L}}}(\tilde{q})-(n-\operatorname{ind}_{f_{\mathcal{L}_{W}}}(z))=N_{L}+\operatorname{ind}_{f_{\mathcal{L}_{W}}}(z)\geq 2.

Therefore,
\partial_{H_{W}}(o_{q_{m_{L}}})=0
holds, and
T(1_{\mathrm{RFH}})
lies in the image of
\mathcal{V}
.
∎

By Lemma
5.4
, there is a unique
\beta\in\mathrm{HW}_{*}^{\mathrm{red}}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W})
satisfying
\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{red}}(\beta)=T(1_{\mathrm{RFH}})
. Since
\mathrm{\mathcal{V}}^{\mathrm{red}}
is injective,
\beta
lies in the center of the ring
\mathrm{HW}_{*}^{\mathrm{red}}(\mathcal{L}_{W}^{\hat{\sigma}})
.
Using the triangle product
\star
on
\mathrm{HW}_{*}^{\mathrm{red}}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W})
,
we define a
\mathbb{Z}[T]
-algebra structure on
\mathrm{HW}_{*}^{\mathrm{red}}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W})
by

\mathbb{Z}[T]\times\mathrm{HW}_{*}^{\mathrm{red}}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W})\to\mathrm{HW}_{*}^{\mathrm{red}}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W}),\qquad(T^{k},a)\mapsto\beta^{k}\star a.

One can readily see that
\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{red}}
is
\mathbb{Z}[T]
-linear and hence a
\mathbb{Z}[T]
-algebra homomorphism.
Let us consider the localization

T^{-1}\mathrm{HW}_{*}^{\mathrm{red}}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W}):=\big\{\beta^{k}\big\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}}^{-1}\,\mathrm{HW}_{*}^{\mathrm{red}}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W}),

which has a natural
\mathbb{Z}[T,T^{-1}]
-algebra structure.

Proposition 5.5
.

The map
\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{red}}
induces an
\mathbb{Z}[T,T^{-1}]
-algebra isomorphism

\widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{\mathrm{red}}:T^{-1}\mathrm{HW}_{*}^{\mathrm{red}}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W})\to\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(\mathcal{L}_{W}^{\hat{\sigma}},\mathcal{L})\cong\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}}),\qquad\frac{A}{\beta^{k}}\mapsto T^{-k}(\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{red}}(A)),

where
A\in\mathrm{HW}_{*}^{\mathrm{red}}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W})
and
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.20
,
\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}})
is generated by some
A_{1},\dots,A_{l}\in\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}})
over
\mathbb{Z}[T,T^{-1}]
.
As pointed out in (
5.5
),
\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{red}}:\mathrm{HW}_{i}^{\mathrm{red}}(\mathcal{L}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{W})\to\mathrm{RFH}_{i}(\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\sigma}})
is an isomorphism for large
i
. Thus, for large
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
T^{N}(A_{j})
lies in the image of
\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{red}}
for every
j=1,\dots,l
. This proves that
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{\mathrm{red}}
is surjective. Moreover,
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{\mathrm{red}}
is injective since
\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{red}}
is so. This completes the proof.
∎

6
Applications

6.1
Computations for the unit cotangent fiber of Zoll manifolds

Let
T^{*}N
be the cotangent bundle of a closed, connected,
n
-dimensional Riemannian manifold
N
with
n\geq 3
, equipped with its canonical exact symplectic form.
We denote the unit disk bundle by
D^{*}N
and the unit circle bundle by
S^{*}N=\partial D^{*}N
with respect to the dual metric on
T^{*}N
.
The unit cotangent fiber
S^{*}_{q}N
over
q\in N
is a Legendrian sphere, which comes with an exact Lagrangian filling
D^{*}_{q}N
.
Let
w_{2}^{TN}\in\mathrm{H}^{2}(N;\mathbb{Z}_{2})
denote the second Stiefel–Whitney class of the tangent bundle
TN
. For the projection
\mathrm{pr}:T^{*}N\to N
, we write

\hat{b}:=\mathrm{pr}^{*}w_{2}^{TN}.

(6.1)

Since
T^{*}_{q}N
is contractible, there is a unique relative
\mathrm{Spin}
-structure
\hat{\sigma}
on
T^{*}_{q}N
with respect to the background class
\hat{b}
. Let
\tilde{\sigma}
be the restriction of
\hat{\sigma}
to
\mathbb{R}\times S^{*}_{q}N
.
We also use the notation
(D_{q}^{*}N;\hat{\sigma})=(D_{q}^{*}N)^{\hat{\sigma}}
and
(S_{q}^{*}N;\tilde{\sigma})=(S_{q}^{*}N)^{\tilde{\sigma}}
.

In general, the Rabinowitz Floer homology for the symplectization of
S^{*}N
may not be well-defined. However, as in Section
5.1
, one can define the Rabinowitz Floer homology
\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(D^{*}_{q}N,S^{*}_{q}N;\hat{\sigma})
for the pair
(D^{*}_{q}N,S^{*}_{q}N)
with
\hat{\sigma}
inside
T^{*}N
.

Lemma 6.1
.

The transfer map
\mathcal{V}:\mathrm{HW}_{*}(D^{*}_{q}N;\hat{\sigma})\to\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(D^{*}_{q}N,S^{*}_{q}N;\hat{\sigma})
is injective. In particular,
\mathrm{HW}_{*}^{\mathrm{red}}(D_{q}^{*}N;\hat{\sigma})=\mathrm{HW}_{*}(D_{q}^{*}N;\hat{\sigma})
.

Proof.

We recall from (
5.4
) the long exact sequence

\dots\to\mathrm{HW}^{n-1-i}(D^{*}_{q}N;\hat{\sigma})\to\mathrm{HW}_{i}(D^{*}_{q}N;\hat{\sigma})\xrightarrow{\mathcal{V}}\mathrm{RFH}_{i}(D^{*}_{q}N,S^{*}_{q}N;\hat{\sigma})\to\cdots.

As established in
[
APS08
,
AS10
,
Abo12
,
AS14
]
, we have

\mathrm{HW}_{*}(D^{*}_{q}N;\hat{\sigma})\cong\mathrm{H}_{*-n+1}(\Omega^{0}N),\qquad\mathrm{HW}^{n-1-*}(D^{*}_{q}N;\hat{\sigma})\cong\mathrm{H}^{-*}(\Omega^{0}N),

(6.2)

where
\Omega^{0}N
is the contractible component of the based loop space of
N
. Recall that, under our grading convention, the unit of
\mathrm{HW}_{*}(D^{*}_{q}N;\hat{\sigma})
lies in degree
\operatorname{dim}S_{q}^{*}N=n-1
.
This yields that
\mathrm{HW}_{i}(D^{*}_{q}N;\hat{\sigma})=0
for
i<n-1
, and
\mathrm{HW}^{n-1-i}(D^{*}_{q}N;\hat{\sigma})=0
for
i>0
. Therefore, the map
\mathrm{HW}^{n-1-i}(D^{*}_{q}N;\hat{\sigma})\to\mathrm{HW}_{i}(D^{*}_{q}N;\hat{\sigma})
vanishes, and
\mathcal{V}
is injective. The last assertion follows from the definition of the reduced wrapped Floer homology.
∎

Lemma 6.2
.

For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the map
\pi_{k}(S^{*}N,S^{*}_{q}N)\to\pi_{k}(D^{*}N,D^{*}_{q}N)
induced by the inclusion
\iota:(S^{*}N,S^{*}_{q}N)\hookrightarrow(D^{*}N,D^{*}_{q}N)
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

The projections
\wp:(D^{*}N,D^{*}_{q}N)\to(N,q)
and
\wp|_{S^{*}N}:(S^{*}N,S^{*}_{q}N)\to(N,q)
induce isomorphisms on homotopy groups.
Since
\wp|_{S^{*}N}=\wp\circ\iota
,
\iota
also induces isomorphisms.
∎

This lemma and the well-known fact
\mu_{D_{q}^{*}N}(\pi_{2}(D^{*}N,D_{q}^{*}N))=0
verify condition (B2) (except the condition on
N_{L}
) introduced in Section
5
. Condition (B1) holds automatically in the current setting.

For condition (B3) (as well as
N_{L}
) to be well-defined, we now assume that the metric on
N
is Zoll, i.e., all geodesics are closed and have the same minimal length.
After rescaling, we normalize this minimal length to be
1
.
The associated cogeodesic flow induces a free
S^{1}
-action on
S^{*}N
, preserving the Liouville 1-form, and hence defining a prequantization bundle
\pi_{S^{*}N}:S^{*}N\to\Sigma:=S^{*}N/S^{1}
.
For each
q\in N
, the unit cotangent fiber
S^{*}_{q}N
projects to a possibly immersed Lagrangian sphere in
\Sigma
.
We further assume that all prime geodesics on
N
are simple, i.e., have no self-intersections, so that

L_{N}:=\pi_{S^{*}N}(S^{*}_{q}N)

is an embedded Lagrangian sphere in
\Sigma
.

Remark 6.3

(Cf. Remark
2.1
)
.

Since
S^{*}_{q}N
and
D^{*}_{q}N
are orientable, we consider relative
\mathrm{Spin}
-structures rather than relative
\mathrm{Pin}^{\pm}
-structures. Recall that
\mathrm{Spin}(n)
is the unique central extension of
\mathrm{SO}(n)
by
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
An
n
-dimensional orientable submanifold
Q
of
M
is said to be relatively
\mathrm{Spin}
if

w_{2}^{TQ}\in\mathrm{im}\Big(\iota^{*}:\mathrm{H}^{2}(M;\mathbb{Z}_{2})\to\mathrm{H}^{2}(Q;\mathbb{Z}_{2})\Big).

Let
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{V}
be good covers of
Q
and
M
, respectively, as in Remark
2.1
. A relative
\mathrm{Spin}
-structure on
Q
is an equivalence class of a pair

\sigma=(g,\beta)\in\check{\mathrm{C}}^{1}(\mathcal{U};\mathrm{Spin}(n))\times\check{\mathrm{Z}}^{2}(\mathcal{V};\mathbb{Z}_{2})

where
g
is a lift of the element of
\check{\mathrm{C}}^{1}(\mathcal{U};\mathrm{SO}(n))
representing the frame bundle of
TQ
and
\beta\in\check{\mathrm{Z}}^{2}(\mathcal{V};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
satisfies
\beta|_{L}=\delta g
.
The equivalence relation is given as in Remark
2.1
.

Lemma 6.4
.

Assume
\operatorname{dim}N\geq 3
. Let
\hat{\sigma}=[(\hat{g},\hat{\beta})]
be the unique relative
\mathrm{Spin}
-structure with background class
\hat{b}=\mathrm{pr}^{*}w_{2}^{TN}
as above. If there exists
b\in\mathrm{H}^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}_{2})
satisfying

\iota^{*}\hat{b}=\pi_{S^{*}N}^{*}b\in\mathrm{H}^{2}(S^{*}N;\mathbb{Z}_{2})

for
\iota:S^{*}N\hookrightarrow T^{*}N
, then, for each such
b
, there is a unique relative
\mathrm{Spin}
-structure
\sigma=[(g,\beta)]
with
[\beta]=b
on
L_{N}
whose lift
\tilde{\sigma}
to
\mathbb{R}\times S^{*}_{q}N
coincides with
\hat{\sigma}
in the sense of (B3).

Proof.

We consider the diagram in (
5.1
) with
\mathrm{Pin}^{\pm}
replaced by
\mathrm{Spin}
. Let
[(\iota^{*}\hat{g},\iota^{*}\hat{\beta})]
be a relative
\mathrm{Spin}
-structure on
\mathbb{R}\times S^{*}_{q}N
obtained from
\hat{\sigma}
. Since
[\iota^{*}\hat{\beta}]=\iota^{*}\hat{b}
, we have
w_{2}^{TS_{q}^{*}N}=(\iota^{*}\hat{b})|_{S_{q}^{*}N}=(\pi_{S^{*}N}^{*}b)|_{S_{q}^{*}N}
. Since
\pi^{*}_{S^{*}N}
restricts to a diffeomorphism from
S^{*}_{q}N
to
L_{N}
, we have
w_{2}^{TL_{N}}=b|_{L_{N}}
and in particular
w_{2}^{TL_{N}}
is in the image of the map
\mathrm{H}^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}_{2})\to\mathrm{H}^{2}(L_{N};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
. Therefore, there exists a relative
\mathrm{Spin}
-structure
[(g,\beta)]
on
L_{N}
with
[\beta]=b
.

It remains to show that the lift
\tilde{\sigma}
of
\sigma
with background class
[\pi_{S^{*}N}^{*}\beta]
agrees with
[(\iota^{*}\hat{g},\iota^{*}\hat{\beta})]
.
Since
\operatorname{dim}N\geq 3
, the map
\mathrm{H}^{2}(S^{*}N,S^{*}_{q}N;\mathbb{Z}_{2})\to\mathrm{H}^{2}(S^{*}N;\mathbb{Z}_{2})
is injective. Hence, there exists a unique relative
\mathrm{Spin}
-structure with respect to the background class
\iota^{*}\hat{b}
. Both
\pi^{*}_{S^{*}N}\beta
and
\iota^{*}\hat{\beta}
represent
\iota^{*}\hat{b}
, and this completes the proof.
∎

Corollary 6.5
.

Let
N
be a closed, connected, Riemannian Zoll manifold with
\operatorname{dim}N\geq 3
, all of whose prime geodesics are simple. Assume that there exists a relative
\mathrm{Spin}
-structure
\sigma
on
L_{N}
described in Lemma
6.4
and that the minimal Maslov number of
L_{N}
is at least
\mathrm{max}\{\frac{1}{2}(\operatorname{dim}L_{N}+1),2\}
. Then, there exist
\mathbb{Z}[T,T^{-1}]
-algebra isomorphisms

\mathrm{QH}_{*}(L_{N}^{\sigma})\cong\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(S^{*}_{q}N;\tilde{\sigma})\cong T^{-1}\mathrm{HW}_{*}(D_{q}^{*}N;\hat{\sigma}),

where the
\mathbb{Z}[T]
-algebra structure on
\mathrm{HW}_{*}(D_{q}^{*}N;\hat{\sigma})\cong\mathrm{HW}_{*}^{\mathrm{red}}(D_{q}^{*}N;\hat{\sigma})
is defined before Proposition
5.5
.

Proof.

By Theorem
1.1
, Proposition
5.2
, and Proposition
5.5
, we have isomorphisms

\mathrm{QH}_{*}(L_{N}^{\sigma})\cong\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(S^{*}_{q}N;\tilde{\sigma})\cong\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(D^{*}_{q}N,S^{*}_{q}N;\hat{\sigma})\cong T^{-1}\mathrm{HW}_{*}^{\mathrm{red}}(D^{*}_{q}N;\hat{\sigma}).

By Lemma
6.1
,
\mathrm{HW}_{*}^{\mathrm{red}}(D^{*}_{q}N;\hat{\sigma})\allowbreak=\mathrm{HW}_{*}(D^{*}_{q}N;\hat{\sigma})
, and this finishes the proof.
∎

In the rest of this section, we examine the Lagrangian sphere
L_{N}
for the cases
N\in\{S^{k},\mathbb{RP}^{k},\mathbb{CP}^{k}\}
equipped with the standard metric. In these cases, well-known computations of
\mathrm{H}_{*}(\Omega^{0}N)
enable one to compute
\mathrm{QH}_{*}(L_{N}^{\sigma})
as a ring.

6.1.1
N=S^{k}
for
k\geq 3

We consider the unit cotangent bundle of the unit sphere
S^{k}\subset\mathbb{R}^{k+1}
,

D^{*}S^{k}=\{(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})\in\mathbb{R}^{k+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{k+1}\mid|\mathbf{a}|^{2}\leq 1,\;|\mathbf{b}|^{2}=1,\;\mathbf{a}\cdot\mathbf{b}=0\}

and the smooth quadric

Q^{k-1}=\{[z_{0}:\dots:z_{k}]\in\mathbb{CP}^{k}\mid z_{0}^{2}+\dots+z_{k}^{2}=0\}

equipped with the restriction of the Fubini-Study form
\omega_{\mathrm{FS}}
.
The cogeodesic flow on
S^{*}S^{k}
induces the
S^{1}
-action on
S^{*}S^{k}

t\cdot(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})=\big(\cos(2\pi t)\mathbf{a}-\sin(2\pi t)\mathbf{b},\sin(2\pi t)\mathbf{a}+\cos(2\pi t)\mathbf{b}\big),\qquad t\in\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}=S^{1}.

The associated prequantization bundle is given by

\begin{split}\pi_{S^{*}S^{k}}:S^{*}S^{k}\to Q^{k-1},\qquad(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})&\mapsto[a_{0}+ib_{0}:\dots:a_{k}+ib_{k}]\end{split}

with the Euler class
-[\omega_{\mathrm{FS}}|_{Q^{k-1}}]
.
The unit cotangent fiber
S^{*}_{q}S^{k}
projects to

L_{S^{k}}=\left\{[i:x_{1}:\dots:x_{k}]\in\mathbb{CP}^{k}\mid\sum_{j=1}^{k}x_{j}^{2}=1,x_{j}\in\mathbb{R}\right\}\subset Q^{k-1}.

It is a monotone Lagrangian sphere with minimal Maslov number
2(k-1)
as
(Q^{k-1},\omega_{\mathrm{FS}}|_{Q^{k-1}})
is monotone with minimal Chern number
k-1
.

Next, we investigate relative
\mathrm{Spin}
-structures on
L_{S^{k}}
. Let
[\omega_{\mathrm{FS}}|_{Q^{k-1}}]_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\in\mathrm{H}^{2}(Q^{k-1};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
be the class corresponding to
[\omega_{\mathrm{FS}}|_{Q^{k-1}}]
. Since the second Stiefel–Whitney class
w_{2}^{TS^{k}}\in\mathrm{H}^{2}(S^{k};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
vanishes, the background class
\hat{b}\in\mathrm{H}^{2}(T^{*}S^{k};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
defined in (
6.1
) is zero. Thus
\hat{\sigma}
is actually an absolute
\mathrm{Spin}
-structure.
The Gysin exact sequence

\dots\to\mathrm{H}^{0}(Q^{k-1};\mathbb{Z}_{2})\xrightarrow{\cup\,[\omega_{\mathrm{FS}}|_{Q^{k-1}}]_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}}\mathrm{H}^{2}(Q^{k-1};\mathbb{Z}_{2})\xrightarrow{\pi_{S^{*}S^{k}}^{*}}\mathrm{H}^{2}(S^{*}S^{k};\mathbb{Z}_{2})\to 0,

where we used the fact
\mathrm{H}^{1}(Q^{k-1};\mathbb{Z}_{2})=0
, implies that
\pi_{S^{*}S^{k}}^{*}
is surjective and thus an (absolute)
\mathrm{Spin}
-structure
\sigma
on
L_{S^{k}}
satisfying
\tilde{\sigma}=\iota^{*}\hat{\sigma}
exists due to Lemma
6.4
.
Furthermore, there are precisely two such relative
\mathrm{Spin}
-structures since, by Lemma
6.4
, there is a unique one for each element in
(\pi_{S^{*}S^{k}}^{*})^{-1}(\iota^{*}\mathrm{pr}^{*}w_{2}^{TS^{k}})=\operatorname{ker}(\pi_{S^{*}S^{k}}^{*})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}\langle[\omega_{\mathrm{FS}}|_{Q^{k-1}}]\rangle
.
Let
\sigma
be one of these two relative
\mathrm{Spin}
-structures on
L_{S^{k}}
.

Proposition 6.6
.

(Cf.
[
KS21a
, Remark 36]
)
There exists a ring isomorphism

\mathrm{QH}_{*}(L_{S^{k}}^{\sigma})[k-1]\cong\mathbb{Z}[x,x^{-1}],

where
x
has degree
k-1
. Here
[k-1]
denotes a degree shift by
k-1
.

Proof.

The minimal Maslov number
2(k-1)
of
{L_{S^{k}}}
is greater than
\mathrm{max}\{\frac{1}{2}(\operatorname{dim}L_{S^{k}}\allowbreak+1),2\}
. It follows from Corollary
6.5
that

\mathrm{QH}_{*}(L_{S^{k}}^{\sigma})\cong T^{-1}\mathrm{HW}_{*}(D_{q}^{*}S^{k};\hat{\sigma})

as
\mathbb{Z}[T,T^{-1}]
-algebras. By
[
BS53
, Theorem III.1.A]
and (
6.2
), we have
\mathrm{HW}_{*}(D_{q}^{*}S^{k};\hat{\sigma})[k-1]\cong\mathrm{H}_{*}(\Omega S^{k})\cong\mathbb{Z}[x]
where the formal variable
x
has degree
k-1
. Since
T(1_{\mathrm{HW}})
is a generator of
\mathrm{HW}_{3(k-1)}({D_{q}^{*}S^{k}};\hat{\sigma})
, it corresponds to
\mu x^{2}\in\mathrm{H}_{2(k-1)}(\Omega S^{k})
for
\mu\in\{-1,1\}
. Therefore, we have

T^{-1}\mathrm{HW}_{*}(D_{q}^{*}S^{k};\hat{\sigma})[k-1]\cong T^{-1}\mathbb{Z}[x]/(T-\mu x^{2})\cong\mathbb{Z}[x,x^{-1}],

where
x^{-1}=\mu T^{-1}x
.
This completes the proof.
∎

6.1.2
N=\mathbb{RP}^{k}
for
k\geq 3

Let
\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}
and
\hat{\sigma}
be relative
\mathrm{Spin}
-structures on
T^{*}_{[q]}\mathbb{RP}^{k}
and
T^{*}_{q}S^{k}
, respectively, with the background classes described in (
6.1
).
Since
\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(D^{*}_{[q]}\mathbb{RP}^{k},S^{*}_{[q]}\mathbb{RP}^{k};\hat{\sigma}^{\prime})
is defined using only contractible chords and the homology and cohomology groups of
\Omega^{0}\mathbb{RP}^{k}
are naturally isomorphic to those of
\Omega S^{k}
, we obtain

\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(D^{*}_{[q]}\mathbb{RP}^{k},S^{*}_{[q]}\mathbb{RP}^{k};\hat{\sigma}^{\prime})\cong\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(D^{*}_{q}S^{k},S^{*}_{q}S^{k};\hat{\sigma}).

(6.3)

In what follows, we provide an alternative perspective of this isomorphism.
We equip
\mathbb{RP}^{k}
with the metric induced from
S^{k}
via the antipodal quotient.
The cogeodesic flow induces the prequantization bundle

\pi_{S^{*}\mathbb{RP}^{k}}:S^{*}\mathbb{RP}^{k}\longrightarrow Q^{k-1}

with the Euler class
-2[\omega_{\mathrm{FS}}|_{Q^{k-1}}]
. Moreover,

L_{\mathbb{RP}^{k}}=\pi_{S^{*}\mathbb{RP}^{k}}(S^{*}_{[q]}\mathbb{RP}^{k})=\pi_{S^{*}S^{k}}(S^{*}_{q}S^{k})=L_{S^{k}}.

The second Stiefel–Whitney class of
T\mathbb{RP}^{k}
is computed as

w_{2}^{T\mathbb{RP}^{k}}=\binom{k+1}{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}\cong\mathrm{H}^{2}(\mathbb{RP}^{k};\mathbb{Z}_{2}).

By the Gysin exact sequence

\dots\to\mathrm{H}^{0}(Q^{k-1};\mathbb{Z}_{2})\xrightarrow{\cup\,2[\omega_{\mathrm{FS}}|_{Q^{k-1}}]_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}}\mathrm{H}^{2}(Q^{k-1};\mathbb{Z}_{2})\xrightarrow{\pi_{S^{*}\mathbb{RP}^{k}}^{*}}\mathrm{H}^{2}(S^{*}\mathbb{RP}^{k};\mathbb{Z}_{2})\to 0,

we know that
\pi_{S^{*}\mathbb{RP}^{k}}^{*}
is an isomorphism.
Thus, there exists a unique class
b\in\mathrm{H}^{2}(Q^{k-1};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
satisfying
\pi^{*}_{S^{*}\mathbb{RP}^{k}}b=\iota^{*}\mathrm{pr}^{*}w_{2}^{T\mathbb{RP}^{k}}
.
By Lemma
6.4
, there is a unique relative
\mathrm{Spin}
-structure
\sigma^{\prime}
on
L_{\mathbb{RP}^{k}}
which lifts to
\tilde{\sigma}^{\prime}
, the restriction of
\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}
to
\mathbb{R}\times S^{*}_{[q]}\mathbb{RP}^{k}
.
The relative
\mathrm{Spin}
-structure
\sigma^{\prime}
lifts to
\tilde{\sigma}
, the restriction of
\hat{\sigma}
to
\mathbb{R}\times S^{*}_{q}S^{k}
.
For such
\sigma^{\prime}
, we have

\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(S^{*}_{[q]}\mathbb{RP}^{k};\tilde{\sigma}^{\prime})\cong\mathrm{QH}_{*}(L_{\mathbb{RP}^{k}}^{\sigma^{\prime}})=\mathrm{QH}_{*}(L_{S^{k}}^{\sigma^{\prime}})\cong\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(S^{*}_{q}S^{k};\tilde{\sigma})

where the isomorphisms follow from Corollary
6.5
. By Proposition
5.2
, the first and last terms are identified with
\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(D^{*}_{[q]}\mathbb{RP}^{k},S^{*}_{[q]}\mathbb{RP}^{k};\hat{\sigma}^{\prime})
and
\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(D^{*}_{q}S^{k},S^{*}_{q}S^{k};\hat{\sigma})
, respectively. Thus, we recover the isomorphism in (
6.3
).

6.1.3
N=\mathbb{CP}^{k}
for
k\geq 2

We consider the metric on
\mathbb{CP}^{k}
induced by the round metric on
S^{2k+1}
via
the Hopf fibration
\pi_{S^{2k+1}}:S^{2k+1}\to\mathbb{CP}^{k}
. We identify the unit tangent bundle
S\mathbb{CP}^{k}
with the Hilbert 1-form and
S^{*}\mathbb{CP}^{k}
with the Liouville 1-form via the musical isomorphism. The set of horizontal unit vectors of
T_{q}S^{2k+1}
is written as

\{v\in T_{q}S^{2k+1}\mid v\cdot iq=0\}=\{v\in\mathbb{R}^{2k+2}\mid\|v\|=1,\;v\cdot q=0,\;v\cdot iq=0\},

where
i
is the complex structure on
\mathbb{R}^{2k+2}
and
\cdot
is the dot product.
The map
\pi_{S^{2k+1}}
gives a bijection between oriented closed geodesics on
\mathbb{CP}^{k}
and oriented horizontal great circles in
S^{2k+1}
modulo the
S^{1}
-action on
S^{2k+1}
. The latter corresponds to oriented horizontal real 2-planes in
\mathbb{C}^{k+1}
modulo the
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-action. Here, a 2-plane spanned by
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2k+2}
is said to be horizontal if
x\cdot iy=0
.
This correspondence implies the following.
For
(\bar{q},\bar{v})\in S\mathbb{CP}^{k}
, we choose
(q,v)\in TS^{2k+1}
such that
v
is horizontal and
(q,v)
projects to
(\bar{q},\bar{v})
by
\pi_{S^{2k+1}}
. The closed geodesic on
\mathbb{CP}^{k}
with initial velocity
\bar{v}
at
\bar{q}
lifts to the oriented great circle given by the intersection of
S^{2k+1}
and the oriented horizontal real 2-plane in
\mathbb{C}^{k+1}
spanned by
\{q,v\}
. The
S^{1}
-action on
S\mathbb{CP}^{k}
given by the geodesic flow induces the prequantization bundle

\begin{split}\pi_{S\mathbb{CP}^{k}}:S\mathbb{CP}^{k}&\longrightarrow F:=\{([z],[w])\in\mathbb{CP}^{k}\times\mathbb{CP}^{k}\mid\langle z,w\rangle=0\},\\[4.30554pt]
(\bar{q},\bar{v})&\longmapsto([q+iv],[q-iv])\end{split}

where
\langle z,w\rangle
denotes the standard Hermitian inner product.
Indeed,

\begin{split}\pi_{S{\mathbb{CP}^{k}}}(t\cdot(\bar{q},\bar{v}))&=\big([\cos(2\pi t)q-\sin(2\pi t)v+i(\sin(2\pi t)q+\cos(2\pi t)v)],\\
&\qquad[\cos(2\pi t)q-\sin(2\pi t)v-i(\sin(2\pi t)q+\cos(2\pi t)v)]\big)\\[2.15277pt]
&=\big([e^{2\pi it}(q+iv)],[e^{-2\pi it}(q-iv)]\big)\\[2.15277pt]
&=\pi_{S{\mathbb{CP}^{k}}}(\bar{q},\bar{v}),\end{split}

and

\langle q+iv,q-iv\rangle=\|q\|^{2}-\|v\|^{2}+i(\langle q,v\rangle+\overline{\langle q,v\rangle})=0

since
q
and
v
are orthogonal unit vectors. The base
F
is equipped with the symplectic form
\omega_{F}:=(\omega_{\mathrm{FS}}\oplus-\omega_{\mathrm{FS}})|_{F}
. A straightforward computation shows that
-\pi_{S{\mathbb{CP}^{k}}}^{*}\omega_{F}
equals the exterior derivative of the Hilbert 1-form. The unit tangent fiber
S_{q}\mathbb{CP}^{k}
at
q=[1:0:\cdots:0]
projects to the Lagrangian sphere

L_{\mathbb{CP}^{k}}=\{([1:w_{1}:\dots:w_{k}],[1:-w_{1}:\dots:-w_{k}])\in F\}.

Its minimal Maslov number is
2k
as the minimal Chern number of
F
is
k
.

To investigate relative
\mathrm{Spin}
-structures, we observe

w_{2}^{T\mathbb{CP}^{k}}=k+1\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}\cong\mathrm{H}^{2}(\mathbb{CP}^{k};\mathbb{Z}_{2}).

(6.4)

The Gysin exact sequence for
S^{2k-1}\hookrightarrow S\mathbb{CP}^{k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{pr}}}{{\to}}\mathbb{CP}^{k}
shows that

\mathrm{pr}^{*}:\mathrm{H}^{2}(\mathbb{CP}^{k};\mathbb{Z}_{2})\longrightarrow\mathrm{H}^{2}(S\mathbb{CP}^{k};\mathbb{Z}_{2})

for
k\geq 2
is an isomorphism. By the Gysin exact sequence for
S^{1}\hookrightarrow S\mathbb{CP}^{k}\to F
,

\dots\to\mathrm{H}^{0}(F;\mathbb{Z}_{2})\xrightarrow{\cup[\omega_{F}]_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}}\mathrm{H}^{2}(F;\mathbb{Z}_{2})\xrightarrow{\pi_{S\mathbb{CP}^{k}}^{*}}\mathrm{H}^{2}(S\mathbb{CP}^{k};\mathbb{Z}_{2})\to 0,

where we used
\mathrm{H}^{1}(F;\mathbb{Z}_{2})=0
,
\pi_{S\mathbb{CP}^{k}}^{*}
is surjective with kernel generated by
[\omega_{F}]_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\in\mathrm{H}^{2}(F;\mathbb{Z}_{2})
, the class corresponding to
[\omega_{F}]
. By the Lefschetz hyperplane theorem,
\mathrm{H}^{2}(F;\mathbb{Z}_{2})\cong\mathrm{H}^{2}(\mathbb{CP}^{k}\times\mathbb{CP}^{k};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
. By (
6.4
),
\pi^{*}_{S\mathbb{CP}^{k}}(b)=\mathrm{pr}^{*}w_{2}^{T\mathbb{CP}^{k}}
holds for
b\in\{0,[\omega_{F}]_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\}
when
k
is odd, and for
b\in\mathrm{H}^{2}(F;\mathbb{Z}_{2})\setminus\{0,[\omega_{F}]_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\}
when
k
is even. For each such background class
b
, there exists a unique
\mathrm{Spin}
-structure on
L_{\mathbb{CP}^{k}}
by Lemma
6.4
. Let
\sigma
be any such relative
\mathrm{Spin}
-structure.

Proposition 6.7
.

There exists a ring isomorphism

\mathrm{QH}_{*}(L_{\mathbb{CP}^{k}}^{\sigma})[2k-1]\cong\mathbb{Z}[x,y,y^{-1}]/(x^{2}),

where
x
has degree
1
and
y
has degree
2k
.

Proof.

We argue as in the proof of Proposition
6.6
.
Since the minimal Maslov number of
{L_{\mathbb{CP}^{k}}}
equals
2k
, Corollary
6.5
yields

\mathrm{QH}_{*}(L_{\mathbb{CP}^{k}}^{\sigma})\cong T^{-1}\mathrm{HW}_{*}(D_{[q]}^{*}\mathbb{CP}^{k};\hat{\sigma})

as rings.
It is computed in
[
CJY04
, Section 3]
that

\mathrm{HW}_{*}(D_{[q]}^{*}\mathbb{CP}^{k};\hat{\sigma})[2k-1]\cong\mathrm{H}_{*}(\Omega\mathbb{CP}^{k})\cong\mathbb{Z}[x,y]/(x^{2}),

where the formal variables
x
and
y
have degrees
1
and
2k
, respectively. For a degree reason,
T(1_{\mathrm{HW}})\in\mathrm{HW}_{4k-1}(D_{[q]}^{*}\mathbb{CP}^{k};\hat{\sigma})
corresponds to
\mu y\in\mathrm{H}_{2k}(\Omega\mathbb{CP}^{k})
where
\mu\in\{-1,1\}
. This completes the proof. ∎

Proposition 6.8
.

For the Lagrangian
3
-sphere
L_{\mathbb{CP}^{2}}\subset F\subset\mathbb{CP}^{2}\times\mathbb{CP}^{2}
with its unique absolute
\mathrm{Spin}
-structure
\sigma_{0}
, we have

\mathrm{QH}_{*}(L_{\mathbb{CP}^{2}}^{\sigma_{0}};\mathbb{K})=0,

where
\mathbb{K}
is any field of characteristic different from
2
.

Proof.

Let
\hat{\sigma}_{0}
be the absolute
\mathrm{Spin}
-structure on
T_{[q]}^{*}\mathbb{CP}^{2}
whose restriction is the lift
\tilde{\sigma}_{0}
of
\sigma_{0}
.
It follows from
[
Sei10
]
, see also
[
BKK24
, Section 6.3]
, that
\mathrm{SH}_{*}(D^{*}\mathbb{CP}^{2};\mathbb{K})=0
. Since
\mathrm{HW}_{*}(D_{[q]}^{*}\mathbb{CP}^{2};\hat{\sigma}_{0},\mathbb{K})
is a module over
\mathrm{SH}_{*}(D^{*}\mathbb{CP}^{2};\mathbb{K})
, it also vanishes.
Arguing as in the proof of Corollary
6.5
, we conclude
\mathrm{QH}_{*}(L_{\mathbb{CP}^{2}}^{\sigma_{0}};\mathbb{K})\cong T^{-1}\mathrm{HW}_{*}(D_{[q]}^{*}\mathbb{CP}^{2};\hat{\sigma}_{0},\mathbb{K})=0
. ∎

6.2
Some consequences of quantum invertibility of
\omega

Let
(\Sigma,\omega)
be a closed, connected symplectic manifold with an integral lift
[\omega]_{\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathrm{H}^{2}(\Sigma)
as before.
In this section, we present some consequences of the invertibility of
[\omega]_{\mathbb{Z}}
in the quantum cohomology
\mathrm{QH}^{*}(\Sigma)
of
\Sigma
.

Lemma 6.9
.

Assume that
[\omega]_{\mathbb{Z}}
is invertible in
\mathrm{QH}^{*}(\Sigma)
. Then any other integral lift of
[\omega]
is also invertible.

Proof.

Let
\beta
be another integral lift of
[\omega]
. Then,
\mu:=[\omega]_{\mathbb{Z}}-\beta
is a torsion class. By the divisor axiom for the Gromov-Witten invariants,
\mathrm{GW}_{A,3}(\cdot,\cdot,\mu)=\mathrm{GW}_{A,2}(\cdot,\cdot)\mu(A)
for every nonzero
A\in\mathrm{H}_{2}(\Sigma)
. Since
\mu
is torsion, these Gromov-Witten invariants vanish, and hence the quantum cup product with
\mu
agrees with the ordinary cup product with
\mu
. In particular,
\mu
is nilpotent in
\mathrm{QH}^{*}(\Sigma)
. If we denote by
[\omega]_{\mathbb{Z}}^{-1}
the inverse of
[\omega]_{\mathbb{Z}}
, then
[\omega]_{\mathbb{Z}}^{-1}\star\mu
is also nilpotent, where
\star
denotes the quantum cup product. Since
\beta=[\omega]_{\mathbb{Z}}-\mu=[\omega]_{\mathbb{Z}}\star(1_{\mathrm{QH}}-[\omega]_{\mathbb{Z}}^{-1}\star\mu)
, we have

\beta\star\Big(\big(1_{\mathrm{QH}}+([\omega]_{\mathbb{Z}}^{-1}\star\mu)+\cdots+([\omega]_{\mathbb{Z}}^{-1}\star\mu)^{m}\big)\star[\omega]_{\mathbb{Z}}^{-1}\Big)=1_{\mathrm{QH}}

for sufficiently large
m
, where
1_{\mathrm{QH}}
denotes the unit. This proves the lemma.
∎

Proposition 6.10
.

Let
L\subset(\Sigma,\omega)
be a closed, connected monotone Lagrangian submanifold such that
N_{L}\geq 3
and the map
\pi_{2}(\Sigma,L)\to\mathrm{H}_{1}(L)
is surjective. If
[\omega]_{\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathrm{QH}^{*}(\Sigma)
is invertible, then, for any
\mathrm{Pin}^{\pm}
-structure
\sigma
on
L
,
\mathrm{QH}_{*}(L^{\sigma})
is torsion of order
d=\frac{2c_{\Sigma}}{\gcd(2c_{\Sigma},m_{\Sigma}N_{L})}
.

Proof.

We consider the long exact sequence

\dots\longrightarrow\mathrm{H}^{2}(\Sigma,L)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\jmath^{*}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{H}^{2}(\Sigma)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota^{*}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{H}^{2}(L)\longrightarrow\cdots

and claim that
d[\omega]_{\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathrm{im\,}\jmath^{*}
. To see this, we choose any
A\in\mathrm{H}_{2}(\Sigma,L)
. By the surjectivity of
\pi_{2}(\Sigma,L)\to\mathrm{H}_{2}(\Sigma,L)\to\mathrm{H}_{1}(L)
, there exists
B\in\mathrm{im}(\pi_{2}(\Sigma,L)\to\mathrm{H}_{2}(\Sigma,L))
such that
A+B\in\mathrm{im}(\mathrm{H}_{2}(\Sigma)\to\mathrm{H}_{2}(\Sigma,L))
. Since
\omega
is integral, we have
\omega(A+B)\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Using the monotonicity of
L
and
c_{\Sigma}=\tau_{\Sigma}m_{\Sigma}
, we have

d\cdot\omega(B)=\frac{2\tau_{\Sigma}m_{\Sigma}\omega(B)}{\gcd(2c_{\Sigma},m_{\Sigma}N_{L})}=\frac{m_{\Sigma}\mu_{L}(B)}{\gcd(2c_{\Sigma},m_{\Sigma}N_{L})}\in\frac{m_{\Sigma}N_{L}}{\gcd(2c_{\Sigma},m_{\Sigma}N_{L})}\mathbb{Z}\subset\mathbb{Z}.

It follows that
d\cdot\omega(A)\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Hence there exists a class
\eta\in\mathrm{H}^{2}(\Sigma,L)
such that
\jmath^{*}\eta-d[\omega]_{\mathbb{Z}}
is torsion, i.e.,

\jmath^{*}\eta-d[\omega]_{\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathrm{im}(\mathrm{Ext}(\mathrm{H}_{1}(\Sigma),\mathbb{Z})\to\mathrm{H}^{2}(\Sigma)),

where the map
\mathrm{Ext}(\mathrm{H}_{1}(\Sigma),\mathbb{Z})\to\mathrm{H}^{2}(\Sigma)
is the natural homomorphism arising from the universal coefficient theorem.
By our assumption, the map
\mathrm{H}_{2}(\Sigma,L)\to\mathrm{H}_{1}(L)
is surjective, and thus the map
\mathrm{H}_{1}(\Sigma)\to\mathrm{H}_{1}(\Sigma,L)
is injective.
Since
\mathbb{Z}
is a principal ideal domain, this injectivity implies that the induced map
\mathrm{Ext}(\mathrm{H}_{1}(\Sigma,L),\mathbb{Z})\to\mathrm{Ext}(\mathrm{H}_{1}(\Sigma),\mathbb{Z})
is surjective.
Consequently,

d[\omega]_{\mathbb{Z}}\in\jmath^{*}\big(\eta+\mathrm{im}(\mathrm{Ext}(\mathrm{H}_{1}(\Sigma,L),\mathbb{Z})\to\mathrm{H}^{2}(\Sigma,L))\big),

and therefore
d[\omega]_{\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathrm{im\,}\jmath^{*}
. This proves the claim.

Next, we recall from
[
Bir06
, Section 5.2]
and
[
KS21b
, Proposition 15]
the spectral sequence converging to
\mathrm{QH}_{*}(L^{\sigma})
whose first page is given by

E^{1}=\mathrm{H}_{*}(L;\mathbb{Z}[T,T^{-1}]\otimes\mathcal{O})\oplus\mathrm{H}_{*}(L;\mathcal{T}\otimes\mathcal{O})[N_{L}],

where
\mathcal{O}
is the local system of orientations and
\mathcal{T}
is a local system of
\mathbb{Z}[T,T^{-1}]
-module with
\deg T=2N_{L}
. Here
[N_{L}]
denotes a grading shift by
N_{L}
.
The image of
\mathrm{PD}_{\Sigma}([\omega]_{\mathbb{Z}})
under the closed-open map
\mathrm{CO}:\mathrm{QH}_{*}(\Sigma)\to\mathrm{QH}_{*-\operatorname{dim}L}(L^{\sigma})
is represented by the class

\iota_{!}(\mathrm{PD}_{\Sigma}([\omega]_{\mathbb{Z}}))\otimes 1\in\mathrm{H}_{*}(L;\mathcal{O})\otimes\mathbb{Z}[T,T^{-1}]\cong\mathrm{H}_{*}(L;\mathbb{Z}[T,T^{-1}]\otimes\mathcal{O})

in
E^{1}
for degree reasons by the assumption
N_{L}\geq 3
. Here,
\iota_{!}=\mathrm{PD}_{L}\circ\iota^{*}\circ\mathrm{PD}_{\Sigma}^{-1}:\mathrm{H}_{*}(\Sigma)\to\mathrm{H}_{*-\operatorname{dim}L}(L;\mathcal{O})
.
The invertibility of
[\omega]_{\mathbb{Z}}
implies that
\mathrm{CO}(\mathrm{PD}_{\Sigma}([\omega]_{\mathbb{Z}}))\in\mathrm{QH}_{\operatorname{dim}L-2}(L^{\sigma})
is invertible.
Combining this with
\iota^{*}(d[\omega]_{\mathbb{Z}})=0
, we conclude that
\mathrm{QH}_{*}(L^{\sigma})
is
d
-torsion.
∎

Remark 6.11
.

Using field coefficients
\mathbb{F}
makes it more likely that the homology class corresponding to
\omega
is invertible.
In this case, the proof of Proposition
6.10
yields
\mathrm{QH}_{*}(L^{\sigma};\mathbb{F})=0
, provided the characteristic of
\mathbb{F}
does not divide
d
.

Recall from Lemma
3.1
that the Lagrangian submanifold
L
in Proposition
6.10
admits a Legendrian lift to the prequantization bundle
Y
over
(\Sigma,[\omega]_{\mathbb{Z}})
.
The covering degree of this lift is
\frac{2c_{\Sigma}}{\gcd(2c_{\Sigma},m_{\Sigma}N_{L})}
, which coincides with the torsion degree of
\mathrm{QH}_{*}(L^{\sigma})
.
In fact, Proposition
6.10
admits a proof that relies on the prequantization bundle
Y
, as demonstrated by the following theorem.

Theorem 6.12
.

Let
L\subset(\Sigma,\omega)
be a closed, connected monotone Lagrangian submanifold with
N_{L}\geq 3
. Assume that
L
admits a
d
-fold Legendrian lift
\mathcal{L}
to the prequantization bundle
Y
over
(\Sigma,[\omega]_{\mathbb{Z}})
. If
[\omega]_{\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathrm{QH}^{*}(\Sigma)
is invertible,
then, for any
\mathrm{Pin}^{\pm}
-structure
\sigma
on
L
,
\mathrm{QH}_{*}(L^{\sigma})
is
d
-torsion.

Proof.

We consider the Rabinowitz Floer homology
\mathrm{RFH}_{*}(Y)
, whose chain-level generators consist of constant orbits in
Y
and periodic orbits of the vector fields
\pm R
, where
R
